Illposedness via degenerate dispersion for generalized surface quasi-geostrophic equations with singular velocities
Dongho Chae, In-Jee Jeong, Sung-Jin Oh

Abstract
We prove strong nonlinear illposedness results for the generalized SQG equation ∂ t θ + ∇ ⟂ Γ ​ [ θ ] ⋅ ∇ θ = 0 \begin{split}\partial_{t}\theta+\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma[\theta]\cdot\nabla\theta=0\end{split} in any sufficiently regular Sobolev spaces, when Γ \Gamma is a singular multiplier in the sense that its symbol satisfies | Γ ⁡ ( ξ ) | → ∞ |\Gamma(\xi)|\rightarrow\infty as | ξ | → ∞ |\xi|\rightarrow\infty together some mild regularity assumptions. The key mechanism is degenerate dispersion, i.e., the rapid growth of frequencies of solutions around certain shear states, as in the second and third author’s earlier work on Hall-magnetohydrodynamics [ 46 ] . The robustness of our method allows one to extend linear and nonlinear illposedness to fractionally dissipative systems, as long as the order of dissipation is lower than that of Γ \Gamma . Our illposedness results are completely sharp in view of various existing wellposedness statements as well as those from our companion paper [ 20 ] . Key to our proofs is a novel construction of degenerating wave packets for the class of linear equations ∂ t ϕ + i ​ p ​ ( t , X , D ) ​ ϕ = 0 \begin{split}\partial_{t}\phi+ip(t,X,D)\phi=0\end{split} possibly with lower order terms, where p ⁡ ( t , X , D ) p(t,X,D) is a possibly time dependent pseudo-differential operator which is formally self-adjoint in L 2 L^{2} , degenerate, and dispersive. Degenerating wave packets are approximate solutions to the above linear equation with spatial and frequency support localized at ( X ⁡ ( t ) , Ξ ⁡ ( t ) ) (X(t),\Xi(t)) , which are solutions to the bicharacteristic ODE system associated with p ⁡ ( t , x , ξ ) p(t,x,\xi) . These wave packets explicitly show degeneration as X ⁡ ( t ) X(t) approaches a point where p p vanishes, which in particular allows us to prove illposedness in topologies finer than L 2 L^{2} . While the equation for the wave packet can be formally obtained from a Taylor expansion of the symbol near ξ = Ξ ⁡ ( t ) \xi=\Xi(t) , the difficult part is to rigorously control the error in sufficiently long timescales within which significant degeneration occurs. To achieve this task, we develop a systematic way to obtain sharp estimates for not only degenerating wave packets but also for oscillatory integrals which naturally appear in the error estimate.



1
Introduction

1.1
Generalized SQG equations

In two spatial dimensions, the
generalized surface quasi-geostrophic
(gSQG) equations are given by

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\theta+u\cdot\nabla\theta=0,\\
&u=\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\theta,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.1)

where
\nabla^{\perp}=(-\partial_{x_{2}},\partial_{x_{1}})^{\top}
and
\Gamma
is a Fourier multiplier with a real-valued symbol
\gamma
. Here,
\theta(t,\cdot):\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
u(t,\cdot):\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\Omega
a two-dimensional domain without boundaries (e.g.
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, or
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
). The system (
1.1
) says that the scalar
\theta
is being advected by the flow of
u
, which is determined from
\theta
at each moment of time by the “Biot–Savart” law
u=\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\theta
. For this reason, (
1.1
) is sometimes referred to as an
active scalar
system.

The gSQG system generalizes several important partial differential equations (PDEs) arising in hydrodynamics and magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) and has been intensively studied over the past few decades. A well-known special case occurs when
\Gamma=(-\Delta)^{-1}
, in which case (
1.1
) reduces to the vorticity equation for the two-dimensional incompressible Euler equations, where
\theta
corresponds to the vorticity of the fluid, which is
\nabla\times u
. Another fundamental case is
\Gamma=\Lambda^{-1}
, which yields the (standard) SQG equations, describing the evolution of atmospheric fronts. Here,
\Lambda=(-\Delta)^{1/2}
denotes the Zygmund operator. The SQG model was introduced by Constantin–Majda–Tabak
[
24
,
23
]
to describe Boussinesq dynamics at the boundary of the upper half-space (see
[
66
]
for further details). At the opposite extreme, the cases
\Gamma=\Lambda
and
\Gamma=\Lambda^{2}
arise naturally in MHD and large-scale atmospheric dynamics, respectively, and we will return to these examples below in Section
1.2
.

The pioneering works
[
24
,
23
]
suggested the possibility of rapid small scale creation for the SQG equation, and there have been several works demonstrating large gradient growth for smooth solutions (
[
30
,
37
,
50
,
51
,
52
,
29
]
).
However, it is still unclear whether smooth solutions of the SQG equation could blow up in finite time, in stark contrast with the Euler case
\Gamma=\Lambda^{-2}
for which global regularity is a classical result. This global regularity is based upon the fact that the conservation of
\|{\theta(t,\cdot)}\|_{L^{\infty}}
“almost” controls the Lipschitz norm of the velocity. Therefore, as the multiplier
\Gamma
becomes more singular, the control over the velocity one obtains from
\|{\theta(t,\cdot)}\|_{L^{\infty}}
becomes weaker, and one may expect that smooth solutions are more likely to blow up in finite time. Towards this goal, the global regularity question has been extensively studied for the generalized models where
\Gamma
is taken to be
\Lambda^{\beta}
for
-1\geq\beta>-2
(i.e., between the SQG and 2D Euler equations). When the equation is posed in the half-plane, some finite time singularity formation results were obtained in the range
-1.5>\beta>-2
(
[
53
,
54
,
31
,
60
,
77
,
44
]
) using in a crucial way the presence of the boundary (which, roughly speaking, stabilizes the singularity formation mechanism). The numerical simulations in
[
71
]
report self-similar type singularity formation for SQG patches. On the other hand, some global solutions were constructed in the works
[
14
,
13
,
17
,
25
,
11
,
12
,
34
,
36
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
]
.

As
\beta
increases, however, even the question of
local
regularity for smooth solutions becomes nontrivial. This difficulty arises as soon as the multiplier becomes more singular than in the SQG case (i.e., for
\beta>-1
). In this regime, the velocity field
u
is more singular than
\theta
, which creates serious obstacles in closing energy estimates in Sobolev spaces. Specifically, one must carefully analyze terms where all derivatives in the
H^{m}
estimate fall on
u
:

\begin{split}\langle\partial^{m}(\nabla^{\perp}\Lambda^{\beta}\theta)\cdot\nabla\theta,\partial^{m}\theta\rangle=\langle\nabla\theta\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\Lambda^{\beta}g,g\rangle,\end{split}

where
g:=\partial^{m}\theta
, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the standard
L^{2}
inner product.

A crucial observation here is that the principal part of the operator
\nabla\theta\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\Lambda^{\beta}
is antisymmetric, allowing one to gain a derivative. This idea was exploited by Chae–Constantin–Córdoba–Gancedo–Wu in
[
16
]
to establish local well-posedness for
\beta<0
, and this observation can be generalized to obtain local wellposedness when
|\gamma(\xi)|\lesssim 1
as
|\xi|\to 0
, assuming some natural regularity conditions on the derivatives of
\gamma
. Since then, the behavior of solutions in the range
\beta\in(-1,0)
was considered by many authors (
[
12
,
56
,
3
,
25
,
49
,
26
,
34
]
).

1.2
The case of singular multipliers

Turning to the case of
singular multipliers
, by which we simply mean that
\gamma(\xi)\rightarrow\infty
as
|\xi|\rightarrow\infty
, one first sees that in the “borderline” case when
\beta=0
(
\Gamma=\Lambda^{\beta}
), the nonlinearity vanishes completely:
u\cdot\nabla\theta=\nabla^{\perp}\theta\cdot\nabla\theta\equiv 0
. This could make one speculate that there might be some additional cancellation which gives local regularity even when
\beta>0
. Furthermore, the (formally) conserved quantity
\|{\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\theta(t,\cdot)}\|_{L^{2}}
for (
1.1
) becomes stronger than the other
L^{2}
-based conservation law
\|{\theta(t,\cdot)}\|_{L^{2}}
as soon as
\beta>0
.

Despite these facts, our main result shows
strong illposedness
in Sobolev spaces (with arbitrarily high regularity) for singular
\Gamma
satisfying a few reasonable assumptions. Interestingly, the generalized SQG equations with singular multipliers naturally appear in a variety of situations, as we shall now explain.

•

Ohkitani model
. In the papers
[
63
,
64
]
, Ohkitani considered the collective behavior of solutions to (
1.1
) which are obtained by varying
\beta<0
with the same initial data, towards the goal of settling the question of global regularity versus finite time singularity formation. Numerical simulations in
[
63
,
64
]
did not show any singular behavior of solutions in the limit
\beta\to 0^{-}
, and based on these, Ohkitani conjectured global regularity of the limiting model

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\theta-\nabla^{\perp}\ln(\Lambda)\theta\cdot\nabla\theta=0\end{split}

(1.2)

which is (
1.1
) with
\Gamma=-\ln(\Lambda)
. This has been referred to as
Ohkitani model
in
[
16
]
. To see how (
1.2
) arises, one can simply rewrite (
1.1
) with
\Gamma=\Lambda^{\beta}
with
\beta<0
as

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\beta}\partial_{t}\theta+\nabla^{\perp}\left(\frac{\Lambda^{\beta}-1}{\beta}\right)\theta\cdot\nabla\theta=0,\end{split}

(1.3)

and formally we have that as
\beta\to 0^{-}
, (
1.3
) converges to (
1.2
) in the rescaled timescale
\beta t
. This limit was made rigorous in our companion paper
[
20
]
, and in particular, gave a long-time existence result for solutions in the limit
\beta\to 0^{-}
.

•

E-MHD system
. The electron magnetohydrodynamics (E-MHD) system takes the form

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}B+\nabla\times((\nabla\times B)\times B)=0,\\
&\nabla\cdot B=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.4)

where
B(t,x):\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
. This is a leading order model for the Hall–MHD system (
[
57
,
65
]
). Under the so-called
2+\frac{1}{2}
-dimensional assumption,
B
can be written as

\begin{split}B=\nabla\psi\times e_{z}+\Lambda\phi\,e_{z}\end{split}

for some
scalar
functions
\psi,\phi
independent of the third coordinate
z
, and (
1.4
) reduces to the following system in two dimensions (
[
58
]
):

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\psi+\nabla^{\perp}\Lambda\phi\cdot\nabla\psi=0,\\
&\partial_{t}\phi+\Lambda^{-1}(\nabla^{\perp}\Lambda(\Lambda\psi)\cdot\nabla\psi)=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.5)

Up to leading order, the ansatz
\psi\simeq\phi
propagates in time, which simply corresponds to (
1.1
) with
\Gamma=\Lambda
.

•

Asymptotic model for the large-scale quasi-geostrophic (AM-LQG) equation
. The following equation is referred to as the asymptotic model (AM) for the large-scale quasi-geostrophic equation (see
[
55
,
7
,
76
,
8
,
73
,
74
]
):

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\psi+\nabla^{\perp}\Delta\psi\cdot\nabla\psi=0.\end{split}

(1.6)

Notice that this is nothing but (
1.1
) with the choice
\Gamma=\Delta
. The AM equation has received quite a bit of attention from physicists as the solutions exhibit very different features from the usual 2D turbulence
[
8
]
.
One can arrive at (
1.6
) by starting from the so-called Charney–Hasegawa–Mima (CHM) equation

\begin{split}\partial_{t}q+\nabla^{\perp}\psi\cdot\nabla q=0,\end{split}

(1.7)

which is relevant for shallow water quasi-geostrophic dynamics. It is argued that this equation governs ocean front dynamics and planetary atmospheric pattern including Great Red Spot (
[
72
,
66
,
35
]
). Here,
q
denotes the
potential vorticity
and is related by the stream function
\psi
by
q=(\Delta-L_{D}^{-2})\psi
(so that (
1.7
) is nothing but (
1.1
) with
\Gamma=(\Delta-L_{D}^{-2})^{-1}
). Here,
L_{D}
is the so-called
Rossby deformation length
and it is argued in Burgess and Dritschel
[
8
]
that when the characteristic length-scale of the flow
L
satisfies
L\gg L_{D}
, (
1.6
) can be obtained from (
1.7
) in the rescaled timescale
tL_{D}^{2}
. Very recently, Svirsky–Herbert–Frishman
[
73
,
74
]
reported detailed statistical properties of the condensate for (
1.6
).

Let us discuss the physical relevance of illposedness for E-MHD and AM-LQG in Section
1.7
below, after precisely stating our main results.

1.3
Rough statement of the results

Our main result, which is stated roughly for now, gives strong illposedness for a large class of singular symbols, including all of the above three examples.

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider the following symbols
\gamma
and pairs of exponents
s,s^{\prime}
:

Multiplier

Sobolev regularity exponents

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\beta}
,
\beta>1

s^{\prime}=s>3+\frac{3}{2}\beta

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle

s^{\prime}>\frac{9}{2}

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\beta}
,
\beta<1

\frac{1}{1-\beta}s^{\prime}>\max\{s+\frac{\beta^{2}}{2(1-\beta)},\frac{3}{2}\frac{2+\beta}{1-\beta}\}

\gamma=\log^{\beta}(10+|{\xi}|)
,
\beta>0

s^{\prime}=s>3

\gamma=\log^{\beta}(10+\log(10+|{\xi}|))
,
\beta>0

s^{\prime}=s>3

In each of the above cases, the Cauchy problem for (
1.1
) on the domain
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{2}
or
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
is
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
ill-posed
in the following sense:
For any
\epsilon,\delta,A>0
, there exists initial data
\theta_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
with
\|{\theta_{0}}\|_{H^{s}}<\epsilon
such that either

•

there exists no solution
\theta\in L^{\infty}([0,\delta];H^{s^{\prime}})
to (
1.1
) with
\left.\theta\right|_{t=0}=\theta_{0}
, or

•

any solution
\theta
belonging to
L^{\infty}([0,\delta];H^{s^{\prime}})
satisfy the growth

\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\|{\theta(t,\cdot)}\|_{H^{s^{\prime}}}>A.\end{split}

Theorem
1.1
is a
norm inflation
result. When
\Omega=\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
and
s=s^{\prime}
, we may furthermore establish a
non-existence
result.

Theorem 1.2
.

Consider a symbol
\gamma
and a real number
s
such that
\gamma
and
s=s^{\prime}
satisfy the hypothesis of Theorem
1.1
. Then the Cauchy problem for (
1.1
) on the domain
\Omega=\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
is
ill-posed
in the following sense: There exists an initial data set
\theta_{0}\in H^{s}
with arbitrarily small
H^{s}
norm, for which there does
not
exist a solution to the Cauchy problem for (
1.1
) in
L^{\infty}_{t}([0,\delta],H^{s^{\prime}})
.

The above illposedness results suggest that one needs to be careful when working with models with singular multipliers: either an appropriate dissipative term must be supplied,
1
1

1

We note that while these models are often written without any dissipative terms in many physics texts, numerical simulations are always performed by adding very strong dissipative terms.
or one should restrict to an appropriate class of functions (see Remark
1.4
below for more discussion). Indeed, local wellposedness of all the above singular examples, namely (
1.2
), (
1.4
), (
1.5
), and (
1.6
), has been obtained with appropriate dissipation terms: consider now

\left\{\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\theta+u\cdot\nabla\theta+\kappa\Upsilon(\theta)=0,\\
&u=\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma[\theta],\end{split}\right.

(1.8)

where
\kappa>0
and
\Upsilon
is a multiplier with strictly positive symbol. The authors of
[
16
]
have shown local regularity of the Ohkitani model with arbitrarily fractional dissipation (i.e.
\Upsilon=(-\Delta)^{\epsilon}
for any
\epsilon>0
), which have been improved to any super-logarithmic dissipation in
[
20
]
. In the case of the E-MHD (and Hall-MHD), the works
[
18
,
21
]
obtained local regularity with magnetic dissipation stronger than
\Lambda
. A similar computation can be done for the AM, which then requires a dissipation term strictly stronger than
\Lambda^{2}
.

While these wellposedness results are obtained by rather standard Sobolev and commutator estimates, our illposedness results for the dissipative systems (
1.8
) show that these existing results are completely sharp:

Theorem 1.3
.

Consider the following pairs of symbols
\gamma,\upsilon
and exponents
s,s^{\prime}
:

Multiplier
\Gamma

Dissipation
\Upsilon

Sobolev regularity exponents

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\beta}
,
\beta>1

\upsilon=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\alpha}
,
\alpha<\beta

\frac{s^{\prime}}{1-(\beta-\alpha)}>s+\frac{\beta(\beta-\alpha)}{2(1-(\beta-\alpha))}

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle

\upsilon=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\alpha}
,
\alpha<1

\frac{s^{\prime}}{\alpha}>s+\frac{1-\alpha}{2\alpha}

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\beta}
,
\beta<1

\upsilon=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\alpha}
,
\alpha<\beta

\frac{s^{\prime}}{1-(\beta-\alpha)}>s+\frac{\beta(\beta-\alpha)}{2(1-(\beta-\alpha))}

\gamma=\log^{\beta}(10+|{\xi}|)
,
\beta>0

\upsilon=\log^{\alpha}(10+|{\xi}|)
,
\alpha<\beta

s^{\prime}=s

The restrictions on
s
and
s^{\prime}
in the table are
in addition
to those from the nondissipative case. In each of the above cases, the Cauchy problem for (
1.8
) on the domain
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{2}
or
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
is
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
ill-posed
in the same sense as in Theorem
1.1
.

Remark 1.4
.

Our results establish illposedness of (
1.1
) for a large class of singular symbols in standard function spaces near the trivial solution
\mathring{\theta}=0
. Nevertheless, wellposedness in standard function spaces may still be possible around nontrivial background solutions, which are sometimes physically motivated. For instance, small data local wellposedness of E-MHD in weighted Sobolev space around
\mathring{\theta}=x_{2}
– corresponding to a uniform magnetic field – may be established as an application of the techniques in
[
59
]
.

1.4
Degenerate dispersive equations

It turns out that the linearization around degenerate shear steady states for (
1.1
) shows
degenerate dispersion
, which is the mechanism behind strong illposedness in the singular regime. Indeed, a large part of this work is devoted to the construction of
degenerating wave packets
for the class of linear equations

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\phi+ip(t,X,D)\phi=0\end{split}

(1.9)

possibly with lower order terms, where
p
is a time-dependent real pseudo-differential operator which is degenerate and dispersive. Once degenerating wave packets are constructed for all large frequencies, their time evolution essentially governs in which topologies the initial value problem for (
1.9
) could be well-posed. While our framework works for the general class of equations (
1.9
), we have chosen to focus on its applications towards the family of singular generalized SQG equations in this work, as the class of linearized equations arising from this family by varying
\Gamma
and the steady state profile forms a representative class of (
1.9
).

Degenerate dispersive equations appear in a variety of physical contexts, besides those related with the gSQG equations described in the above. Primary examples include shallow water wave (
[
10
,
6
,
33
]
) and sedimentation models (
[
4
,
9
,
78
,
69
,
70
]
). Many of these models, most notably the Camassa–Holm and
abcd
-Boussinesq equations (introduced in
[
10
]
and
[
6
]
respectively and extensively studied since) feature principal terms which involve non-local and non-linear dispersion. More comprehensive list of physical systems involving degenerate dispersion (as well as related mathematical progress) is given in
[
2
,
32
,
45
]
. We shall review a few recent developments on the Cauchy problem for these type of equations, which are most directly relevant for the current work.

Well/Illposedness of
K(m,n)
equations and their variants
. The family of
K(m,n)
equations introduced in
[
68
,
67
]
is given by

\begin{split}\partial_{t}u+(u^{n})_{xxx}+(u^{m})_{x}=0.\end{split}

(1.10)

For
n>1
, this model could be considered as the simplest equations featuring a quasilinear dispersive principal term (see
[
78
]
where this type of term appears for a model of particle suspensions). Various numerical simulations for this equation hinted at illposedness in strong topologies (see
[
2
,
42
,
28
]
). In the case of degenerate Airy equation
\partial_{t}u+2uu_{xxx}=0
(which is a further simplified model for
n=2
),
[
2
]
gave illposedness in
H^{2}
of the initial value problem using explicit self-similar solution with scaling symmetries of the equation. We note that (uni-directional in time) illposedness for
\partial_{t}u\pm xu_{xxx}=0
was obtained earlier in
[
27
]
, based on the explicit solution formula. A general illposedness result, which works not only for (
1.10
) but also for many variants, was obtained in our recent work
[
45
]
. Here, the illposedness is deduced from the construction of degenerating wave packets for the linearized equation around degenerate solutions. On the other hand,
[
32
]
obtained a well-posedness result for certain variant of (
1.10
) in the case of “subcritically” degenerate (cf.
[
32
, Section 1.5]
) data. This well-posedness result is not contradictory to illposedness results from
[
45
]
; the solutions of
[
32
]
live in a suitably weighted space, which takes into account the rate of degeneracy of the solution.

Illposedness of the Hall- and electron-MHD systems
. Based on construction of degenerating wave packets, the system (
1.4
) (as well as the Hall–MHD system) was shown to be strongly ill-posed near degenerate shear magnetic backgrounds, in the recent work
[
46
,
47
]
of the second and third authors. Indeed, the mechanism of illposedness for (
1.4
) is the same with the current paper. Additional difficulties arising in this work is that the system is
non-local
(opposed to (
1.4
)) and the symbol of
\Gamma
could be only slightly singular, where the issue of well/illposedness becomes very delicate (as demonstrated explicitly in Theorem
E
).

The remainder of the introduction is organized as follows.
In
Section
1.5
, we give precise statements of the main results of this paper, of which Theorems
1.1
–
1.2
are special cases.
In
Section
1.6
, we present a toy model for the linearized equation which is almost explicitly solvable in the Fourier space yet contains the main features of the linearized dynamics. The model is obtained simply by dropping the sub-principal term and replacing the principal coefficient with a linear function. This solvable toy model demonstrates that the illposedness behavior is caused by degenerate dispersion, and gives the optimal growth rate of Sobolev norms that can be achieved. Furthermore, by comparing the toy model with actual linear equations, we explain the main difficulties in understanding the dynamics of linear equations.
Then we end the introduction with an outline of the
Organization of the Paper
.

1.5
Main Results

We now give precise formulation of the main results of this paper.

Assumptions on
\Gamma
.
In what follows, we assume that
\gamma
is a
smooth even positive symbol
that satisfies the following properties for some
\Xi_{0}>1
:

1.

\gamma
is elliptic and slowly varying:

|{\partial_{\xi}^{I}\gamma(\xi)}|\lesssim_{I}\langle{\xi}\rangle^{-|{I}|}\gamma(\xi)
for any multi-index
I
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

2.

\gamma\nearrow\infty
as
|{\xi}|\nearrow\infty
:

\gamma(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\to\infty
as
|{\xi}|\to\infty
.

3.

\xi_{2}\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma
is elliptic and slowly varying:

|{\partial_{\xi}^{I}(\xi_{2}\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma(\xi))}|\lesssim_{I}\langle{\xi}\rangle^{-|{I}|}\xi_{2}\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma(\xi)
for any multi-index
I
and
\Xi_{0}\leq|{\xi_{1}}|\leq|{\xi_{2}}|
.

4.

\xi_{2}\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma
is almost comparable to
\gamma
:

\xi_{2}\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma(\xi)\gtrsim\frac{1}{(\log|{\xi_{2}}|)^{2}}\gamma(\xi)

for
\Xi_{0}\leq|{\xi_{1}}|\leq|{\xi_{2}}|
.

A brief explanation of each assumption is in order:

•

Assumption 1 is a natural assumption that justifies, in particular, symbolic calculus (see Section
3
).

•

Assumption 2 is a basic requirement for an arbitrarily fast frequency growth.

•

Assumptions 3 and 4 arise naturally in the control of the focusing of nearby bicharacteristics, which are put together to construct a suitable approximate solution to the linearized equation, called
degenerating wave packets
(see Sections
3
–
6
). In particular, Assumption 4 allows us to quantify the scale of the degenerating wave packets (denoted by
\mu^{-1}
later) in terms of its frequency (denoted by
\lambda
later). The factor
(\log|{\xi_{2}}|)^{-2}
is somewhat arbitrary but fixed for simplicity.

Many natural choices of
\gamma
satisfy the above assumptions, including
\gamma(\xi)=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\beta}
for any
\beta>0
,
\gamma(\xi)=\exp(\beta\log^{\alpha}(10+|{\xi}|))
for any
\beta>0
and
0<\alpha<1
,
\gamma(\xi)=\log^{\beta}(10+|{\xi}|)
for any
\beta>0
,
\gamma(\xi)=\log^{\beta}(10+\log^{\alpha}(10+|{\xi}|))
for any
\alpha,\beta>0
etc.

By Assumption 1, there exists
\beta_{0}>0
such that

\sup_{\xi^{\prime}:|{\xi}|\leq|{\xi^{\prime}}|\leq 2|{\xi}|}\gamma(\xi^{\prime})\leq 2^{\beta_{0}}\gamma(\xi)\quad\hbox{ for }|{\xi}|\geq\Xi_{0}.

(1.11)

For the remainder of this paper,
we fix one such
\beta_{0}>0
and let other constants depend on it. Iterating this bound, it follows that

\gamma(\xi)\lesssim|{\xi}|^{\beta_{0}}|{\Xi_{0}}|^{-\beta_{0}}\gamma(\Xi_{0})\quad\hbox{ for }|{\xi}|\geq\Xi_{0}.

The infimum of possible
\beta_{0}
’s (among all real numbers) is called the
order
of
\gamma
. The justification for this terminology comes from the property that if
\gamma=|{\xi}|^{\beta_{0}}
, then
\beta_{0}
is its order in the usual sense.

Assumptions on
\Upsilon
.

For the dissipative operator
\Upsilon
, we simply assume that its symbol
\upsilon
is a
smooth even positive symbol
that is elliptic and slowly varying, in the sense that

|{\partial_{\xi}^{{I}}\upsilon(\xi)}|\lesssim_{{I}}|{\xi}|^{-|{{I}}|}\upsilon(\xi).

(1.12)

As in the case of
\gamma
, there exists
\alpha_{0}>0
such that

\sup_{\xi^{\prime}:|{\xi}|\leq|{\xi^{\prime}}|\leq 2|{\xi}|}\upsilon(\xi^{\prime})\leq 2^{\alpha_{0}}\upsilon(\xi)\quad\hbox{ for }|{\xi}|\geq\Xi_{0}.

(1.13)

For the remainder of this paper,
we fix one such
\alpha_{0}>0
and let other constants depend on it.

Assumptions on
\mathring{\theta}
. Next, we specify the class of shear states that will be proved to be unstable, in linear/nonlinear settings and with/without dissipation, in high-regularity Sobolev spaces.

In the absence of dissipation, recall that any shear steady state
\theta=f(x_{2})
(with reasonable regularity assumptions) solves (
1.1
). We shall
assume that
f
is smooth, bounded and has a quadratic degeneracy
in the following sense:

Definition 1.5
.

We say that a shear steady state
\mathring{\theta}=f(x_{2})
for (
1.1
) is
quadratically degenerate
at
\mathring{x}_{2}\in(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})
if

f^{\prime}(\mathring{x}_{2})=0,\qquad f^{\prime\prime}(\mathring{x}_{2})\neq 0.

Remark 1.6
.

Our method easily extends to the case when the order of vanishing of
f^{\prime}(x_{2})
at
x_{2}=\mathring{x}_{2}
is generalized to any positive real number. The general heuristic principle is that the slower the vanishing (i.e., the lower the order), the faster the frequency growth. The quadratically degenerate case considered in Definition
1.5
is distinguished by the fact that it is the generic order for a smooth
f^{\prime}(x_{2})
. We also note that the boundedness assumption can be readily generalized to a polynomial growth condition at infinity.

In the presence of dissipation, equation (
1.8
) for a shear state
\mathring{\theta}=f(t,x_{2})
reduces to

(\partial_{t}+\kappa\Upsilon)f(t,x_{2})=0,

(1.14)

where
\Upsilon
obeys the assumptions made above. By Fourier analysis, (
1.14
) is clearly well-posed forward in time in
H^{s}((\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R})_{x_{2}})
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for any well-posed solution
f
, if
f(0,x_{2})
is even then so is
f(t,x_{2})
for each
t>0
. In what follows, we shall take as our background shear state a
smooth bounded solution
\mathring{\theta}=f(t,x_{2})
such that
f_{0}(x_{2})=f(0,x_{2})
is
even
and
{f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(0)}\neq 0
.

Remark 1.7
.

The evenness assumption brings a technical simplification in our argument, as the degenerate point
x_{2}=0
is then fixed in
t
. Our methods may be extended to the case when this assumption is removed, in which case the degenerate point may move in time, but at the price of additional technical constraints on the length of the time interval.

Linear results.

We begin by stating our main results concerning the linearization of (
1.1
) and (
1.8
) around
\mathring{\theta}
introduced above. The direct linearization of (
1.1
) around
\mathring{\theta}
is given by
L_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=0
, where

L_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=\partial_{t}\phi-\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma\phi+\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\phi,

(1.15)

where as for (
1.8
), the linearization around
\mathring{\theta}
takes the form
L^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=0
, where

L^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=\partial_{t}\phi-\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma\phi+\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\phi+\kappa\Upsilon\phi.

(1.16)

To formulate a linear illposedness result of the desired generality and precision, it is convenient to introduce the following set of parameters. Given
\lambda_{0}>1
, called the
initial frequency parameter
and
M>1
, called the
frequency growth factor
, define the corresponding (normalized)
frequency growth time

\tau_{M}
to be

\tau_{M}:=\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{M\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}.

(1.17)

Justification of the formula (
1.17
) shall be given later in Section
2
, but let us briefly explain the terminology we use here. Our wave packets will have frequency near
\lambda_{0}
, in both variables, at the initial time. The consideration of bicharacteristic ODE system (see Section
2
, (
2.1
)) predicts that wave packets would take time equal to
\tau_{M}
for their initial frequency
\lambda_{0}
to grow by the factor of
M
. In addition to these parameters, we also fix an arbitrarily small parameter
0<\delta_{0}<\frac{1}{100}
and a nonnegative parameter
0\leq\sigma_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}-\delta_{0}
, which shall be used in the conditions that
\lambda_{0},M
need to satisfy.

Non-dissipative case
. Roughly speaking, our main linear result in the non-dissipative case, Theorem
A
, states that given
\lambda_{0}
and
M
obeying a suitable condition (see (
1.18
)–(
1.20
) below), there exists an initial data set
\phi_{0}
for the linearized equation
L_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=0
with frequency
O(\lambda_{0})
such that any corresponding solution exhibits frequency growth by factor
M
in time
O_{f}(\tau_{M})
.

Theorem A

(Linear illposedness, non-dissipative case)
.

Let
\mathring{\theta}=f(x_{2})
be a smooth bounded shear steady state that is quadratically degenerate at
x_{2}=\mathring{x}_{2}
and fix a small parameter
0<\delta_{0}<\frac{1}{100}
and
0\leq\sigma_{0}\leq\frac{1}{3}(1-2\delta_{0})
. Then there exist
\Lambda_{0}=\Lambda_{0}(f,\gamma,\delta_{0},\sigma_{0})>1
and
T_{0}=T_{0}(f,\gamma,\delta_{0},\sigma_{0})>0
such that the following holds: For each
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda_{0}
,
\tau_{M}\leq T_{0}
and
M>1
satisfying the
growth conditions

\displaystyle\sup_{M^{\prime}\in[1,M]}\frac{\gamma(\lambda_{0},M^{\prime}\lambda_{0})}{{M^{\prime}}}\tau_{M^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq{\min\{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})^{1-\delta_{0}},\lambda_{0}^{\sigma_{0}}\}},

(1.18)

\displaystyle\tau_{M}

\displaystyle\leq\min\left\{\frac{\lambda_{0}^{1-\delta_{0}{-3\sigma_{0}}}}{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})},1\right\},

(1.19)

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{0}^{\frac{1}{\delta_{0}}},

(1.20)

there exists a smooth function
\phi_{0}
such that

\|{{\phi_{0}}}\|_{H^{s^{\prime}}}\leq{C_{0;s^{\prime},s}}\lambda_{0}^{s^{\prime}-s}\|{{\phi_{0}}}\|_{H^{s}}\quad\hbox{ for any }s^{\prime}\geq s,

(1.21)

yet any
\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2}
-solution
\phi
to
L_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=0
on
\left[0,\tfrac{100}{99}\tfrac{1}{|{f^{\prime\prime}(\mathring{x}_{2})}|}\tau_{M}\right]
with
\left.\phi\right|_{t=0}=\phi_{0}
obeys

\sup_{t\in\left[0,\tfrac{100}{99}\tfrac{1}{|{f^{\prime\prime}(\mathring{x}_{2})}|}\tau_{M}\right]}\|{{\phi}(t,\cdot)}\|_{H^{s^{\prime}}}\geq C_{s^{\prime},s}{\frac{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\gamma(\lambda_{0},M\lambda_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}}M^{s^{\prime}}\lambda_{0}^{s^{\prime}-s}\|{{\phi_{0}}}\|_{H^{s}}\quad\hbox{ for any }s,s^{\prime}>0.

(1.22)

A
\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2}
solution to
L_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi
is a natural notion of a weak solution in view of the energy structure of
L_{\mathring{\theta}}
; we postpone its precise definition to Section
2
below.

Remark 1.8
.

Condition (
1.18
) is used to control the evolution of the frequency of the wave packet. The condition
\tau_{M}\leq\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})^{-1}\lambda_{0}^{1-\delta_{0}-3\sigma_{0}}
in (
1.19
) arises naturally from the error estimate (see Section
6.3
), while
\tau_{M}\leq 1
is natural in view of the local-in-time energy argument employed in the proof (see Section
7
). Condition (
1.20
) is a mild technical condition that is assumed to simplify the control of some non-main error terms; we expect that it can be removed for specific
\gamma
’s (see, for instance,
[
46
]
for electron and Hall MHD, where the frequency growth factor could be of size
e^{c\lambda_{0}}
).

Remark 1.9
.

We note that the LHS of (
1.18
) is
uniformly bounded
when
the order
\beta_{0}
of
\gamma
is less than
1
, in which case we only need (
1.19
) with
\sigma_{0}=0
. In fact, the parameter
\sigma_{0}
is simply a device introduced to handle (in an non-optimal way) the case
\beta_{0}\geq 1
, which is “supercritical” in many ways; see the discussion following Corollary
1.13
. If we assume that
f^{\prime\prime\prime}
vanishes to a high order at
\mathring{x}_{2}
, then the factor
3
in front of
\sigma_{0}
in (
1.19
) can be lowered to any number greater than
2
, and the condition
\sigma_{0}\leq\frac{1}{3}(1-2\delta_{0})
would be relaxed accordingly; see Remark
4.2
.

Remark 1.10
.

The above statement may give the impression that illposeness occurs only for a very carefully selected initial data, but this is not the case. We have stated the result in this way both for simplicity and also to achieve the sharp possible growth rate dictated by the bicharacteristic ODE system. In the proof, we shall see that the same growth rate (possibly up to multiplicative constants) persists for all initial data which are sufficiently close in
L^{2}
to
\phi_{0}
we constructed.

We now discuss the implications of Theorem
A
. We first demonstrate that under our assumptions on
\gamma
,
the linearized equation around any smooth shear steady state with a quadratic degeneracy is always ill-posed in
H^{s}
for any
s>{\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}}
.
As
\lambda\mapsto\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda)
is increasing on
[\lambda_{0},\infty)
, we have the obvious bound

\tau_{M}\leq\frac{M}{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})}\quad\hbox{ for $\lambda_{0}$ sufficiently large.}

As a result, if we set

M(\lambda_{0})={\min\left\{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})^{1-\delta_{0}},\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{0}},\,\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})^{\frac{1-2\delta_{0}}{\beta_{0}}},\lambda_{0}^{\frac{1-5\delta_{0}}{3\beta_{0}}}\right\}},

then (
1.19
) (with
\sigma_{0}=\frac{1}{3}(1-2\delta_{0})
) and (
1.20
) are satisfied for sufficiently large
\lambda_{0}
. To check (
1.18
), we estimate

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma(\lambda_{0},M^{\prime}\lambda_{0})}{M^{\prime}\min\{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})^{1-\delta_{0}},\lambda_{0}^{\frac{1}{3}(1-2\delta_{0})}\}}\tau_{M^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq C(M^{\prime})^{\beta_{0}}\frac{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})}{\min\{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})^{1-\delta_{0}},\lambda_{0}^{\frac{1}{3}(1-2\delta_{0})}\}}\frac{1}{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\min\{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})^{\delta_{0}},\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{0}}\}}\leq 1,

using the above bound for
\tau_{M^{\prime}}
and
M(\lambda_{0})\leq\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})^{\frac{1-2\delta_{0}}{\beta_{0}}},\lambda_{0}^{\frac{1-5\delta_{0}}{3\beta_{0}}}
. Furthermore, observe that
M(\lambda_{0})\to\infty
and
\tau_{M(\lambda_{0})}\to 0
as
\lambda_{0}\to\infty
. Finally, by (
1.11
), note that

\frac{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\gamma(\lambda_{0},M\lambda_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\gtrsim M^{-\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}},

(1.23)

so the RHS of (
1.22
) is increasing in
M
for
s^{\prime}>\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}
. We have therefore proved:

Corollary 1.11

(Linear
H^{s}
illposedness for any
s>{\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}}
)
.

Let
\mathring{\theta}=f(x_{2})
be a smooth bounded shear steady state that is quadratically degenerate at
x_{2}=\mathring{x}_{2}
. For any
s>{\frac{\beta_{0}}{2}}
, there exists a sequence
{\phi_{(n)0}}
of initial data sets and times
T_{(n)}
such that

\|{{\phi_{(n)0}}}\|_{H^{s}}\leq 1,\qquad 0<T_{(n)}\leq 1,\qquad T_{(n)}\searrow 0,

yet for any sequence
{\phi_{(n)}}
of
\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2}
-solutions to
L_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=0
on
[0,T_{(n)}]
with
\left.\phi_{(n)}\right|_{t=0}=\phi_{(n)0}
, we have

\frac{\sup_{t\in[0,T_{(n)}]}\|{{\phi_{(n)}}(t,\cdot)}\|_{H^{s}}}{\|{{\phi_{(n)0}}}\|_{H^{s}}}\to\infty\quad\hbox{ as }n\to\infty.

On the other hand, an inspection of the size of the optimal frequency growth factor
M
for various model cases (see Corollary
1.13
) shows that the instability mechanism in hand is stronger for a multiplier
\gamma
with faster growth. One way to make quantify this idea is to introduce the following notion:

Definition 1.12
.

We say that the shear steady state
\mathring{\theta}
with a quadratic degeneracy is
linearly
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
unstable by degenerate dispersion according to Theorem
A
if there exist a sequence
(M_{(n)},\lambda_{0(n)},{\tau_{M(n)}})
satisfying (
1.18
)–(
1.20
),
\lambda_{0(n)}\geq\Lambda_{0}
and
\tau_{M(n)}\leq T_{0}
such that

{\frac{\gamma(\lambda_{0(n)},\lambda_{0(n)})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\gamma(\lambda_{0(n)},M_{(n)}\lambda_{0(n)})^{\frac{1}{2}}}}M_{(n)}^{s^{\prime}}\lambda_{0(n)}^{s^{\prime}-s}\to\infty,\qquad\tau_{M_{(n)}}\to 0.

In what follows, we shall often drop the proviso
according to Theorem
A
. Given a shear steady state
\mathring{\theta}
that is linearly
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
unstable by degenerate dispersion, no mapping from
H^{s}
to a set of
\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2}
-solutions to
L_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=0
in
L^{\infty}_{t}([0,\delta],H^{s^{\prime}})
can be bounded thanks to (
1.22
) in Theorem
A
. Note that if Definition
1.12
holds for one shear steady state
\mathring{\theta}
with a quadratic degeneracy, then it holds for any other such shear steady states. Indeed, the key conditions (
1.18
)–(
1.20
) depend only on
\gamma
; the only dependence on the specific shear steady state
\mathring{\theta}
is through the lower and upper bounds
\Lambda_{0}
and
T_{0}
on
\lambda_{0(n)}
and
\tau_{M(n)}
, respectively, which can be trivially enforced by restricting
n
to be large enough. Hence, Definition
1.12
is a property of the system (
1.1
) (more precisely, of
\gamma
).

Next, we specialize
\gamma
to several model cases and compute the (essentially) optimal growth factor given by Theorem
A
.

Corollary 1.13
.

Let
\mathring{\theta}=f(x_{2})
be a smooth shear steady state that is quadratically degenerate at
x_{2}=\mathring{x}_{2}
.
In each of the following cases, Theorem
A
applies with the specified choice of
M
for any
0\leq\sigma<1
, provided that
\delta_{0}
and
\sigma_{0}
are chosen appropriately depending on
\sigma
and
\beta
; moreover, in each case,
\tau_{M}\to 0
as
\lambda_{0}\to\infty
. As a result, (
1.1
) is linearly
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
unstable by degenerate dispersion for the specified values of
s,s^{\prime}
.

Multiplier
\Gamma

Freq. growth
M

Lin.
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
inst.

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\beta}
,
\beta>1

M=\lambda_{0}^{\sigma\frac{1}{3(\beta-1)}}

s^{\prime},s>\frac{\beta}{2}
,
{(1+\frac{1}{3(\beta-1)})s^{\prime}>s+\frac{\beta}{6(\beta-1)}}

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle

M=\lambda_{0}^{\frac{1}{1-\sigma}}

s^{\prime},s>\frac{1}{2}

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\beta}
,
\beta<1

M=\lambda_{0}^{\sigma\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}}

s^{\prime},s>\frac{\beta}{2}
,
{\frac{1}{1-\beta}s^{\prime}>s+\frac{\beta^{2}}{2(1-\beta)}}

\gamma=\log^{\beta}(10+|{\xi}|)
,
\beta>0

M=\log^{\sigma\beta}\lambda_{0}

s^{\prime}=s>0

\gamma=\log^{\beta}(10+\log(10+|{\xi}|))
,
\beta>0

M=\log^{\sigma\beta}\log\lambda_{0}

s^{\prime}=s>0

Corollary
1.13
makes quantitative the expectation that the faster growth of
\gamma
, the stronger the instability by degenerate dispersion.
2
2

2

The case
\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\beta}
for
\beta>1
is an exception, but this seems to be due to the inefficiency of our method in this case.

Dissipative case
. We now state the main linear result in the dissipative case. Let
\lambda_{0}
,
M
and
\tau_{M}
be defined as before. We introduce two small constants
0<\delta_{1}\leq\delta_{0}<\frac{1}{100}
. In place of
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|^{-1}{\tau_{M}}
that arose in Theorem
A
, we define the function
t_{f}(\tau_{M})
by the relation

\int_{0}^{t_{f}(\tau_{M})}|{f^{\prime\prime}(t^{\prime},0)}|\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}=\tau_{M}.

Note that
f^{\prime\prime}
is time-independent, then
t_{f}(\tau_{M})=|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|^{-1}\tau_{M}
.

Theorem B

(Linear illposedness, dissipative case)
.

Let
\mathring{\theta}_{0}=f_{0}(x_{2})
a smooth even function with
f_{0}^{\prime\prime}(0)\neq 0
, let
\mathring{\theta}=f(t,x_{2})
be the smooth solution to (
1.14
) with
f(0,x_{2})=f_{0}(x_{2})
and fix small parameters
0<\delta_{1}\leq\delta_{0}<\frac{1}{100}
and a parameter
0\leq\sigma_{0}\leq\frac{1}{3}(1-2\delta_{0})
.
Then there exist
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{1}({f,\gamma,\upsilon,\delta_{0},\delta_{1},\sigma_{0}})
and
T_{1}=T_{1}({f,\gamma,\upsilon,\delta_{0},\delta_{1},\sigma_{0}})>0
such that the following holds. For each
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda_{1}
,
\tau_{M}\leq T_{1}
and
M>1
satisfying the nondissipative growth conditions (
1.18
)–(
1.20
), as well as the conditions

\displaystyle\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{M\lambda_{0}}\frac{\upsilon(\lambda_{0},\lambda)}{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}=o_{\lambda_{0}}(1),

(1.24)

\displaystyle\sup_{M^{\prime}\in[1,M]}\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\left(\frac{\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda)}{\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma(\lambda_{0},M^{\prime}\lambda_{0})}\right)^{1-\delta_{1}}\frac{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})}{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda)^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}\leq 1,

(1.25)

there exists a smooth function
{\phi_{0}}
such that

\|{{\phi_{0}}}\|_{H^{s^{\prime}}}\leq{C_{s,s^{\prime}}}\lambda_{0}^{s^{\prime}-s}\|{{\phi_{0}}}\|_{H^{s}}\quad\hbox{ for any }s^{\prime}\geq s,

(1.26)

yet any
\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2}
-solution
\phi
to
L^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=0
on
\left[0,\tfrac{100}{99}t_{f}(\tau_{M})\right]
with
\phi(t=0)=\phi_{0}
obeys

\sup_{t\in\left[0,\tfrac{100}{99}t_{f}(\tau_{M})\right]}\|{{\phi}(t,\cdot)}\|_{H^{s^{\prime}}}\geq C_{s^{\prime},s}{\frac{\gamma(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\gamma(\lambda_{0},M\lambda_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}}}M^{s^{\prime}}\lambda_{0}^{s^{\prime}-s}\|{{\phi_{0}}}\|_{H^{s}}\quad\hbox{ for any }s^{\prime}>0.

(1.27)

Remark 1.14
.

Condition (
1.24
) arises naturally from the contribution of the dissipative term in the error estimate in our degenerating wave packet construction. Condition (
1.25
) is a technical condition arising due to the time dependence of
\mathring{\theta}=f(t,x_{2})
. We note that when
\delta_{1}=0
and
\tau_{M}\leq 1
, (
1.25
) already holds up to a logarithmic power of
\lambda_{0}
(from Assumption 4 for
\gamma
); see (
4.53
) and (
4.56
).

We introduce the following analogue of Definition
1.12
:

Definition 1.15
.

We say that the shear state
\mathring{\theta}
satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
B
is
linearly
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
unstable by degenerate dispersion according to Theorem
B
if there exist a sequence
(M_{(n)},\lambda_{0(n)})
satisfying (
1.18
)–(
1.20
), (
1.24
)–(
1.25
) and
\lambda_{0(n)}\geq\Lambda_{1}
such that

{\frac{\gamma(\lambda_{0(n)},\lambda_{0(n)})^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\gamma(\lambda_{0(n)},M_{(n)}\lambda_{0(n)})^{\frac{1}{2}}}}M_{(n)}^{s^{\prime}}\lambda_{0(n)}^{s^{\prime}-s}\to\infty,\qquad\tau_{M_{(n)}}\to 0.

In what follows, we shall often drop the proviso
according to Theorem
B
. As before, Theorem
B
implies that given a shear state
\mathring{\theta}
that is linearly
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
unstable by degenerate dispersion, no mapping from
H^{s}
to a set of
\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2}
-solutions to
L_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=0
in
L^{\infty}_{t}([0,\delta],H^{s^{\prime}})
can be bounded. Moreover, Definition
1.15
is a property of the system (
1.8
) (more precisely, of
\gamma
,
\kappa\upsilon
) in the same sense as before.

We now specialize
\gamma
and
\upsilon
to several model cases and exhibit instances of illposedness in the dissipative case given by Theorem
B
.

Corollary 1.16
.

Fix
\kappa>0
, let
\mathring{\theta}_{0}=f_{0}(x_{2})
a smooth odd function with
f_{0}^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
, and let
\mathring{\theta}=f(t,x_{2})
be the smooth solution to (
1.14
). In each of the following cases, Theorem
B
applies with the specified choice of
M
for any
0\leq\sigma<1
, provided that
\lambda_{0}
is sufficiently large depending on
\sigma
; moreover, in each case,
\tau_{M}\to 0
as
\lambda_{0}\to\infty
. As a result, (
1.8
) is linearly
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
unstable by degenerate dispersion for the specified values of
s^{\prime}
. In the table below, the restrictions on
M
and
(s,s^{\prime})
are
in addition
to those from the nondissipative case.

Multiplier
\Gamma

Dissipation
\Upsilon

Freq. growth
M

Lin.
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
inst.

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\beta}
,
\beta>1

\upsilon=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\alpha}
,
\alpha<\beta

M=\lambda_{0}^{\sigma\frac{\beta-\alpha}{1-(\beta-\alpha)}}

\frac{s^{\prime}}{1-(\beta-\alpha)}>s+\frac{\beta(\beta-\alpha)}{2(1-(\beta-\alpha))}

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle

\upsilon=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\alpha}
,
\alpha<1

M=\lambda_{0}^{\sigma\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}}

\frac{s^{\prime}}{\alpha}>s+\frac{1-\alpha}{2\alpha}

\gamma=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\beta}
,
\beta<1

\upsilon=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\alpha}
,
\alpha<\beta

M=\lambda_{0}^{\sigma\frac{\beta-\alpha}{1-(\beta-\alpha)}}

\frac{s^{\prime}}{1-(\beta-\alpha)}>s+\frac{\beta(\beta-\alpha)}{2(1-(\beta-\alpha))}

\gamma=\log^{\beta}(10+|{\xi}|)
,
\beta>0

\upsilon=\log^{\alpha}(10+|{\xi}|)
,
\alpha<\beta

M=\log^{\sigma(\beta-\alpha)}\lambda_{0}

s^{\prime}=s

The illposedness results in Corollary
1.16
are essentially sharp, in that it is not difficult to prove using standard energy estimates that in each case,
L^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=0
is locally well-posed in
H^{s}
for any
s\geq 0
if the dissipative exponent
\beta
is greater than
\alpha
.

Nonlinear illposedness results.

Remarkably, the linear norm growth results (Theorems
A
and
B
) may be extended to corresponding norm inflation properties of the nonlinear Cauchy problem.

Theorem C

(Nonlinear illposedness)
.

Assume that a shear steady state
\mathring{\theta}
with a quadratic degeneracy (resp. a shear state
\mathring{\theta}
satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
B
) is linearly
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
unstable by degenerate dispersion according to Theorem
A
(resp. Theorem
B
) with
s^{\prime}>{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{0}+3
.
Then
\mathring{\theta}
is nonlinearly
H^{s}\to H^{s^{\prime}}
ill-posed with respect to (
1.1
) (resp. (
1.8
)) in the following sense: For any
\epsilon,\delta,A>0
, there exists initial data
\theta_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
with
{\|{\theta_{0}}\|_{H^{s}}}<\epsilon
such that either

•

there exists no solution
\theta\in\mathring{\theta}+L^{\infty}([0,\delta];{H^{s^{\prime}}})
to (
1.1
) (resp. (
1.8
)) with
\left.\theta\right|_{t=0}=\left.\mathring{\theta}\right|_{t=0}+\theta_{0}
, or

•

any solution
\theta
belonging to
\mathring{\theta}+L^{\infty}([0,\delta];{H^{s^{\prime}}})
satisfy the growth

\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\|{{(\theta-\mathring{\theta})}(t,\cdot)}\|_{H^{s^{\prime}}}>A.\end{split}

Since
\mathring{\theta}(t,\cdot)
can be chosen to have an arbitrarily small
H^{s^{\prime}}\cap H^{s}
norm on a sufficiently short time interval in both the dissipative and non-dissipative cases, we immediately obtain the following illposedness statement with
\mathring{\theta}=0
:

Corollary 1.17
.

Assume that (
1.1
) (resp. (
1.8
)) is linearly
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
unstable by degenerate dispersion with
s^{\prime}>3+{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{0}
. Then (
1.1
) (resp. (
1.8
)) is nonlinearly
H^{s}\to H^{s^{\prime}}
ill-posed in the following sense: For any
\epsilon,\delta,A>0
, there exists initial data
\theta_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
with
{\|{\theta_{0}}\|_{H^{s}}}<\epsilon
such that either

•

there exists no solution
\theta\in L^{\infty}([0,\delta];{H^{s^{\prime}}})
to (
1.1
) (resp. (
1.8
)) with
\left.\theta\right|_{t=0}=\theta_{0}
, or

•

any solution
\theta
belonging to
L^{\infty}([0,\delta];{H^{s^{\prime}}})
satisfy the growth

\begin{split}\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\|{{\theta}(t,\cdot)}\|_{H^{s^{\prime}}}>A.\end{split}

Remark 1.18
.

While we shall refrain from giving details, we note that the assumption
s^{\prime}>3+{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{0}
can be lowered (up to
s^{\prime}>2
) when
\Gamma
becomes less singular. The issue is to control the nonlinearity in
L^{2}
using the hypothesis that
\theta\in H^{s^{\prime}}
, and more precisely, to have an estimate of the form

\begin{split}\|{\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma[\theta]\cdot\nabla\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\leq C(\|{\theta}\|_{H^{s^{\prime}}})\|{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

When
\Gamma=\Lambda^{\beta}
(which means
\beta_{0}=\beta
), using Sobolev inequalities we have that for any
s>2
,

\begin{split}\|{\nabla^{\perp}\Lambda^{\beta}[\theta]\cdot\nabla\theta}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|{\nabla\theta}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|{\nabla^{\perp}\Lambda^{\beta}[\theta]}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|{\theta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2-\frac{2+\beta}{s^{\prime}}}\|{\theta}\|_{H^{s^{\prime}}}^{\frac{2+\beta}{s^{\prime}}}\end{split}

and hence we see that the requirement is precisely
s^{\prime}\geq 2+\beta
.

By Corollary
1.11
, it follows that
any (
1.1
) satisfying our assumptions for
\gamma
is nonlinearly
H^{s}\to H^{s^{\prime}}
ill-posed for
s^{\prime}>3+{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{0}
.
Moreover, Corollaries
1.13
and
1.16
provide ranges of
(s,s^{\prime})
for which (
1.1
) and (
1.8
), respectively, are nonlinearly
H^{s}\to H^{s^{\prime}}
ill-posed for some model cases of
\gamma
and
\kappa\upsilon
.

When
\Omega=\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
and
s=s^{\prime}
sufficiently large, we may furthermore exhibit an
H^{s}(\Omega)
initial data corresponding to which no
L^{\infty}H^{s}
solution to (
1.1
) exists on any time interval.

Theorem D

(Nonexistence)
.

Let
\Omega=\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
. For any
s>\frac{3}{2}\beta_{0}+3
and
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\theta_{0}\in H^{s}(\Omega)
satisfying
\|{\theta_{0}}\|_{H^{s}}<\epsilon
such that for any
\delta>0
, there is no solution to (
1.1
) belonging to
L^{\infty}([0,\delta];H^{s}(\Omega))
with initial data
\theta_{0}
.

Result from
[
20
]
: Nonlinear well(!)posedness of the logarithmically singular case with loss of regularity.

Through Corollary
1.13
and Corollary
1.17
, we concluded nonlinear
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
illposedness of (
1.1
) for
s^{\prime}
strictly smaller than
s
when, say,
\gamma(\xi)=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{\beta}
for
\beta>0
. However, this conclusion did
not
apply to multipliers with slower growth, e.g.,
\gamma(\xi)=\log(10+|{\xi}|)
. This difference is no shortcoming of our approach. In the paper
[
20
]
, we actually obtain local well(!)posedness of (
1.1
) with
\gamma(\xi)=\log(10+|{\xi}|)
in Sobolev spaces with exponents that
decrease in time
. As a result, this system is
not
nonlinearly
H^{s}
-
H^{s^{\prime}}
ill-posed for any
s^{\prime}<s
; in the opposite direction, Corollaries
1.13
and
1.17
demonstrate that a decrease of the Sobolev exponent in time is inevitable.

Theorem E

(Wellposedness in the logarithmically singular system
[
20
]
)
.

Consider the logarithmically singular SQG equation, possibly with dissipation:

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\theta+u\cdot\nabla\theta+\kappa\Upsilon(\theta)=0,\\
u=\nabla^{\perp}\log(10+\Lambda)\theta.\end{split}

(1.28)

In the inviscid case
(\kappa=0)
, for any
s_{0}>4
and
\theta_{0}\in H^{s_{0}}
, there exists some
T=T(s_{0},\|{\theta_{0}}\|_{H^{s_{0}}})>0
such that there is a solution
\theta\in C([0,T];H^{4})
to (
1.28
) with initial data
\theta_{0}
satisfying

\begin{split}\|{\theta(t,\cdot)}\|_{H^{s(t)}}\leq C\|{\theta_{0}}\|_{H^{s_{0}}}\end{split}

for some continuous function
s(t)>4
of
t
with
s(0)=s_{0}
in
t\in[0,T]
. The solution is unique in the class
C([0,T];H^{4})
.

Furthermore, the dissipative system
(\kappa>0)
is locally well-posed in
C([0,T];H^{s})
for any
s>4
, as long as there exists some
\Xi_{0}>0
such that

\begin{split}\frac{\upsilon(|\xi|)}{\log(10+|\xi|)}\geq\frac{C_{s}}{\kappa}\|{\theta_{0}}\|_{H^{s}},\quad|\xi|>\Xi_{0}\end{split}

for some
C_{s}>0
depending only on
s
.

Remark 1.19
.

A similar wellposedness result can be proved for
\Gamma=\log^{\beta}(10+\Lambda)
with any
\beta\leq 1
.

Remark 1.20
.

The nonexistence theorem (Theorem
D
), when combined with Theorem
E
, shows that for logarithmic
\Gamma
, the local solution with
\theta_{0}\in H^{s}
for some
s>4
in general instantaneously escapes
H^{s}
for
t>0
.

1.6
Explicitly solvable toy model and discussion of difficulties

Let us present a toy model (
1.30
) which clearly demonstrates degeneration of linear solutions. To arrive at the toy model, we may start from the nonlinear equation

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\theta+\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma(\theta)\cdot\nabla\theta=0,\end{split}

and consider the linearization around the steady state
\mathring{\theta}(x_{1},x_{2})=-\frac{x_{2}^{2}}{2}
, under the
formal
assumption that
\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma(x_{2}^{2})\equiv 0
. The resulting equation for the perturbation, which is again denoted by
\theta
, is simply

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\theta+x_{2}\partial_{x_{1}}\Gamma(\theta)=0.\end{split}

(1.29)

We may separate
x_{1}
-dependence under the ansatz

\begin{split}\theta^{(\lambda_{0})}(t,x_{1},x_{2})=e^{i\lambda_{0}x_{1}}\varphi(t,x_{2})\end{split}

for some
\lambda_{0}
. Assume for simplicity that the multiplier
\gamma
for
\Gamma
is radial. Denoting the dual variable of
x_{2}
by
\xi
and taking the Fourier transform, we have with
\Lambda(\xi):=\sqrt{\lambda_{0}^{2}+\xi^{2}}
that

\begin{split}\lambda_{0}^{-1}\partial_{t}\widehat{\varphi}(\xi)+\gamma(\Lambda)\partial_{\xi}\widehat{\varphi}(\xi)=-(\partial_{\xi}\gamma)(\Lambda)\frac{\xi}{\Lambda}\widehat{\varphi}(\xi).\end{split}

(1.30)

This is simply a transport equation in
\xi
, which can be explicitly solved along the characteristics: we may define the trajectories
\xi(t;\xi_{0})
by

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{\xi}(t;\xi_{0})&=\lambda_{0}\gamma(\Lambda(\xi(t;\xi_{0}))),\\
\xi(0;\xi_{0})&=\xi_{0}.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.31)

The solution can be written by

\begin{split}\widehat{\varphi}(t,\xi(t;\xi_{0}))=\frac{\gamma(\Lambda(\xi_{0}))}{\gamma(\Lambda(\xi(t;\xi_{0})))}\widehat{\varphi}_{0}(\xi_{0}).\end{split}

(1.32)

The Jacobian of the flow map
\xi_{0}\mapsto\xi(t;\xi_{0})
is given simply by

\begin{split}\frac{\gamma(\Lambda(\xi(t;\xi_{0})))}{\gamma(\Lambda_{0})}.\end{split}

In the following, we shall take initial data
\widehat{\varphi}_{0}
which is sharply concentrated near
\xi_{0}\simeq\lambda_{0}
and introduce simplifying notation
\Lambda(t):=\Lambda(\xi(t;\lambda_{0}))
,
\Lambda_{0}:=\sqrt{2}\lambda_{0}
. Then, we have that

\begin{split}\|{\widehat{\varphi}(t)}\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\simeq\left(\frac{\gamma(\Lambda(t))}{\gamma(\Lambda_{0})}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|{\widehat{\varphi}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}.\end{split}

This is consistent with propagation of
\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\widehat{\varphi}
in
L^{2}
. On the other hand,

\begin{split}\|{|\xi|^{s}\widehat{\varphi}(t)}\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\simeq\frac{|\xi(t;\lambda_{0})|^{s}}{|\lambda_{0}|^{s}}\left(\frac{\gamma(\Lambda(t))}{\gamma(\Lambda_{0})}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|{|\xi|^{s}\widehat{\varphi}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}.\end{split}

Hence, for (
1.29
) to be ill-posed in
H^{s}
, it suffices to have for
|\lambda_{0}|\gg 1

and
T=T(\lambda_{0})\ll 1

that

\begin{split}\frac{|\xi(T;\lambda_{0})|^{s}}{|\lambda_{0}|^{s}}\left(\frac{\gamma(\Lambda(T;\lambda_{0}))}{\gamma(\Lambda_{0}(\lambda_{0}))}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\gg 1\end{split}

(1.33)

or simply

\begin{split}\frac{|\xi(T)|^{2s}}{\gamma(\xi(T))}\gg\frac{|\lambda_{0}|^{2s}}{\gamma(\lambda_{0})}.\end{split}

Assuming that
\gamma
is increasing, we have

\begin{split}\xi(t;\lambda_{0})\geq\lambda_{0}+t\lambda_{0}\gamma(\lambda_{0}).\end{split}

(This is expected to be sharp for small timescales.) Therefore, for some
T=T(\lambda_{0})
satisfying
\frac{1}{\gamma(\lambda_{0})}\ll T\ll 1
, we have
\xi(T)\gg\lambda_{0}
, and this will guarantee (
1.33
) for
s>0
large.

Given some concrete symbol
\gamma
(e.g.
\gamma(\Lambda)=\langle{\Lambda}\rangle^{\beta}
for some
\beta>0
), one can see the range of
s,s^{\prime}
where the toy model (
1.29
) is
H^{s}
–
H^{s^{\prime}}
unstable in the sense of Definition
1.12
, using the above formula for the solution in the Fourier variable. We leave the details of this computation for the interested reader. Let us demonstrate that in the logarithmic case (where
\gamma(\xi)\lesssim\log(\xi)
for large
|\xi|
),
s=s^{\prime}
is forced. Indeed, for
s(T)=s-MT
with some
M>0
independent of
\lambda_{0}
, we have

\begin{split}\frac{|\xi(T)|^{2s(T)}}{\gamma(\xi(T))}\lesssim\frac{|\lambda_{0}|^{2s}}{\gamma(\lambda_{0})},\end{split}

which shows a losing estimate in the scale of time-dependent Sobolev spaces
H^{s(T)}
. It suggests that there could be a similar estimate in the nonlinear case; this is precisely the content of
[
20
]
.

One may use the above model equation to understand the dissipative case as well. Then, (
1.29
) and (
1.30
) (after separating
x_{1}
-dependence) are simply replaced with

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\theta+x_{2}\partial_{x_{1}}\Gamma(\theta)=-\kappa\Upsilon(\theta)\end{split}

(1.34)

and

\begin{split}\lambda_{0}^{-1}\partial_{t}\widehat{\varphi}(\xi)+\gamma(\Lambda)\partial_{\xi}\widehat{\varphi}(\xi)=-(\partial_{\xi}\gamma)(\Lambda)\frac{\xi}{\Lambda}\widehat{\varphi}(\xi)-\kappa\upsilon(\Lambda)\widehat{\varphi}(\xi).\end{split}

(1.35)

(We are assuming that the symbol
\upsilon
is radial.) Again, this equation is solvable along characteristics. Using the solution, one may see that when
\upsilon\gg\gamma
, there is well-posedness in
H^{s}
-spaces, while
\upsilon\ll\gamma
still gives
H^{s}
illposedness.

Discussion of difficulties.

We are now in a good position to explain the main difficulties in establishing Sobolev illposedness for the actual linear homogeneous equations associated with (
2.1
) and (
2.3
), as well as the nonlinear equations (
1.1
) and (
1.8
). Comparing the inviscid linear case (
2.1
) with (
1.29
), there are two differences: (1) first, the principal coefficient is not exactly linear, and (2) second, a lower order term is present in (
2.1
).
With these two differences
combined
, it becomes a challenging problem to just construct an approximate solution to (
2.1
) which exhibits the same illposedness behavior with an explicit solution to (
1.29
). Meanwhile, once a good approximate construction has been constructed, it can be upgraded to illposedness results for (
2.1
), and even the nonlinear equation (
1.1
), using the robust testing (or duality) method introduced in
[
46
]
.

Henceforth, we focus on approximate solution construction. Regarding
difference (1),
it has been known that having an exactly linear principal coefficient significantly simplifies the analysis (Craig–Goodman
[
27
]
is a good example). We have seen in the above that in such a case, taking the Fourier transform results in a transport term. Moreover, one can perform WKB analysis with a linear phase. In view of this, when the principal term is given by a differential operator with a linearly degenerate coefficient, it is natural to first apply a coordinate transform which makes the coefficient exactly linear near the degeneracy; this was the approach in our previous work
[
46
]
. Such a coordinate change is not available in the case of pseudo-differential principal term. Next, regarding
difference (2)
, note that the lower order term in (
2.1
) is again given by a pseudo-differential operator, and its (generalized) order could become very close to that for the principal operator when
\Gamma
is only
slightly
singular (e.g. when
\gamma(\xi)=\log(10+|\xi|),\log(10+\log(10+|\xi|))
). In such cases, it is basically impossible to distinguish the lower order term from the principal term, and in the proof we indeed incorporate a part of the lower order term into the principal part.

In the dissipative linear case (
2.3
), the main idea is to use the same approximate solution to (
2.1
) and treat the dissipative term as an error. Nevertheless, there is yet another serious difficulty: in general, there is no nontrivial steady solutions to (
1.14
) and we have to work with time-dependent shear flows. In general, the strength of the degeneracy of the principal coefficient
\Gamma\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}
changes with time
3
3

3

In principle, the location of the degeneracy would move in time as well; in the current work, we prohibit such behavior by imposing even symmetry both in the shear profile
\mathring{\theta}
and the dissipative operator
\Upsilon
.
, whose effect cannot be regarded as a perturbation from the initial data
\Gamma\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}_{0}
. Again, with respect to this difficulty, the most problematic case is when
\Gamma
is slightly singular, because then the frequency growth is slower (hence most sensitive to time dependence of the background shear).

To overcome these difficulties, we develop a fairly general framework for directly constructing degenerating wave packets for a linear pseudo-differential equation of the form

\partial_{t}\phi+ip(t,x,D)\phi=0,

where
p(t,x,D)
takes into account not only the principal term but also key lower order terms in (
2.1
), and may possibly be time-dependent. While the formal derivation of the ansatz for the approximate solution is straightforward (see Section
3
), the difficulty is to rigorously control the ansatz and the error in sufficiently long time scales within which significant degeneration occurs. Among others, two key ideas in this work that allow us to resolve this difficulty are: observations concerning the Hamilton–Jacobi equation for
\partial_{t}+ip
and the associated transport equations that allow for controlling the ansatz in long enough time scales (see Section
4
) and sharp estimates for oscillatory integrals appearing in the symbol for the error term (see Section
5
). We refer the reader to Section
2.2
for a more detailed discussion of the key ideas.

1.7
Interpretation of strong illposedness

We would like to discuss the potential physical interpretations of strong illposedness, especially since we have demonstrated in the above that some generalized SQG equations with singular multipliers have physical origin.

Naïvely, strong illposedness for a PDE of physical origin (especially the nonexistence statement from Theorem
D
) suggests that the model is physically invalid and should therefore be rejected. At this point, one could argue that the illposedness simply arises from choosing the wrong function spaces: the illposedness results we obtained in this work pertain to Sobolev spaces rather than, for example, analytic (or Gevrey) spaces. However, we shall proceed under the assumption that this is
not
the case, which is supported by the rapid frequency growth inherent in the bicharacteristic ODE system. Actually, based on the fact that the frequency rate itself grows with the initial frequency, illposedness in analytic spaces for the Hall-MHD case was established in
[
46
]
. Returning to the point where we should decide whether or not to reject the model, one should consider several possibilities (and answer the resulting questions):

•

Incorporating a higher order term
. Indeed, for both of Hall-MHD and AM-LQG equations we discussed in the above, it is natural to have some dissipative terms, which makes the equations (at least locally in time) well-posed. Then the natural question is: what is the implication of strong illposedness for the dissipative case? More concretely, what happens in the limit for the sequence of solutions when the dissipation coefficient vanishes? At this point, it is worth mentioning that dissipation is not the only physical mechanism for fixing illposedness; in the case of Hall-MHD, it was reported recently in
[
22
]
that by adding “electron inertial effects” to the Hall-MHD system (this is referred to extended magnetohydrodynamics, or XMHD for short, system in the physics community), wellposedness in Sobolev spaces is restored, without incorporating the dissipation term for the magnetic field. This “inertial” term is hyperbolic and not parabolic. Indeed, physicists have reported (
[
1
,
61
]
) that in magnetohydrodynamic turbulence (which roughly describes the behavior of solutions in the zero-dissipation limit), there are three frequency regimes: the smallest is governed by ideal MHD, the intermediate by the Hall current effect, and the largest by the inertial effect.

•

Wellposedness theory with nondegenerate data
. Without the addition of any other terms, the equations could be perfectly valid for certain “regimes”: our linear and nonlinear illposedness results are crucially relying upon quadratic degeneracy of the background shear profile. It is possible that at least small perturbations of a linearly degenerate background is well-behaved (this is clear in the case of the linearized equation, at least). Indeed, in the case of Hall-MHD system, local wellposedness can be established near a nonzero constant magnetic field (
[
48
]
), which turns out to be the original physical setup by Lighthill
[
57
]
who first derived the model.

In conclusion, we emphasize that the illposedness results presented in this paper serve merely as a starting point for the mathematical study of generalized SQG equations with singular velocities, opening up several important and challenging problems.

1.8
Further open problems

We discuss a few technical open problems that naturally arise after this work.

•

Existence of weak solutions
. The paper
[
16
]
proves the existence of global in time
L^{p}
weak solutions for the case when
\Gamma=\Lambda^{\beta}
with
\beta<0
. It seems that their existence proof fails as soon as
\Gamma
becomes singular (in the sense that the multiplier diverges to infinity as
|\xi|\to\infty
). It would be interesting to see whether convex integration techniques can be adapted to give existence of solutions with singular multipliers.

•

Gevrey illposedness
. While we expect the singular SQG equations to be ill-posed in analytic (and even in Gevrey) spaces, it seems quite difficult to obtain such an illposedness statement based on the norm growth estimates in the current paper. For this purpose, it might be necessary to construct degenerating wave packet solutions for a larger interval of time. Note that our previous work on the illposedness of the Hall-MHD system
[
46
]
includes analytic/Gevrey illposedness. A similar problem, which could be also challenging, is to study whether the range of
(s,s^{\prime})
given in Corollary
1.13
is sharp. To this end, one may consider the perturbation dynamics near other steady states.

•

Nonexistence in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathbb{T}^{2}
. Note that our nonexistence statement (Theorem
D
) requires the physical domain to be
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
. When the domain is
\mathbb{T}^{2}
(or more generally a compact two-dimensional manifold), there is a serious difficulty in superposing degenerating wave packets to upgrade norm inflation to nonexistence. On the other hand, when the domain is
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the difficulty comes from spatially truncating the degenerating wave packets.

•

Illposedness for 3D vector systems
. One may consider the
active vector system
for the variable
B(t,\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
given by

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}B+\nabla\times((\nabla\times(-\Delta)^{-\alpha}B)\times B)=0,\\
&\nabla\cdot B=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.36)

for some
\alpha\geq 0
. This turns out to be a 3D generalization of SQG models, and interpolates between the 3D vorticity equations (when
\alpha=1
) and the E-MHD system (when
\alpha=0
)
[
19
,
15
]
. Local wellposedness as well as existence of some global solutions were obtained for
\alpha\geq 1/2
in
[
19
,
15
]
. It would be interesting to study local wellposedness of (
1.36
) for general values of
\alpha
.

1.9
Organization of the paper

The rest of the paper is divided into six sections, which we briefly describe below. The proofs of the main results in each section is largely independent of those from other sections.

•

Section
2
contain preliminary computations on the linearized operators and a preview of the proofs of main results.

•

Section
3
begins with algebraic preliminaries regarding pseudo-differential calculus. The equations satisfied by the phase and amplitude functions are fixed in this section. Then, we provide expansion formulas for the pseudo-differential operators appearing after the conjugation by the phase function. In particular, an explicit representation formula for the remainder operator is derived.

•

Section
4
deals with the equations for the phase and amplitude functions chosen in Section
3
. In this section, we fix the choice of initial data for the phase and amplitude functions and derive sharp high order estimates for them.

•

In Section
5
, we obtain operator bounds for symbols appeared in Section
3
.

•

In Section
6
, key estimates for the degenerating wave packet solutions are obtained, by applying sharp estimates from Section
4
to operator bounds from Section
5
.

•

All the main theorems are proved in Section
7
, combining all the ingredients.

2
Preliminaries and ideas of the proof

In Section
2.1
, we derive the linearized operators and discuss its energy structure. We then proceed to define the notion of a
L^{2}
solution, and derive the generalized energy identity. An outline of the proof together with some key ideas are given in Section
2.2
.

2.1
Energy structure of the linearized operator

2.1.1
Conjugated linearized operators

Note that
{\mathring{\theta}}=f(x_{2})
is a (formal) solution to (
1.1
) for any (regular, decaying) profile
f
. Indeed,

u\cdot\nabla{\mathring{\theta}}=\partial_{x_{2}}\psi(x_{2})\partial_{x_{1}}f(x_{2})-\partial_{x_{1}}\psi(x_{2})\partial_{x_{2}}f(x_{2})=0.

The direct linearization of (
1.1
) around
\mathring{\theta}
is given as follows:

\displaystyle L_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=\partial_{t}\phi-\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma\phi+\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\phi=0.

Indeed, writing
\theta=\mathring{\theta}+\phi
,

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma(\mathring{\theta}+\phi),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(f+\phi)+u\cdot\nabla(\mathring{\theta}+\phi)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\phi+\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\phi\cdot\nabla\mathring{\theta}+\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\phi+\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\phi\cdot\nabla\phi

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\phi-\nabla\Gamma\phi\cdot\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}+\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\phi+\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\phi\cdot\nabla\phi.

Already we may observe that

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=\partial_{t}\phi+\widetilde{P}_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi,

where the principal symbol of
\widetilde{P}_{\mathring{\theta}}
is

{p_{\mathring{\theta}}:=}-i\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}(x_{1},x_{2})\cdot\xi\Gamma(\xi)

where
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
, and we assumed that
\gamma(\xi)\to\infty
as
|{\xi}|\to\infty
. Note that
p_{\mathring{\theta}}
is purely imaginary. However, as we will see soon,
L_{\mathring{\theta}}
does not have a good energy structure; there is a problem with the sub-principal terms. For this reason, we will have to conjugate
L_{\mathring{\theta}}
by
\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

As a motivation, let us first discuss the energy structure of
L_{\mathring{\theta}}
. Our computation will be formal. We first write

\displaystyle\int L_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi\phi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\phi^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y-\int\left(\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma\phi\right)\phi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\frac{1}{2}\int\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\nabla\phi^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

Integrating
\nabla
by parts, the last term vanishes since
\nabla\cdot\nabla^{\perp}=0
. For the second term, we have the following chain of identities:

\displaystyle-\int\left(\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma\phi\right)\phi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int\left(\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma\phi\right)\phi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\frac{1}{2}\int\phi\Gamma\left(\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\phi\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int\phi[\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla,\Gamma]\phi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

However,
[\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla,\Gamma]
is symmetric and is not bounded in
L^{2}
, which is problematic.

We wish to remove
[\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla,\Gamma]
, we need to conjugate the equation. Motivated by the desire to make the principal term exactly anti-symmetric (alternatively, via a formal, symbolic-calculus computation), we work with the variable
\varphi
given by

\phi=\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi.

Accordingly, we define

\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}L_{\mathring{\theta}}(\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi).

We compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=\partial_{t}\varphi-\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi+\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi.

(2.1)

We now investigate the energy structure of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}
; as before, we present only a formal computation. We begin with

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int\varphi^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y-\int\left(\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\int\left(\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

By the symmetry of
\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}
and anti-symmetry of
\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla
, the second term vanishes. However, the last term does not vanish (cf. the computation for
L_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi
). For this term, we compute

\displaystyle\int\left(\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\left(\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y-\frac{1}{2}\int\varphi\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\left(\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\varphi\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\frac{1}{2}\int\left([\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\frac{1}{2}\int\varphi\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y-\frac{1}{2}\int\varphi\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}[\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla,\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}]\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\varphi\left([\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\right)\varphi\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

An important observation is that the operator

[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]

(2.2)

is bounded on
L^{2}
; hence this term is acceptable. There are many ways to see this fact. For instance, one may utilize the symbolic calculus developed in Section
3.1
below, and observe that
[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\in Op(S(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}))
and
\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\in Op(S(\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}))
, so (
2.2
) belongs to
Op(S(1))
(i.e., is a quantization of a classical pseudodifferential operator of order
0
) and thus is bounded on
L^{2}
. For the convenience of the reader, we include an independent elementary proof of boundedness of this operator below in Proposition
2.1
.

Finally, we state the linearized operator for (
1.8
):

\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=\partial_{t}\varphi-\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi+\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi+\kappa\Upsilon\varphi.

(2.3)

Note that
\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi=\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}L^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
\mathring{\theta}\in H^{s}
with
s
sufficiently large depending on
\Gamma
. Then, we have

\begin{split}\|{[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi}\|_{L^{2}}&\leq C(\Gamma,\mathring{\theta})\|{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}},\\
\|{\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\varphi}\|_{L^{2}}&\leq C(\Gamma,\mathring{\theta})\|{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}}\end{split}

for
\varphi\in L^{2}
.

Proof.

We provide a proof just for the first inequality, since the argument for the other is similar. For simplicity, we set
F=\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}
. Then, we have that the Fourier transform of
[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},F\cdot\nabla]\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi
is given by

\begin{split}I(\xi):=\int\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi)\hat{F}(\xi-\eta)i\eta\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\eta)\hat{\varphi}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta-\int\hat{F}(\xi-\eta)i\eta\hat{\varphi}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta,\end{split}

where
\hat{F}
and
\hat{\varphi}
are the Fourier transforms of
F
and
\varphi
, respectively.

We proceed differently depending on the size of frequencies of
F
and
\varphi
. First, when
|\xi-\eta|\geq|\eta|/10
, we simply take absolute values inside the integrals and use
|\xi|\lesssim|\xi-\eta|
,
\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi)\lesssim_{\Xi_{0}}1+\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi-\eta)
(see (
1.11
)) and
\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\eta)\lesssim 1
to bound

\begin{split}&\left|\int_{\{|\xi-\eta|\geq|\eta|/10\}}\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi)\hat{F}(\xi-\eta)i\eta\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\eta)\hat{\varphi}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta-\int_{\{|\xi-\eta|\geq|\eta|/10\}}\hat{F}(\xi-\eta)i\eta\hat{\varphi}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right|\\
&\quad\lesssim\left|\int_{\{|\xi-\eta|\geq|\eta|/10\}}(1+\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi-\eta))|\xi-\eta||\hat{F}(\xi-\eta)||\hat{\varphi}(\eta)|\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right|.\end{split}

Next, when
|\xi-\eta|<|\eta|/10
, we combine the integrals to rewrite
