\begin{split}\int_{\{|\xi-\eta|<|\eta|/10\}}\left(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi)-\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\eta)\right)\hat{F}(\xi-\eta)i\eta\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\eta)\hat{\varphi}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta.\end{split}

We now take the absolute value inside the integral and use the mean value theorem with the condition
|\xi-\eta|<|\eta|/10
to observe

\begin{split}\left|\left(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi)-\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\eta)\right)\eta\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\eta)\right|\lesssim|\xi-\eta|.\end{split}

Therefore, combining two cases, we obtain altogether

\begin{split}|I(\xi)|\lesssim\left|\int(1+\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi-\eta))|\xi-\eta||\hat{F}(\xi-\eta)||\hat{\varphi}(\eta)|\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right|\end{split}

and applying Young’s convolution inequality, we obtain

\begin{split}\|{I}\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}}\lesssim\|{(1+\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi))|\xi||\hat{F}(\xi)|}\|_{L^{1}_{\xi}}\|{\hat{\varphi}}\|_{L^{2}_{\xi}},\end{split}

which finishes the proof by the Plancherel theorem.
∎

2.1.2
Notion of a
L^{2}
solution

In the following, for simplicity, we consider (
2.1
) as a special case of (
2.3
) obtained by taking
\kappa=0
.
Recall from above that a sufficiently smooth and decaying solution to (
2.3
) satisfies

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\kappa\|{\Upsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\langle{\varphi,\left([\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\right)\varphi}\rangle=0.\end{split}

Since

\begin{split}\|{\left([\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\right)\varphi}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{\Gamma,\mathring{\theta}}\|{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}},\end{split}

we obtain from Grönwall’s inequality that

\begin{split}\|{\varphi(t)}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|{\varphi_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}\exp\left(C(\Gamma,\mathring{\theta})t\right).\end{split}

(2.4)

This motivates the following definition of a
L^{2}
-solution:

Definition 2.2
.

Given some interval
I=[0,\tau]
, we say that
\varphi
is a
L^{2}
-solution of
\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=0
if

•

\varphi\in C_{w}(I;L^{2})\cap L^{2}_{t}(I;\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Upsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2})
;

•

\varphi
satisfies
\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=0
in the sense of distributions;

•

\varphi
satisfies (
2.4
).

We say that
\phi
is an
\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2}
-solution of
L^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\phi=0
if
\varphi=\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi
is a
L^{2}
-solution of
\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=0
. Moreover, we simply drop the requirement
\varphi\in L^{2}_{t}(I;\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Upsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2})
in the inviscid case
\kappa=0
.

Here,
C_{w}(I;L^{2})
is a subspace of
L^{\infty}_{t}(I;L^{2})
containing functions weakly continuous in time with values in
L^{2}
. The space
L^{2}_{t}(I;\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Upsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2})
is defined by the norm
\int_{I}\|{\kappa^{\frac{1}{2}}\Upsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi(t)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t
.

We have the following existence result:

Proposition 2.3
.

Given any
\varphi_{0}\in L^{2}
, there is at least one
L^{2}
solution to
\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=0
satisfying Definition
2.2
for any
\kappa\geq 0
.

We omit the proof, which is a simple application of the Aubin–Lions lemma; see
[
46
, Appendix A]
for details.

2.1.3
Generalized energy identity

We now present the generalized energy identity, which is one of the main tools in the proof of linear and nonlinear illposedness. For the moment assume that
\varphi
and
\psi
are sufficiently smooth, decaying fast at infinity, and solve
\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=0
with errors
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\varphi}
and
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\psi}
; that is,

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\varphi},\qquad\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\psi=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\psi}.\end{split}

Then, we compute

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\langle{\varphi,\psi}\rangle+2\kappa\langle{\Upsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi,\Upsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\varphi},\psi}\rangle+\langle{\varphi,\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\psi}}\rangle+\langle{\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi,\psi}\rangle+\langle{\varphi,\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi}\rangle

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\langle{\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi,\psi}\rangle-\langle{\varphi,\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\psi}\rangle.

Similarly as in the derivation of the energy identity, we have that the third and fourth terms on the right hand side cancel each other, using the anti-symmetry of
\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla
. Next, following the proof of the energy identity, the last two terms can be combined as follows:

\begin{split}\langle{\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi,\psi}\rangle+\langle{\varphi,\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\psi}\rangle=\langle{\varphi,\left([\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\right)\psi}\rangle.\end{split}

Assuming for simplicity that
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\varphi}=0
, we have the following generalized energy identity:

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\varphi
be a
L^{2}
solution to
\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=0
on
I
in the sense of Definition
2.2
, and assume that
\psi
satisfy
\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\psi=\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\psi}
on
I
with regularity

\begin{split}\psi\in C_{t}(I;L^{2})\cap L^{2}_{t}(I;\kappa^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Upsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}L^{2})\cap L^{2}_{t}(I;H^{1}),\qquad\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\psi}\in L^{1}_{t}(I;L^{2}).\end{split}

Then, we have

\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\langle{\varphi,\psi}\rangle+2\kappa\langle{\Upsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi,\Upsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi}\rangle=\langle{\varphi,\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\psi}}\rangle+\langle{\varphi,\left([\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla]\right)\psi}\rangle\end{split}

(2.5)

on
t\in I
.

The proof follows from first mollifying
\varphi,\psi
and repeating the computations above, which gives a generalized energy identity with some error terms arising from the mollification. Then it is not difficult to observe that the mollification errors vanish as the mollification parameter goes to zero, using the assumed regularity of
\varphi,\psi,\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\psi}
. We omit the straightforward details (cf.
[
46
, Proposition 2.3]
).
Moreover, one may generalize the above proposition to the case when
\varphi
is a
L^{2}
solution with a
L^{1}_{t}(I;L^{2})
error, denoted by
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\varphi}
. Then, we again have (
2.5
) with
\langle{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\varphi},\psi}\rangle
added to the right hand side.

2.2
Key ideas

Outline of the proof.
We give an overall picture of the illposedness proofs. Given (
2.1
), most of the work goes into construction of
degenerating wave packet
solutions
\widetilde{\varphi}
to (
2.1
). Basically, we would like to construct some approximate solutions which behave similarly with the solution of the toy model (
1.32
), given a quadratically degenerate shear steady state
\mathring{\theta}
. Some key properties that are required for the degenerating wave packets solutions can be summarized as follows:

•

Frequency localization: the initial data is sharply concentrated near some frequency
\lambda_{0}\gg 1
;
\|{\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}}\|_{H^{s}}\sim\lambda_{0}^{s}
for
s\geq 0
.

•

Error bound: for some interval
I=[0,{t_{0}}]
with
{t_{0}}>0
,
\|{\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}[\widetilde{\varphi}]}\|_{L^{1}_{t}(I;L^{2})}\ll 1
. That is,
\widetilde{\varphi}
is an approximate solution to (
2.1
). From the energy identity, it also follows that
\|{\widetilde{\varphi}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(I;L^{2})}\lesssim\|{\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1
.

•

Decay of negative Sobolev norms (degeneration estimate): we have a decomposition
\widetilde{\varphi}=\widetilde{\varphi}^{main}+\widetilde{\varphi}^{small}
such that
\|{\widetilde{\varphi}^{small}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}(I;L^{2})}\ll 1
and
\lambda_{0}^{s}\|{\widetilde{\varphi}^{main}(\tau)}\|_{H^{-s}}\ll 1
for some
s>0
.
4
4

4

The decomposition
\widetilde{\varphi}=\widetilde{\varphi}^{main}+\widetilde{\varphi}^{small}
is necessary since we would like to take advantage of the amplitude function compactly supported
in space
. Therefore, unlike the toy model solution (which is compactly supported in the frequency side), there is some low-frequency part in
\widetilde{\varphi}
which does not degenerate.

(The large parameter is
\lambda_{0}\gg 1
.)

Even in the case of dissipative linear equation
\mathcal{L}^{(\kappa)}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=0
, the above requirements remain unchanged, except that the profile
\mathring{\theta}
becomes time-dependent (where
\partial_{t}f+\kappa\Upsilon f=0
): we shall always incorporate the dissipation term
\kappa\Upsilon\widetilde{\varphi}
as a part of the error term. Assuming for a moment that we are given
\widetilde{\varphi}
satisfying the above, the rest of the illposedness proof follows a
duality
or
generalized energy argument
that originated from
[
46
]
for Hall- and electron-MHD systems, which we now explain. For the moment we consider the linear illposedness result. To begin, simply take the initial data
\varphi_{0}=\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}
and apply the generalized energy identity (
2.5
) with
\psi=\widetilde{\varphi}
and
\varphi
some
L^{2}
-solution to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=0
on
I=[0,{t_{0}}]
associated with
\varphi_{0}
. We then obtain

\begin{split}\left|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\langle{\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}}\rangle\right|\lesssim\|{\varphi(t)}\|_{L^{2}}(\|{\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}[\widetilde{\varphi}](t)}\|_{L^{2}}+\|{\widetilde{\varphi}(t)}\|_{L^{2}}).\end{split}

Using

\begin{split}\|{\varphi(t)}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|{\varphi_{0}}\|_{L^{2}},\qquad\|{\widetilde{\varphi}(t)}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|{\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}},\end{split}

which is valid for
0\leq t\leq{t_{0}}
with
{t_{0}}=O_{\mathring{\theta}}(1)
, and then integrating in time,

\begin{split}\langle{\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}}\rangle(t)\geq\langle{\varphi_{0},\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}}\rangle-C\|{\varphi_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}(\|{\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}[\widetilde{\varphi}]}\|_{L^{1}([0,t];L^{2})}+t\|{\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}).\end{split}

Using the error bound, we can deduce for
t_{0}>0
small that
\langle{\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}}\rangle(t_{0})>\frac{9}{10}\langle{\varphi_{0},\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}}\rangle
(say). Then, combining this estimate with

\begin{split}\langle{\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}}\rangle({t_{0}})\leq\|{\varphi({t_{0}})}\|_{L^{2}}\|{\widetilde{\varphi}^{small}({t_{0}})}\|_{L^{2}}+\|{\varphi({t_{0}})}\|_{H^{s}}\|{\widetilde{\varphi}^{main}({t_{0}})}\|_{H^{-s}}\end{split}

and
\|{\varphi({t_{0}})}\|_{L^{2}}\|{\widetilde{\varphi}^{small}({t_{0}})}\|_{L^{2}}<\frac{1}{10}\langle{\varphi_{0},\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}}\rangle
(say), we deduce that
\lambda_{0}^{-s}\|{\widetilde{\varphi}({t_{0}})}\|_{H^{s}}\gg 1
. Here, the initial data
\varphi_{0}
only needs to satisfy that
\langle{\varphi_{0},\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}}\rangle
is comparable with
\|{\varphi_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}\|{\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}
.

To prove the nonlinear illposedness results, we assume towards a contradiction that a sufficiently smooth solution to the nonlinear equation exists, with initial data which is given by a small and smooth perturbation of some quadratically degenerate shear steady state
\mathring{\theta}
. Here, a key observation is that then the perturbation, after applying
\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}
, is a solution of the linear operator with a nonlinear error, and that the nonlinear error in
L^{2}
can be bounded in terms of the
L^{2}
-norm of the perturbation, under the assumption that the perturbation remains sufficiently smooth. This is responsible for the restriction on
s^{\prime}
in the nonlinear statements.

In what follows, we explain a few key ideas that are involved in the construction of degenerating wave packets. In
[
46
, Sections 1–2]
, a detailed introduction is given regarding the construction of degenerating wave packets for the case of Hall- and electron-MHD systems. For this reason, here we emphasize on how the additional difficulties (over the Hall- and electron-MHD cases) are handled.

Degenerating wave packet construction.
There is a general recipe for construction of wave packet solutions, sometimes referred to as the WKB ansatz; one prepares the ansatz

\begin{split}\widetilde{\varphi}={\Re\left(\exp(i\Phi)a\right)},\end{split}

and derive the equations that
\Phi
and
a
should satisfy, based on the given linear operator. The functions
\Phi
and
a
will be referred to as the
phase
and the
amplitude
, respectively, and basic assumption in the analysis is that
a
varies slower than
\Phi
(i.e.,
\partial_{t}a,\nabla a
are smaller than
\partial_{t}\Phi,\nabla\Phi
in a suitable way). In our problem, it is not only nontrivial to choose the correct evolution equations for the phase and amplitude but also to choose the initial data whose associated solution is well-behaved (in particular satisfying the required properties in the above) for a sufficiently long period of time. When the linear operator is given by a differential operator, this process of extracting the equations for the phase and amplitude is rather straightforward. See classical works of Birkhoff
[
5
]
and Mizohata
[
62
]
for the case of linear Schrödinger operator (possibly with lower order terms).
5
5

5

Furthermore, in the case of a differential operator, to deal with the variable coefficient of the principal term, one may work with a renormalized independent variable
x^{\prime}
which makes the coefficient into a constant. In turn, this allows us to propagate
\Phi(t,x^{\prime})\sim\xi_{0}\cdot x^{\prime}
, see below for the bicharacteristic ODE as well as the equation for
\Phi
. See the lecture notes of the second author
[
43
, Chap. 4]
where this analysis is carried out in some detail for the case of AM-LQG.

Before we proceed to explain how such choices are made, let us give some heuristics for the evolution of wave packets (for a more detailed outline, see Section
3.2
below). Roughly, we would like to regard
\widetilde{\varphi}
as a function which is well-localized both in the physical and Fourier variables, centered at some point
(X(t),\Xi(t))
in the phase space. Writing our linearized operator as
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}=\partial_{t}+ip_{\mathring{\theta}}
modulo lower order terms and observing that
p_{\mathring{\theta}}
is purely real, we expect that if
\widetilde{\varphi}
at the initial time corresponds to
(X_{0},\Xi_{0})
, then for
t>0
,
\widetilde{\varphi}(t)
corresponds to
(X(t),\Xi(t))
which is given by the solution to the
Hamiltonian ODE system
associated with
p_{\mathring{\theta}}(t,x,\xi)
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{X}&=\nabla_{\xi}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(t,X,\Xi),\\
\dot{\Xi}&=-\nabla_{x}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(t,X,\Xi),\end{aligned}\right.

(2.6)

with initial data
(X_{0},\Xi_{0})
. We shall take
|\Xi_{0}|\gg 1
and
X_{0}
sufficiently close to the degeneracy of
\mathring{\theta}
, and choose the sign of
\Xi_{0}
in a way that
X(t)
moves towards the degeneracy for
t>0
.

To actually construct wave packets following the above ODE trajectories, given
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}
, we begin with writing

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}=\partial_{t}+ip_{\mathring{\theta}}+s_{\mathring{\theta}}+r_{\mathring{\theta}},\end{split}

(2.7)

where
s_{\mathring{\theta}}
and
r_{\mathring{\theta}}
are smoother than
ip_{\mathring{\theta}}
by order at least one and two, respectively. Here, we take

\begin{split}p_{\mathring{\theta}}:=-\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\xi\gamma(\xi)+\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\xi\end{split}

and define the equation for
\Phi
by

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\Phi+p_{\mathring{\theta}}(t,x,\nabla\Phi)=0.\end{split}

This scalar first-order PDE is called the
Hamilton–Jacobi equation
associated with the symbol
p_{\mathring{\theta}}(t,x,\xi)
. It is formally derived by collecting the “worst terms” in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\widetilde{\varphi}
, i.e., those only involving
\partial_{t}\Phi
or
\nabla\Phi
, but
not

\partial_{t}a
or
\nabla a
. Indeed, this procedure can be directly implemented when
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}
is a differential operator, and in the present pseudodifferential case, we rely on symbolic calculus (in particular, Proposition
3.3
) to extract such terms. We remark that the Hamilton–Jacobi equation provides a direct link between the wave packet construction and the Hamiltonian ODE (
2.6
) for
(X,\Xi)(t)
, in the sense that (
2.6
) is nothing but the bicharacteristic ODEs associated with the Hamilton–Jacobi equation (viewed as a scalar first-order PDE).

When
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}
is a differential operator,
ip_{\mathring{\theta}}
can be simply chosen to be the principal part, since the remainder will automatically be of at least one order lower. However, in our case, we must exercise care in selecting these parts. Indeed, observe that we have taken the main part of the lower order operator
\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}
into
p_{\mathring{\theta}}
. This is inevitable, as these two terms in
p_{\mathring{\theta}}
are barely distinguishable when
\gamma
is only slightly singular, and in general we do not have any control over the coefficient
\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}
. As a result of our choice of
p_{\mathring{\theta}}
, we are forced to design the initial data
\Phi_{0}
which takes the lower order term
\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\xi
into account. Again, the goal is to propagate that
\nabla\Phi(t,x)\simeq\Xi(t)
for
x\simeq X(t)
for a sufficiently long interval of time, within which degeneration occurs. We will return to this point below, under “
Estimate for the derivatives of the phase and amplitude
.”

Returning to the expression (
2.7
), note that the difference
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}-(\partial_{t}+ip_{\mathring{\theta}})
consists of a few commutators. The operator
s_{\mathring{\theta}}
is defined by taking the principal terms of the commutators which are given by Poisson brackets, and then we simply write
r_{\mathring{\theta}}
for the remainder (see Proposition
3.4
for the precise expressions).
We then declare that
a
solves the linear transport equation

\begin{split}\partial_{t}a+\nabla_{\xi}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x,\nabla\Phi)\cdot\nabla a+\left(\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}_{\xi}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x,\nabla\Phi):\nabla^{2}\Phi+s_{\mathring{\theta}}(x,\nabla\Phi)\right)a=0.\end{split}

with the initial data
a_{0}
taken to be a smooth bump function supported in a small neighborhood of
X_{0}
. Heuristically, modulo the zeroth order term, this transport equation is derived by collecting the “second worst terms” in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\widetilde{\varphi}
. Indeed, when
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}
is a differential operator, the principal part
\partial_{t}a+\nabla_{\xi}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x,\nabla\Theta)\cdot\nabla a
consists precisely of those terms in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\widetilde{\varphi}
involving only one factor of
\partial_{t}a
or
\nabla a
; in the present pseudodifferential case, we rely on symbolic calculus (Proposition
3.3
) to extract such terms. Observe that (
2.6
) is also the bicharacteristic ODEs for the this linear transport equation. Finally, we note note that the last (zeroth order) term in the preceding transport is chosen precisely so that
\|{a(t,\cdot)}\|_{L^{2}}
is conserved; see Proposition
4.12
below.

After solving the equations for
\Phi
and
a
to find
\widetilde{\varphi}={\Re(e^{i\mathbf{\Phi}}a)}
, we then need to estimate Sobolev norms of both
\widetilde{\varphi}
and the error
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}(\widetilde{\varphi})
. To begin with, compared with
p_{\mathring{\theta}}
, the operator
r_{\mathring{\theta}}
is smoother by order 2, and therefore we expect
r_{\mathring{\theta}}(\widetilde{\varphi})
to be small. However, in the error there is also a contribution from
ip_{\mathring{\theta}}
and
s_{\mathring{\theta}}
, which occurs since
e^{i\Phi}
is not completely localized in the frequency space. To see this contribution, we introduce the conjugation operator
{}^{(\Phi)}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x,D)
defined by

\begin{split}e^{-i\Phi}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x,D)(e^{i\Phi}a)=:{}^{(\Phi)}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x,D)a.\end{split}

We can then formally expand (more precisely, see Proposition
3.3
)

\begin{split}{}^{(\Phi)}r_{p}:={}^{(\Phi)}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x,\xi)-\left(p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x,\nabla\Phi)+\nabla_{\xi}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x,\nabla\Phi){\nabla\Phi}-\frac{i}{2}\nabla_{\xi}^{2}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x,\nabla\Phi)\nabla^{2}\Phi\right).\end{split}

Note that the last two terms are taken into account in the equation for
a
above. The symbol for
{}^{(\Phi)}r_{p}
can be expressed explicitly by an oscillatory integral, and we need a sharp bound for its operator norm into
L^{2}
. It turns out that both for the oscillatory integral bound and the degeneration estimate, the key step is to find the
inverse wave packet scale

\mu(t)
such that
\mu(t)\ll\lambda(t)
,
|\partial^{k+1}\Phi(t)|\lesssim\mu^{k}(t)\lambda(t)
(on the support of
a(t)
), and
|\partial^{k}a(t)|\lesssim\mu^{k}(t)
, where
\lambda(t)\simeq|\partial\Phi(t)|(\simeq|{\Xi(t)}|)
is the overall
wave packet frequency
. Below, let us briefly explain how it is done.

Estimate for the derivatives of the phase and amplitude.

A basic difficulty in controlling the high derivatives of
\partial\Phi
is that
\partial^{2}\Phi
obeys a Ricatti-type ODE along characteristics, which tends to blow up in finite time (indeed, such a blow up corresponds to a focal point along a bicharacteristic). Therefore, some care is needed to control
\partial^{2}\Phi
on a sufficiently long time interval, so as to see the effect of degeneration. Equivalently, we need to carefully make a suitable choice of the initial data
\Phi(t=0)
for
\Phi
.

When the shear profile
\mathring{\theta}=f(x_{2})
is time-independent and degenerate at
x_{2}=0
, it turns out that the following solution given by
separation of variables
avoids the basic difficulty and achieves the required bounds:

\begin{split}\mathbf{\Phi}(t,x)=E\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{0}x_{1}+\partial_{x_{2}}^{-1}\left(\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{-1}\left(\frac{E+\Gamma f^{\prime}}{-f^{\prime}}\right)\right),\end{split}

where

\begin{split}E=-f^{\prime}(\overline{x})\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1-\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})-\Gamma f^{\prime}(\overline{x})\end{split}

with some
\overline{x}>0
and
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small,
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\cdot):=\gamma(\lambda_{0},\cdot)
, and
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{-1}
is the inverse of
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
. As in the solution of the toy model above, we have separated out the dependence in
x_{1}
, which does not change in time due to the
x_{1}
-independence of the linear operator. While the formula looks somewhat complicated, when
p_{\mathring{\theta}}
is given by a differential operator, this form corresponds to the linear ansatz in the renormalized coordinates in which the coefficient of the principal symbol of
p_{\mathring{\theta}}
is non-degenerate, which seems to be a natural choice. This choice of initial data is especially important in the case of time-dependent background
\mathring{\theta}=f(t,x_{2})
. As discussed before, already in the case
k=1
, the equation for
\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\Phi
becomes a Riccati-type ODE along characteristics, which could in principle grow much faster than
|\partial_{x}\Phi|^{2}\simeq\lambda(t)^{2}
. On the other hand, one can check that our choice of
\Phi(t=0)
makes the variable

\begin{split}h(t,X_{2}(t)):=\frac{\partial_{\xi_{2}}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(t,X_{2}(t),\Xi_{2}(t))}{\partial_{x_{2}}p_{\mathring{\theta}}(t,X_{2}(t),\Xi_{2}(t))}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\Phi(t,X_{2}(t))+1\end{split}

vanish at
t=0
. (Here we are neglecting the dependence on
x_{1}
and
\xi_{1}
.) Moreover, one can check that
the quantity
h
satisfies a remarkably simple equation
, which allows to propagate
|h(t)|\ll 1
for a suitably long time and in turn gives a sharp bound for
\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\Phi(t,X_{2}(t))
. Moreover, we obtain a sharp control on the instantaneous expansion of characteristics

\left.\partial_{x_{2}}(\partial_{\xi_{2}}p(t,x_{2},\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi(t,x_{2})))\right|_{t,x_{2}=X_{2}(t)}=-\left(\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi_{2}(t))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi_{2}(t))}-\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime\prime}(\Xi_{2}(t))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi_{2}(t))}\right)\dot{\Xi}_{2}+\cdots=:-A(t)+\cdots.

The wave packet scale
\mu^{-1}
is then nothing but the product of the initial scale
\Delta x_{0}
and the integrated expansion factor, i.e.,

\mu^{-1}\simeq\Delta x_{0}\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{t}A(t^{\prime})\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\right)\simeq\Delta x_{0}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda(t))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})}\qquad\hbox{(up to a small power of $\lambda_{0}$).}

The actual proof of the bound
\partial_{x_{2}}^{k+1}\Phi\lesssim\mu(t)^{k}\lambda(t)
proceeds by first propagating sharp bounds for
\partial_{x_{2}}^{k-1}h(t)
, and then converting it to that for
\partial_{x_{2}}^{k+1}\Phi
. It turns out to be essential to take advantage of the cancellations embedded in the transformation from
\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\Phi
to
h
, which are very difficult to see when one works directly with the equations for
\partial_{x_{2}}^{k+1}\Phi
. Given the bounds for
\partial_{x_{2}}^{k+1}\Phi(t)
, it is relatively straightforward to obtain the bounds for
\partial_{x_{2}}^{k}a(t)
.

3
Algebraic preliminaries for degenerate wave packet construction

Our goal in Sections
3
–
6
is to construct an approximate solution with initial frequency
O(\lambda_{0})
to the equation
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=0
that is valid for times
[0,\frac{1}{1-\epsilon(\lambda_{0})}t_{f}(\tau_{M})]
, where
\mathring{\theta}
,
M
and
\tau_{M}
obey the hypotheses of either Theorem
A
(steady state case) or Theorem
B
(
f
is time-dependent but even) and
\epsilon(\lambda_{0})
is a small parameter that will be fixed in the construction (see Section
4.1
). We look for an approximate solution of the form

\widetilde{\varphi}=\Re\left(a(t,x)e^{i\mathbf{\Phi}(t,x)}\right),

where the
amplitude

a(t,x)
is “slowly varying” compared to the real-valued
phase

\mathbf{\Phi}
, such that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\widetilde{\varphi}
is sufficiently small. Anticipating the spatial degeneration property of
\widetilde{\varphi}
(which comes with the frequency growth property that we want), we will refer to such an object
\widetilde{\varphi}
as a
degenerating wave packet
adapted to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}
.

In order to construct a degenerating wave packet
\widetilde{\varphi}
and, more importantly, to bound the error
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\widetilde{\varphi}
, we need to develop tools for computing the action of the multiplier
\Gamma
on
ae^{i\mathbf{\Phi}}
. Section
3.1
is devoted to accomplishing this task. Then in Section
3.2
, we use the algebraic identities derived in Section
3.1
to specify the construction of the phase
\mathbf{\Phi}
and the amplitude
a
of a degenerate wave packet. The tasks of verifying the degeneration property of
\widetilde{\varphi}
and bounding the error
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\widetilde{\varphi}
are deferred to Section
6
, after further necessary tools are developed (see also the end of Section
3.2
).

3.1
Some symbolic calculus

In this subsection, it will be convenient to generalize the set-up a bit and work with classical pseudo-differential operators with generalized order on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let
m=m(\xi)
be a smooth, even, positive, and slowly varying (i.e.,
|{\partial_{\xi}^{I}m(\xi)}|\lesssim_{I}\langle{\xi}\rangle^{-|{I}|}m(\xi)
for any multi-index
I
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
) symbol on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. As introduced in Section
1.5
,
\gamma(\xi)
is an example of such a symbol on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and so is
\gamma^{-1}(\xi)
. As in the case of Fourier multipliers, we say that
a symbol
p=p(x,\xi)
belongs to the class
S(m)
if

|{\partial_{x}^{J}\partial_{\xi}^{I}p(x,\xi)}|\lesssim_{I,J}\langle{\xi}\rangle^{-|{I}|}m(\xi)\quad\hbox{ for every pair of multi-indices $I$, $J$.}

(3.1)

When
m=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{s}
,
S(m)
coincides with the class of classical symbols of order
s
. Nonstandard examples of
m
include
m(\xi)=\log^{s}(10+|{\xi}|)
,
m(\xi)=\log^{s}(10+\log(10+|{\xi}|))
etc.

Given
p(x,\xi)\in S(m)
, we define its
left quantization
to be the operator

{p(x,D_{1},D_{2})u=\int p(x,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})u(y)e^{i(\xi_{1}(x^{1}-y^{1})+\xi_{2}(x^{2}-y^{2}))}\,\mathrm{d}y^{1}\mathrm{d}y^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}}.}

(3.2)

Accordingly, we use the notation
D_{j}
for the quantization of
\xi_{j}
, or equivalently, the operator
i^{-1}\partial_{x^{j}}
. We will also often use the shorthand
D=(D_{1},D_{2})
and simply write
p(x,D)=p(x,D_{1},D_{2})
. We denote by
Op(S(m))
the space of all left quantizations of symbols in
S(m)
.

We begin by stating the basic symbolic calculus for composition of
Op(S(m))
-operators.

Lemma 3.1

(Symbolic calculus)
.

Let
p\in S(m_{p})
and
q\in S(m_{q})
. Then the composition
p(x,D)q(x,D)
belongs to
Op(S(m_{p}m_{q}))
, and for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, its symbol
p\circ q(x,\xi)
obeys

p\circ q(x,\xi)-\sum_{\alpha:|{\alpha}|\leq N-1}\frac{1}{\alpha!i^{|{\alpha}|}}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}p(x,\xi)\partial_{x}^{\alpha}q(x,\xi)\in S(\langle{\xi}\rangle^{-N}m_{p}m_{q}).

(3.3)

In particular,

p\circ q(x,\xi)-q\circ p(x,\xi)-i^{-1}\{p,q\}(x,\xi)\in S(\langle{\xi}\rangle^{-2}m_{p}m_{q}),

(3.4)

where
\{p,q\}=\sum_{j}\left(\partial_{\xi_{j}}p\partial_{x_{j}}q-\partial_{x_{j}}p\partial_{\xi_{j}}q\right)
is the Poisson bracket.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is analogous to the standard case
m_{p}(\xi)=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{s_{p}}
,
m_{q}(\xi)=\langle{\xi}\rangle^{s_{q}}
, so we shall only sketch the main points.
Formally, the symbol
p\circ q(x,\xi)
for the composition
p(x,D)q(x,D)
is given by

p\circ q(x,\xi)=\iint p(x,\eta)q(y,\xi)e^{-i(x-y)\cdot(\xi-\eta)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\mathrm{d}y.

Then
p\circ q\in S(m_{p}m_{q})
and the expansion (
3.3
) then follows, as usual, by Taylor expansion of
p(x,\eta)
around
\eta=\xi
, integration by parts in
y
and (
3.1
). Moreover, (
3.4
) follows from (
3.3
). ∎

The following conjugation result will be a starting point for our degenerating wave packet construction.

Lemma 3.2

(Conjugation by
e^{i\mathbf{\Phi}}
)
.

Let
p\in S(m)
and
\mathbf{\Phi}\in C^{\infty}
. Then we have

\displaystyle e^{-i\mathbf{\Phi}}p(x,D)(ae^{i\mathbf{\Phi}})={}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p(x,D)a,

where

{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p(x,\xi)=\iint p(x,\eta)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y.

(3.5)

Observe carefully that we have not placed
{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p(x,\xi)
in any standard symbol class. In Propositions
3.3
and
5.1
, we will obtain an expansion and a bound for the operator
{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p(x,\xi)
that is adapted to the specific scenario we are interested in.

Proof.

Indeed,

\displaystyle e^{-i\mathbf{\Phi}}p(x,D)(ae^{i\mathbf{\Phi}})

\displaystyle=\iint p(x,\xi)a(y)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+\xi\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{(2\pi)^{d}}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\iiint p(x,\xi)\hat{a}(\eta)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+\xi\cdot(x-y)+\eta\cdot y)}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{(2\pi)^{d}}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int\left(\iint p(x,\xi)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\xi-\eta)\cdot(x-y)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\xi}{(2\pi)^{d}}\mathrm{d}y\right)\hat{a}(\eta)e^{ix\cdot\eta}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}},

so switching the variables
\xi
and
\eta
, we obtain the desired claim.
∎

For our construction, we would like to expand
{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p
under the assumption that
\xi
and the characteristic frequency of
\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}
(bounded by
\mu
in what follows) are smaller than the typical magnitude of
\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}
(denoted by
\lambda
in what follows). To begin with, the stationary set for the phase of the oscillatory integral on the RHS of (
3.5
) is

\displaystyle\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-(\eta-\xi)=0,\quad x-y=0,

or equivalently,
\eta=\xi+\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)
and
y=x
. Our assumption leads us to also expand in
\xi
about
\xi=0
. Following such a route, we are led to the following formulae for the expansion and the remainder.

Proposition 3.3

(Formal expansion of
{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p
)
.

Let
p\in S(m)
and
\mathbf{\Phi}\in C^{\infty}
. The symbol
{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p(x,\xi)
admits expansions of the form

\displaystyle{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi})+{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}r_{p,-1}(x,\xi)

(3.6)

\displaystyle=p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi})+\sum_{j}\xi_{j}\partial_{\xi_{j}}p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}(x))-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{j,k}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\partial_{\xi_{k}}p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}(x))\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}r_{p,-2}(x,\xi),

where

\displaystyle{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}r_{p,-1}(x,\xi)

(3.7)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\iint\partial_{\xi_{j}}p(x,\sigma\eta+(1-\sigma)\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\sigma\right)\xi_{j}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-i\int_{0}^{1}(1-\sigma)\iint\partial_{\xi_{j}}\partial_{\xi_{k}}p(x,\sigma\eta+(1-\sigma)\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)

\displaystyle\times e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\sigma,

and

\displaystyle{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}r_{p,-2}(x,\xi)

(3.8)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{1}(1-\sigma)\iint\partial_{\xi_{j}}\partial_{\xi_{k}}p(x,\sigma\eta+(1-\sigma)\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\sigma\right)\xi_{j}\xi_{k}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-i\left(\int_{0}^{1}(1-\sigma)\left(\frac{3}{2}-\sigma\right)\iint\partial_{\xi_{j}}\partial_{\xi_{k}}\partial_{\xi_{\ell}}p(x,\sigma\eta+(1-\sigma)\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\partial_{k}\partial_{\ell}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\phantom{\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-i\int_{0}^{1}(1-\sigma)(\frac{3}{2}-\sigma)\iint}\times e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\sigma\right)\xi_{j}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\sigma)^{2}\iint\partial_{\xi_{j}}\partial_{\xi_{k}}\partial_{\xi_{\ell}}p(x,\sigma\eta+(1-\sigma)\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\partial_{\ell}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)

\displaystyle\times e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\sigma

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\sigma)^{2}\iint\partial_{\xi_{j}}\partial_{\xi_{k}}\partial_{\xi_{\ell}}\partial_{\xi_{m}}p(x,\sigma\eta+(1-\sigma)\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\partial_{j}\partial_{m}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)\partial_{k}\partial_{\ell}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)

\displaystyle\times e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\sigma,

where the repeated indices in (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) are implicitly summed.

The formal expansion of
{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p
is standard; see, for instance,
[
75
, Chapter VII]
. The point of Proposition
3.3
is the explicit formulae for the remainder symbols
{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}r_{p,-j}
, which we shall analyze in Section
5
with the specific estimates on
\partial_{x}^{k}\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}
in our problem, to be proved in Section
4
.

Proof.

To ease the notation, in what follows, we work with the convention that the repeated indices are summed. Moreover, we introduce the
n
-th order remainder symbol

r^{(n)j_{1}\ldots j_{n}}[p(x,\cdot)](\xi_{1},\xi_{0})=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\xi_{j_{1}}}\cdots\partial_{\xi_{j_{n}}}p(x,\sigma\xi_{1}+(1-\sigma)\xi_{0})n(1-\sigma)^{n-1}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma.

(3.9)

Then the
(n-1)
-th Taylor expansion of
p(x,\xi)
in
\xi
about
\xi_{0}
takes the form

\begin{split}p(x,\xi_{1})&=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\frac{1}{m!}\partial_{\xi_{j_{1}}}\cdots\partial_{\xi_{j_{m}}}p(x,\xi_{0})(\xi_{1}-\xi_{0})^{j_{1}}\cdots(\xi_{1}-\xi_{0})^{j_{m}}\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{n!}r^{(n)j_{1}\ldots j_{n}}[p(x,\cdot)](\xi_{1},\xi_{0})(\xi_{1}-\xi_{0})^{j_{1}}\cdots(\xi_{1}-\xi_{0})^{j_{n}}.\end{split}

(3.10)

Also note the useful recursive identity (which is simply an integration by parts in
\sigma
)

r^{(n)j_{1}\ldots j_{n}}[p(x,\cdot)](\xi_{1},\xi_{0})=\partial_{\xi_{j_{1}}}\cdots\partial_{\xi_{j_{n}}}p(x,\xi_{0})+\sum_{j_{n+1}}\frac{1}{n+1}r^{(n+1)j_{1}\ldots j_{n}j_{n+1}}[p(x,\cdot)](\xi_{1},\xi_{0})(\xi_{1}-\xi_{0})^{j_{n+1}}.

(3.11)

In view of the stationary set of the phase and the expectation that
|{\xi}|\ll|{\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}}|
, we wish to expand
p(x,\eta)
in
\eta
about
\eta=\xi+\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)
:

\displaystyle{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\iint p(x,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\iint r^{(1)j}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))(\eta_{j}-\partial_{j}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y.

Note the identity

\displaystyle(\eta_{j}-\partial_{j}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}

\displaystyle=(\xi_{j}+\eta_{j}-\xi_{j}-\partial_{j}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}

\displaystyle=\xi_{j}e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}+i\partial_{y^{j}}e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}.

(3.12)

After integrating
\partial_{y^{j}}
by parts, we obtain

\displaystyle{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi})+\xi_{j}\iint r^{(1)j}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\iint r^{(1)j}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))(\eta_{j}-\xi_{j}-\partial_{j}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi})+\xi_{j}\iint r^{(1)j}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\iint r^{(1)j}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))i\partial_{y^{j}}e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi})+\xi_{j}\iint r^{(1)j}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-i\iint\left(\partial_{y^{j}}\left(r^{(1)j}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\right)\right)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y,

which already proves (
3.7
).

We are interested in the next order terms, so we expand

r^{(1)j}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))=\partial_{\xi_{j}}p(x,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))+\frac{1}{2}r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))(\eta_{k}-\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)).

Thus,

\displaystyle{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi})+\xi_{j}(\partial_{\xi_{j}}p)(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}(x))-\left.i\partial_{y^{j}}\partial_{\xi_{j}}p(x,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\right|_{y=x}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\frac{1}{2}\xi_{j}\iint r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))(\eta_{k}-\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\frac{i}{2}\iint\left(\partial_{y^{j}}\left(r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))(\eta_{k}-\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\right)\right)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi})+\xi_{j}(\partial_{\xi_{j}}p)(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}(x))-i\partial_{\xi_{j}}\partial_{\xi_{k}}p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}(x))\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\frac{1}{2}\xi_{j}\iint r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))(\eta_{k}-\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\frac{i}{2}\iint r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\frac{i}{2}\iint\left(\partial_{y^{j}}\left(r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\right)\right)(\eta_{k}-\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y.

For the second to last term, we also expand

r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))=\partial_{\xi_{j}}\partial_{\xi_{k}}p(x,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))+\frac{1}{3}r^{(3)jk\ell}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))(\eta_{\ell}-\partial_{\ell}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)),

so that

\displaystyle{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi})+\xi_{j}(\partial_{\xi_{j}}p)(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}(x))-\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\partial_{\xi_{k}}p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}(x))\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\frac{1}{2}\xi_{j}\iint r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))(\eta_{k}-\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\frac{i}{6}\iint r^{(3)jk\ell}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)(\eta_{\ell}-\partial_{\ell}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\frac{i}{2}\iint\left(\partial_{y^{j}}\left(r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\right)\right)(\eta_{k}-\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y.

Using (
3.12
) and integrating by parts, we get

\displaystyle{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}p(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi})+\xi_{j}(\partial_{\xi_{j}}p)(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}(x))-\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\partial_{\xi_{k}}p(x,\partial_{x}\mathbf{\Phi}(x))\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\frac{1}{2}\xi_{j}\xi_{k}\iint r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\frac{i}{2}\xi_{j}\iint\left(\partial_{y^{k}}\left(r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\right)\right)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\frac{i}{6}\xi_{\ell}\iint r^{(3)jk\ell}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\frac{1}{6}\iint\left(\partial_{y^{\ell}}\left(r^{(3)jk\ell}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\mathbf{\Phi}(y)\right)\right)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\frac{i}{2}\xi_{k}\iint\left(\partial_{y^{j}}\left(r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\right)\right)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\frac{1}{2}\iint\left(\partial_{y^{j}}\partial_{y^{k}}\left(r^{(2)jk}[p(x,\cdot)](\eta,\partial_{y}\mathbf{\Phi}(y))\right)\right)e^{i(\mathbf{\Phi}(y)-\mathbf{\Phi}(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\mathrm{d}y.

Hence, we obtain (
3.6
) with
{}^{(\mathbf{\Phi})}r_{p}(x,\xi)
consisting of all terms on the RHS except for the first three terms. Recalling (
3.9
), (
3.8
) then follows after a straightforward computation.
∎

3.2
Specialization to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}
and equations for the phase and the amplitude

We now return to the problem at hand. In view of the
x_{1}
-independence of
\mathring{\theta}
, we shall choose
\mathbf{\Phi}
and
a
in the separated form

\mathbf{\Phi}(t,x)=\lambda_{0}x_{1}+\Phi(t,x_{2}),\quad a=a(t,x_{2}),

where
\lambda_{0}
is an integer. Using standard symbolic calculus (Lemma
3.1
), we first rewrite
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}
in a form that is more convenient to apply Lemma
3.2
and Proposition
3.3
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\mathring{\theta}(t,x)=f(t,x_{2})
and
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
. We have

\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}(e^{i\lambda_{0}x_{1}}\psi)=e^{i\lambda_{0}x_{1}}\left(\partial_{t}\psi+ip_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},D_{2})\psi+s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},D_{2})\psi+r_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},D_{2})\psi\right),

(3.13)

where

\displaystyle p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=\partial_{x_{2}}f(t,x_{2})\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi_{2})+(\partial_{x_{2}}\Gamma f)(t,x_{2})\lambda_{0},

\displaystyle s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}f(t,x_{2})\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi_{2})\lambda_{0}-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\Gamma f)(t,x_{2})\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{-1}(\xi_{2})\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi_{2})\lambda_{0},

\displaystyle r_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\xi_{2})

\displaystyle\in S\left({\tfrac{\lambda_{0}}{1+\lambda_{0}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}}\gamma(\lambda_{0},\xi_{2})}\right).

Observe that both
p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\xi_{2})
and
s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\xi_{2})
are real-valued and even.

Proof.

We begin by computing

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\varphi-\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi+\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\perp}\Gamma\mathring{\theta}\cdot\nabla\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\varphi+\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{x_{2}}f(t,x_{2})\partial_{x_{1}}\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi-\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{x_{2}}\Gamma f(t,x_{2})\partial_{x_{1}}\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\varphi+\partial_{x_{2}}f\partial_{x_{1}}\Gamma\varphi-\partial_{x_{2}}\Gamma f\partial_{x_{1}}\varphi

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\partial_{x_{2}}f]\partial_{x_{1}}\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi-[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\partial_{x_{2}}\Gamma f]\partial_{x_{1}}\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi.

For the last line, we factor out
\partial_{x_{1}}
(whose symbol is
i\xi_{1}
) and use Lemma
3.1
to write

\displaystyle[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\partial_{x_{2}}f]\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}-\left(i^{-1}\{\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\partial_{x_{2}}f\}\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi)\right)(x,D)\in Op(S(\langle{\xi}\rangle^{-2}m)),

\displaystyle[\Gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\partial_{x_{2}}\Gamma f]\Gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\left(i^{-1}\{\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},\partial_{x_{2}}f\}\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\xi)\right)(x,D)\in Op(S(\langle{\xi}\rangle^{-2})).

Then using the identity

\{\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}},g\}=\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma(\xi)\partial_{x_{2}}g

which holds for any function
g=g(x_{2})
, it follows that

\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\varphi=\partial_{t}\varphi+ip_{\mathring{\theta}}(x_{2},D_{1},D_{2})\varphi+s_{\mathring{\theta}}(x_{2},D_{1},D_{2})\varphi+r_{\mathring{\theta}}(x_{2},D_{1},D_{2})\varphi,

(3.14)

where

\displaystyle p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=\partial_{x_{2}}f(t,x_{2})\xi_{1}\gamma(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}){-}(\partial_{x_{2}}\Gamma f)(t,x_{2})\xi_{1},

\displaystyle s_{\mathring{\theta}}(x_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2})

\displaystyle={+}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}f(t,x_{2})\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\xi_{1}-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\Gamma f)(t,x_{2})\gamma^{-1}(\xi)\partial_{\xi_{2}}\gamma(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\xi_{1},

\displaystyle r_{\mathring{\theta}}(x_{2},\xi_{1}\xi_{2})

\displaystyle\in S\left({\tfrac{\lambda_{0}}{1+\lambda_{0}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}}\gamma(\lambda_{0},\xi_{2})}\right).

Next, we conjugate (
3.14
) by
e^{i\lambda_{0}x_{1}}
. For any symbol
p
, note that the effect of conjugating by the linear phase
\lambda_{0}x_{1}
is simply the translation
\xi_{1}\mapsto\xi_{1}+\lambda_{0}
; i.e.,
{}^{(\lambda_{0}x_{1})}p(x_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=p(x_{2},\lambda_{0}+\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
, where the LHS is given in Lemma
3.2
. Moreover, it is clear from (
3.2
) that for any function
\psi
that is independent of
x_{1}
, we have
p(x_{2},\lambda_{0}+D_{1},D_{2})\psi=p(x_{2},\lambda_{0},D_{2})\psi
. Since
p_{\mathring{\theta}}(x_{2},\lambda_{0},\xi_{2})=p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\xi_{2})
,
s_{\mathring{\theta}}(x_{2},\lambda_{0},\xi_{2})=s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\xi_{2})
and
r_{\mathring{\theta}}(x_{2},\lambda_{0},\xi_{2})=r_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\xi_{2})
, the proof of Proposition
3.4
is complete. ∎

With Proposition
3.4
in hand, we are ready to specify the equations solved by the phase function
\mathbf{\Phi}=\lambda_{0}x_{1}+\Phi(t,x_{2})
and the amplitude function
a=a(t,x_{2})
, which would constitute the desired degenerating wave packet
\widetilde{\varphi}=\Re(e^{i\mathbf{\Phi}}a)=\Re(e^{i(\lambda_{0}x_{1}+\Phi)}a)
. The scheme itself is simply the standard WKB procedure for the nonlocal operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}
, but there are extra twists that arise in our scenario due to the degeneracy of
\mathring{\theta}
; see Remark
3.5
below.

Equation for the phase
\Phi
.
We choose the phase function
\Phi=\Phi(t,x_{2})
to be a solution to the Hamilton–Jacobi equation

\partial_{t}\Phi+p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(t,x_{2},\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi)=0,

(3.15)

with initial data satisfying
|{\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi(0,x_{2})}|\simeq\lambda_{0}
(the precise choice of the initial data has to be well-adapted to our problem; see Section
4
below).
We note that the
(X,\Xi)
-components of the characteristics for (
3.15
) solve the Hamiltonian ODE associated with
p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(t,x_{2},\xi_{2})
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{X}_{2}(t)&=\partial_{\xi_{2}}p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(t,X_{2}(t),\Xi_{2}(t)),\\
\dot{\Xi}_{2}(t)&=-\partial_{x_{2}}p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(t,X_{2}(t),\Xi_{2}(t)).\end{aligned}\right.

Equation for the amplitude
a
.
We choose the amplitude function
a=a(t,x_{2})
to obey the transport equation

\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}a:=\partial_{t}a+\partial_{\xi_{2}}p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi)\partial_{x_{2}}a+\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\xi_{2}}^{2}p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi)\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\Phi+s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi)\right)a=0,

(3.16)

with smooth and compactly supported initial data. At this point, we note that if one takes the inner product of (
3.16
) with
a
and integrate by parts, then the specific structure of
s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}
will show that the
L^{2}
norm of
a
stays bounded on an
O(1)
timescale. This is proved in Proposition
4.12
below.

Key computation for the error term.
The reason for the choices of the equations (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) is the following computation:

\partial_{t}(ae^{i\Phi})+ip_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(ae^{i\Phi})+s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(ae^{i\Phi})={}^{(\Phi)}r_{p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}},-2}+{}^{(\Phi)}r_{s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}},-1}.

(3.17)

Indeed, by the equations for
\partial_{t}\Phi
and
\partial_{t}a
:

\begin{split}\partial_{t}(ae^{i\Phi})+ip_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(ae^{i\Phi})+s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(ae^{i\Phi})&=i\partial_{t}\Phi ae^{i\Phi}+(\partial_{t}a)e^{i\Phi}+ip_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(ae^{i\Phi})+s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(ae^{i\Phi})\\
&=ip_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(ae^{i\Phi})-ip_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)ae^{i\Phi}-\partial_{\xi_{2}}p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi)\partial_{x_{2}}ae^{i\Phi}\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\xi_{2}}^{2}p_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x_{2},\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi)\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\Phi\,ae^{i\Phi}\\
&\quad+s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(ae^{i\Phi})-s_{\mathring{\theta},\lambda_{0}}(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)ae^{i\Phi}.\end{split}

Then, (
3.17
) follows from Proposition
3.3
.

In conclusion, to justify that
\widetilde{\varphi}=\Re(ae^{i(\lambda_{0}x_{1}+\Phi)})
is a good approximate solution, we need to use (
3.13
) and (
3.17
)
to show that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}\widetilde{\varphi}=\Re(\mathcal{L}_{\mathring{\theta}}(ae^{i(\lambda_{0}x_{1}+\Phi)}))
is suitably small. As remarked at the beginning of this section, this goal shall be achieved in Section
6
.

Remark 3.5
.

In our case, due to the degeneracy of
p_{\theta}
, we expect
\partial_{x_{2}}^{1+k}\Phi
and
\partial_{x_{2}}^{k}a
to
grow
rapidly in time for
k\geq 1
(and indeed, this is precisely the behavior we wish to capture in order to show illposedness). For this reason, we cannot simply rely on standard pseudo-differential calculus to construct a wave packet that is valid up to a timescale where such a growth of higher derivatives (i.e.,
degeneration
) occurs. Nevertheless, as we shall see in Section
4
, the growth rate of
\partial_{x_{2}}^{k}\Phi
and
\partial_{x_{2}}^{k}a
will be smaller than
|{\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi}|^{k}
, which we may salvage via Proposition
5.1
in Section
5
.

4
The Hamilton–Jacobi equation and the associated transport operator

This section is devoted to the choice and analysis of the phase and amplitude functions. The main results of this section are Proposition
4.10
and
4.12
, which provide sharp estimates on the derivatives of the phase and amplitude, respectively.

Let us outline the structure of this section. After detailing the choice of parameters in Section
4.1
, we consider the case of time-independent background state in Section
4.2
. In this case, we take our phase function to be a solution of the Hamilton–Jacobi equation in separation of variables form. Thanks to the rather explicit form of the phase function, it is straightforward to obtain pointwise estimates for the derivatives of the phase function (Proposition
4.5
), and the resulting bounds serve as a guide for the time-dependent background case which is handled in Section
4.3
. In this latter case, we use the same initial data for the phase function, and estimate the solution by the method of characteristics and a bootstrapping argument. The key variable
h
is introduced in this section in (
4.33
), which encodes some cancellations in the Hamilton–Jacobi equation and allows us to obtain sharp estimates on the second derivative of the phase function (Lemma
4.8
and Proposition
4.9
). Using the variable
h
, we prove Proposition
4.10
in Section
4.4
, which gives the desired pointwise bounds on the derivatives of the phase function. Then, it is not very difficult to obtain estimates on the amplitude function, which is Proposition
4.12
of Section
4.5
.

4.1
Initial reductions and conventions

Throughout this section, we study the Hamilton–Jacobi equation (
3.15
) with the following conventions:

•

Assume that
\mathring{x}_{2}=0
and
\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}f(0,0)<0
.

•

Write
x=x_{2}
and
\xi=\xi_{2}
. Moreover, a prime (
′
) denotes
\partial_{x_{2}}
.

•

Introduce the shorthand
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)=\gamma(\lambda_{0},\xi)
.

With the above reductions, the Hamilton–Jacobi equation is simply given by

\partial_{t}\Phi+p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)=0\qquad\hbox{ in }\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{x},

(4.1)

where

p(t,x,\xi)=f^{\prime}(t,x)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)+\Gamma f^{\prime}(t,x)\lambda_{0}.

(4.2)

The key point of our analysis is that
f^{\prime}(t,x)
is assumed to vanish linearly at some point for each
t
. As a result, there exist bicharacteristics
(X(t),\Xi(t))
associated with
p(t,x,\xi)
that exhibits rapid growth of
|{\Xi}|
. Our aim is to construct a solution
\Phi
to (
4.1
), which we refer to as a
phase function
, that exhibits such a behavior for a sufficiently long time.

Parameters in the construction.

In this section, we are given a symbol
\gamma
and a function
f
that satisfy the assumptions laid out in Section
1.5
. We are also given
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
M\geq 1
,
\delta_{0}>0
and
\sigma_{0}\geq 0
that satisfy (
1.18
)–(
1.20
)
6
6

6

To be absolutely precise, instead of (
1.19
) we only need
\tau_{M}\leq 1
in this section. The full condition is needed in Section
6
below.
. When
f
is time-dependent, we assume furthermore that
f
is even and that we are given
\delta_{1}>0
such that (
1.25
) holds.

We
fix a nonincreasing function
\epsilon(\lambda_{0})
for
\lambda_{0}\geq\Xi_{0}
such that, for
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda
,

\max\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}^{\sigma_{0}+\frac{1}{6}\delta_{0}}},\,\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})^{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{0}}}\right\}\leq\epsilon(\lambda_{0})\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{100},\frac{1}{(10+2^{2\beta_{0}})\sup_{M^{\prime}\in[1,M]}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(M^{\prime}\lambda_{0})}{M^{\prime}}\tau_{M^{\prime}}}\right\}.

(4.3)

By the second nondissipative growth condition (
1.18
), we see that there always exists such a function, i.e., if
\epsilon(\lambda_{0})
satisfies

\max\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}^{\sigma_{0}+\frac{1}{6}\delta_{0}}},\,\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})^{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{0}}}\right\}\leq\epsilon(\lambda_{0})\leq\frac{1}{2^{\beta_{0}+1}(2^{\beta_{0}}+3)\min\{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})^{1-\delta_{0}},\lambda_{0}^{\sigma_{0}}\}},

for
\lambda_{0}\geq\Xi_{0}
, then it would obey (
4.3
).

The primary role of
\epsilon(\lambda_{0})
is as the relative physical localization scale of the wave packet: the support of
a(t=0,x)
will be contained in an interval of size
\Delta x_{0}
that is comparable to
x_{0}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})
up to a logarithmic power of
\lambda_{0}
, where
x_{0}
is the initial location of the wave packet. For technical simplicity, we shall also choose (see (
4.11
) and (
4.27
) below)

x_{0}=c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0}),

where
we shall retain the freedom to choose
c_{x_{0}}>0
throughout this section
(it will only be fixed in Section
6.1
).

Remark 4.1
.

The restriction
\sigma_{0}\leq\frac{1}{3}(1-2\delta_{0})
in Theorems
A
and
B
arise from the uncertainty principle at
t=0
(which requires
\Delta x_{0}\lambda_{0}\gtrsim 1
) and the wave packet error estimate; see Proposition
6.3
below. We note that the requirement
x_{0}\simeq\epsilon(\lambda_{0})
can be weakened if
f^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,0)=0
, in which case the restriction
\sigma_{0}\leq\frac{1}{3}(1-2\delta_{0})
can be relaxed; see Remark
4.2
below.

In addition to
\delta_{0}
and
\delta_{1}
, we are given
\delta_{2}
and
N_{0}^{-1}
, which will be chosen in Sections
6
and
7
(in fact,
\delta_{2}=\delta_{0}^{10}
and
N_{0}=10^{4}\max\{\delta_{2}^{-1}(1+\beta_{0}),1+\alpha_{0},{1+s,1+s^{\prime}}\}
; see Proposition
6.3
and Section
7.2
below).
In the course of this section,
we will choose additional small parameters in the following order
(each choice may also depend on
\gamma
and
f
):

\delta_{0},\delta_{1},\delta_{2},N_{0}^{-1}\to\delta_{3}\to\delta_{4}\to\delta_{5}.

In this section,
we also require that

\tau_{M}\leq T,\qquad\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda

where
we shall retain the freedom to choose
0<T\leq 1
and
\Lambda\geq 1
throughout this section
(
T
may be fixed at the end of this section, while
\Lambda
will be fixed in Section
6.2
).

4.2
The case of a shear steady state background

Choice of
\Phi
.
When
\mathring{\theta}=f(x_{2})
is time-independent, i.e.,
\partial_{t}f^{\prime}=0
, we may obtain a useful solution to the Hamilton–Jacobi equation by separation of variables: Consider the ansatz

\Phi(t,x)=E\lambda_{0}t+G(x).

Then
G(x)
obeys the equation

p(x,\partial_{x}G)=-E\lambda_{0}.

(4.4)

By the positivity of
\xi\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
,
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\cdot)
is invertible on
(-\infty,-\lambda_{0}]
or
[\lambda_{0},\infty)
. We are going to take
G(x)
whose derivative is blowing up at the origin, and for
\partial_{x}\Phi=\partial_{x}G
to grow (as
t
increases) on characteristic curves, we need to choose
\partial_{x}G
to be positive (indeed, this sign relation may be read off from the bicharacteristic equations). In what follows, we denote by
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{-1}
the inverse of the restriction
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}:[\lambda_{0},\infty)\to(0,\infty)
. Hence, we obtain the explicit formula

\partial_{x}\Phi=\partial_{x}G:=\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{-1}\left(\frac{E+\Gamma f^{\prime}}{-f^{\prime}}\right).

(4.5)

Note that
\partial_{x}G\to\infty
at the origin.

To begin with, we proceed to show that there is a choice of
E
in (
4.5
) so that

\lambda_{0}\leq\partial_{x}\Phi(0,x)\leq(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}

(4.6)

holds for
x_{0}<x<x_{1}
and
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda
, provided that
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
are sufficiently small (with respect to
f^{\prime}
,
\lambda_{0}
, and
\epsilon
) and
\Lambda
is sufficiently large (depending on
\gamma
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|^{-1}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
). Since
\gamma^{-1}_{\lambda_{0}}
is increasing, (
4.6
) is equivalent to having

\begin{split}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})\leq\frac{E+\Gamma f^{\prime}(x)}{-f^{\prime}(x)}\leq\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\epsilon(\lambda_{0}))\lambda_{0}),\qquad x_{0}<x<x_{1}.\end{split}

(4.7)

We first take
x_{1}>0
to be sufficiently small (recall that
f^{\prime\prime}(0)<0
), so that

(1+\tfrac{\epsilon}{10})f^{\prime\prime}(0)<f^{\prime\prime}(x)<(1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{10})f^{\prime\prime}(0)\qquad\hbox{ for $x\in(0,x_{1})$}.

(4.8)

In particular, we have
(1+\tfrac{\epsilon}{10})f^{\prime\prime}(0)x<f^{\prime}(x)<(1-\tfrac{\epsilon}{10})f^{\prime\prime}(0)x
for
x\in(0,x_{1})
. Then, we define

\begin{split}E=-f^{\prime}(x_{1})\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})-\Gamma f^{\prime}(x_{1}).\end{split}

With this choice of
E
, (
4.7
) is trivially satisfied at
x=x_{1}
. We may now choose
x_{0}<x_{1}
in a way that (
4.7
) is satisfied for all
x\in(x_{0},x_{1})
: we take

\begin{split}\frac{x_{1}}{x_{0}}-1=\frac{1}{10}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})-\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}.\end{split}

(4.9)

With these choices, we estimate

\begin{split}\frac{f^{\prime}(x_{1})}{f^{\prime}(x_{0})}=1+\frac{\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{1}}f^{\prime\prime}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{0}^{x_{0}}f^{\prime\prime}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x}\leq 1+10\left|1-\frac{x_{1}}{x_{0}}\right|=\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}.\end{split}

where we take
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda
larger if necessary to decrease
\epsilon
. Then,

\begin{split}\frac{E+\Gamma f^{\prime}(x)}{-f^{\prime}(x)}&=\frac{-f^{\prime}(x_{1})\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})-\Gamma f^{\prime}(x_{1})+\Gamma f^{\prime}(x)}{-f^{\prime}(x)}\\
&\leq\frac{f^{\prime}(x_{1})}{f^{\prime}(x_{0})}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})+\frac{2}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}\|{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}(x_{0},x_{1})}\frac{|x_{0}-x_{1}|}{x_{0}}\\
&\leq\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})+\frac{20\|{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}(x_{0},x_{1})}}{|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})-\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}.\end{split}

To estimate the last line by
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0})
, it suffices to verify

\begin{split}\frac{20\|{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}(x_{0},x_{1})}}{|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})-\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}\leq\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0})-\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0}).\end{split}

(4.10)

From our assumptions on
\gamma
, this is guaranteed for
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda_{0}
with sufficiently large
\Lambda_{0}
. Indeed, by the mean value theorem, there exist
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in[0,1]
and
\alpha_{3}\in[\tfrac{2+\alpha_{1}}{4},\tfrac{3+\alpha_{2}}{4}]
such that

\begin{split}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})-\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0})-\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}&=1+\frac{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{2+\alpha_{1}}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})-\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3+\alpha_{2}}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3+\alpha_{2}}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}\\
&\leq 1+\epsilon\lambda_{0}\frac{|{\partial_{\xi}^{2}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\alpha_{3}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}|}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3+\alpha_{2}}{4}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})}\leq C,\end{split}

where we have used the ellipticity of
\xi\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
for the last inequality. Since
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}\to\infty
as
\lambda_{0}\to\infty
, the desired bound (
4.10
) follows by taking
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda
large enough. We have verified the right inequality of (
4.7
). The proof of the left inequality is only easier since
-f^{\prime}(x)
is increasing in
(x_{0},x_{1})
. We hence omit the proof.

We have shown that if
x_{1}
and
x_{0}
are chosen so that (
4.8
) and (
4.9
) and
\Lambda
is sufficiently large depending on
\gamma
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|^{-1}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
, then
E
can be chosen so that (
4.6
) holds. In what follows,
we choose
x_{0}
,
x_{1}
to be of the form

\begin{split}x_{0}={c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})},\quad{x_{1}=x_{0}+\Delta x_{0}},\quad\hbox{ where }{\frac{cc_{x_{0}}}{(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})}\leq\left|\frac{\Delta x_{0}}{x_{0}}\right|\leq c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0}),\end{split}

(4.11)

where
c
is a constant depending only on
\gamma
and
f
, and
c_{x_{1}}>0
obeys

\|{f^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,c_{x_{1}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0}))}c_{x_{0}}\leq\frac{1}{20}|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|.

(4.12)

Clearly, such a choice of
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
ensures (
4.8
). In the following, there are several occasions in which we need to take
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
sufficiently small. To achieve this,
we shall retain the freedom to shrink
c_{x_{0}}>0
until the end of this section
; note that this action only makes the LHS of (
4.12
) smaller.
The second inequality in (
4.11
) follows from (
4.9
), mean value theorem, and the almost comparability of
\xi\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
and
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
.

Remark 4.2
.

If
f^{\prime\prime\prime}
vanishes at
0
, then we may choose
x_{0}
be be larger. More precisely,

\begin{split}x_{0}={c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{\frac{1}{n_{f}}}}\end{split}

(4.13)

would work, where
n_{f}
is the smallest natural number such that
f^{(n_{f}+2)}(0)\neq 0
.

Bicharacteristics associated with
\Phi
. We consider the bicharacteristic ODE

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{X}&=f^{\prime}(X)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi),\\
\dot{\Xi}&=-f^{\prime\prime}(X)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)+(\Gamma f^{\prime\prime})(X)\lambda_{0}\end{aligned}\right.

(4.14)

with initial data
(X_{0},\Xi_{0})
satisfying
x_{0}<X_{0}<x_{1}
and
\lambda_{0}\leq\Xi_{0}=\partial\Phi(0,X_{0})\leq(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}
.

Control of
\Xi(t)
.
To obtain bounds on
\Xi(t)
, we introduce
\overline{\lambda}(t)
and
\lambda(t)
, which are solutions to the ODEs

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{\overline{\lambda}}(t)&=-(1+\epsilon)f^{\prime\prime}(0)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\overline{\lambda}),\\
\overline{\lambda}(0)&={(1+\epsilon)}\lambda_{0},\end{aligned}\right.

(4.15)

and

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{\lambda}(t)&=-(1-\epsilon)f^{\prime\prime}(0)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda),\\
\lambda(0)&={\lambda_{0}},\end{aligned}\right.

(4.16)

respectively.
We remind the reader that
\epsilon=\epsilon(\lambda_{0})
obeys (
4.3
). We now claim that

\lambda(t)\leq\Xi(t)\leq\overline{\lambda}(t).

(4.17)

To begin with, observe that
\dot{X}=0
when
X=0
, from which we see that no solution
(X(t),\Xi(t))
with
X(0)>0
can traverse to the region
\{X<0\}
. Note furthermore that in the region
\{X>0\}
, we have
\dot{X}<0
. In conclusion, we see that
0<X(t)<X(0)<x_{1}
for any
t>0
.

Next, note that

\begin{split}-(1-\epsilon)f^{\prime\prime}(0)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)\leq-f^{\prime\prime}(X)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)+(\Gamma f^{\prime\prime})(X)\lambda_{0}\leq-(1+\epsilon)f^{\prime\prime}(0)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\overline{\lambda})\end{split}

for any
X\in(0,x_{1})
and
\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda\leq\Xi\leq\overline{\lambda}
.
Indeed, by the first inequality of (
4.3
), we have
\epsilon\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)\to\infty
as
\lambda_{0}\to\infty
. By taking
\Lambda_{0}>0
larger if necessary (in a way depending only on
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|^{-1}\|{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,x_{1})}
,
\gamma
and
\epsilon
), we may guarantee that the preceding inequalities hold. Then, comparing the equations for
\dot{\Xi}
,
\dot{\lambda}
and
\dot{\overline{\lambda}}
, we obtain (
4.17
), as desired.

We now prove the following lemma.

Lemma 4.3
.

The following statements hold.

1.

\lambda(t)=M\lambda_{0}
exactly at
t_{M}:=\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|^{-1}\tau_{M}
, and
\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda(t)\leq M\lambda_{0}
for
0\leq t\leq t_{M}
.

2.

As long as
\lambda(t)\leq M\lambda_{0}
, we have

\lambda(t)\leq\Xi(t)\leq 2\lambda(t).

(4.18)

Proof.

It will be convenient to introduce
\overline{M}^{\prime}(t)
and
M^{\prime}(t)
:
\overline{M}^{\prime}:=\overline{\lambda}/\lambda_{0}
and
M^{\prime}:=\lambda/\lambda_{0}
. We have
\overline{M}^{\prime}(t)\geq M^{\prime}(t)\geq 1
. we start with the identities

\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}=-f^{\prime\prime}(0)(1-\epsilon)t,\qquad\int_{(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}}^{\overline{M}^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}=-f^{\prime\prime}(0)(1+\epsilon)t,

(4.19)

which follow from (
4.16
) and (
4.15
), respectively. Note that the LHS of the first identity is exactly
\tau_{M^{\prime}}
; from this observation, the first statement easily follows. To prove (
4.18
), noting that
M^{\prime}
is strictly increasing in time, we shall split the proof into two time intervals, depending on whether
M^{\prime}<2
or
M^{\prime}\geq 2
.

Case 1
. Assume that
M^{\prime}(t)<2
. We are going to prove that
\overline{M}^{\prime}(t)<2M^{\prime}(t)
holds in this time interval by a bootstrap argument. Towards a contradiction, we may assume that there exists some
0<T<t_{M}
, such that
M^{\prime}(t)<2,\overline{M}^{\prime}(t)<2M^{\prime}(t)
on
[0,T)
and
\overline{M}^{\prime}(T)=2M^{\prime}(T)
.

We now restrict
t
to
[0,T]
. Since
\lambda
and
\overline{\lambda}
are strictly increasing functions of time, we may consider
\overline{\lambda}=\overline{\lambda}(\lambda)
(with some abuse of notation) and obtain

\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}\overline{\lambda}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}=\frac{1+\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\frac{\gamma(\lambda\cdot(\overline{\lambda}/\lambda))}{\gamma(\lambda)}\leq(1+3\epsilon)\left(\frac{\overline{\lambda}}{\lambda}\right)^{C}\end{split}

where we may assume that
C>1
since
2\lambda\geq\overline{\lambda}\geq\lambda
. Integrating in
\lambda
and recalling that
\overline{\lambda}(\lambda_{0})=(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}
, we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\overline{\lambda}^{C-1}}-\frac{1}{((1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0})^{C-1}}\geq(1+3\epsilon)\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{C-1}}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}^{C-1}}\right).\end{split}

Applying this inequality at
t=T
, we have that
\overline{\lambda}=2\lambda
and obtain

\begin{split}\left((1+3\epsilon)-\frac{1}{2^{C-1}}\right)\frac{1}{\lambda^{C-1}}\leq\left((1+3\epsilon)-\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon)^{C-1}}\right)\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}^{C-1}}=O_{C}(\epsilon)\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}^{C-1}}.\end{split}

and we can take
\Lambda_{0}
larger so that
(1+3\epsilon)>\frac{1}{2^{C-1}}
. This gives
(\lambda/\lambda_{0})^{C-1}\gg 1
, which is a contradiction to
M^{\prime}(T)<2
. This guarantees (
4.18
) in this case.

Case 2
. When
M^{\prime}(t)\geq 2
, we now combine both identities in (
4.19
) to obtain

\begin{split}\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}+\frac{2\epsilon}{1+\epsilon}\int_{(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}}^{M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}=\frac{1-\epsilon}{1+\epsilon}\int_{M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}^{\overline{M}^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}.\end{split}

Note that the LHS is bounded by

\begin{split}&\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}+\frac{2\epsilon}{1+\epsilon}\int_{(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}}^{M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}\\
&\leq\frac{\epsilon}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}+2\epsilon\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}\\
&\leq(2^{\beta_{0}}+1)\epsilon\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{2\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}+2\epsilon\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}\\
&\leq(2^{\beta_{0}}+3)\epsilon\tau_{M^{\prime}},\end{split}

where we used the quantitative slow-variance condition (
1.11
) for the second inequality.
By the second inequality in (
4.3
) and the assumption that
M^{\prime}\leq M
, we have

\begin{split}2^{\beta_{0}+1}(2^{\beta_{0}}+3)\epsilon\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(M^{\prime}\lambda_{0})}{M^{\prime}}\tau_{M^{\prime}}\leq 1.\end{split}

As a result,

\begin{split}\int_{M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}^{\overline{M}^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}\leq 2(2^{\beta_{0}}+3)\epsilon\tau_{M^{\prime}}\leq 2^{-\beta_{0}}\frac{M^{\prime}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(M^{\prime}\lambda_{0})}\leq\int_{M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}^{2M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}},\end{split}

where we used (
1.11
) for the last inequality. This implies
\overline{M}^{\prime}\leq 2M^{\prime}
. Then, by (
4.17
), (
4.18
) follows. ∎

Control of
X(t)
.
With Lemma
4.3
for
\Xi(t)
, we may obtain bounds on
X(t)
using the conservation of the Hamiltonian:

p(X(t),\partial_{x}\Phi(X(t)))=p(X(0),\partial_{x}\Phi(X(0))).

This property holds since
(X(t),\partial_{x}\Phi(X(t)))
solves the bicharacteristic ODEs (
4.14
) (by the characteristic method, since
\Phi
is a regular solution to the scalar first-order PDE (
4.1
)), which is a Hamiltonian ODE with a time-independent Hamiltonian
p(x,\xi)=f^{\prime}(x)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)+\Gamma f^{\prime}(x)\lambda_{0}
.
More specifically, we have

f^{\prime}(X(t))=\frac{f^{\prime}(X(0))\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(0))+\Gamma f^{\prime}(X(0))-\Gamma f^{\prime}(X(t))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}.

Recall that
0<X(t)<X(0)
. Therefore, we have

|{\Gamma f^{\prime}(X(0))-\Gamma f^{\prime}(X(t))}|\leq\|{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,x_{1})}|{X(0)-X(t)}|\leq\|{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,x_{1})}X(0).

On the other hand,
|{f^{\prime}(X(0))}|\geq(1-\frac{\epsilon}{10})|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|X(0)
. Therefore, choosing
\Lambda
sufficiently large depending on
\gamma
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|^{-1}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
, we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2^{\beta_{0}+1}}\frac{X(0)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))}\leq X(t)\leq\frac{X(0)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))}\quad\hbox{ for }\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda.\end{split}

(4.20)

Control of
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(X(t))
. As we will see, a fundamental quantity for controlling the geometry of nearby characteristic curves is
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi
. While this quantity may also be computed explicitly by differentiating (
4.5
), it is more convenient to obtain an implicit formula by differentiating (
4.1
). Indeed, from (
4.1
) we see that

\partial_{x}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)+\partial_{\xi}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi=0,

so that

\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi=-\frac{\partial_{x}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)}{\partial_{\xi}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)}=-\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)+\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}.

(4.21)

By taking
\Lambda_{0}
larger if necessary (in a way depending only on
\gamma
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}|^{-1}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
), we obtain the bound

0\leq-\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(X(t))\leq\frac{C}{x}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi(X(t)))}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi(X(t)))}\leq\frac{C}{x_{0}}\frac{(\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t)))^{2}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))}.

Remark 4.4
.

It is worth noting that
-\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(X(t))
remains finite as long as
(X(t),\Xi(t))
