exists. Geometrically, this reflects the fact that there are no focal points along each characteristic associated to our
\Phi
constructed via separation of variables. This fact is not a-priori clear at the level of the ODE for
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(X(t))
, which is of Ricatti-type (see (
4.32
) below); hence, it should be seen as a benefit of our separation of variables approach. The above sharp bound for
-\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(X(t))
will serve as the basis for the sharp estimates for higher derivatives of
\Phi
and the amplitude function
a
(see (
3.16
)) along characteristics in Sections
4.4
–
4.5
.

For the ensuing argument, we record separately the bound at the initial time
t=0
:

0\leq-\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(x)\leq\frac{C}{x}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi(x))}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi(x))}\leq\frac{C}{x_{0}}\lambda_{0}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}.

Higher derivatives of
\Phi
in
(x_{0},x_{1})
.
We now consider higher derivatives of
\Phi
in
(x_{0},x_{1})
. For any
k\geq 2
, we claim that

\begin{split}|\partial_{x}^{k}\Phi(x)|\leq C_{k}x_{0}^{-k+1}\lambda_{0}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2(k-1)},\quad\hbox{ for }x_{0}<x<x_{1}.\end{split}

(4.22)

Note that this corresponds to the bound for
\partial_{x}^{k}\Phi(X(0))
at the initial time. We will obtain bounds for
\partial_{x}^{k}\Phi(X(t))
with
t>0
later in Section
4.4
, based on the bounds for
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(X(t))
and (
4.22
).

We shall prove (
4.22
) with an induction in
k
; assuming it holds for
k=2,\cdots,k_{0}+1
for some
k_{0}
, we compute

\begin{split}\partial_{x}^{k_{0}+2}\Phi&=\partial_{x}^{k_{0}}\left(-\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(x)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)+\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}\right)\\
&=-\sum_{\ell=0}^{k_{0}}C_{\ell,k_{0}}\partial_{x}^{\ell}\frac{1}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}\left(\partial_{x}^{k_{0}-\ell}\left(\frac{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime}}\right)+\sum_{j=0}^{k_{0}-\ell}C_{j,k_{0}-\ell}\partial_{x}^{k_{0}-\ell-j}\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime}}\right)\partial_{x}^{j}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)\right),\end{split}

where
C_{\ell,k_{0}}
and
C_{j,k_{0}-\ell}
are combinatorial coefficients. To begin with, it is not difficult to see that

\begin{split}\partial_{x}^{k_{0}-\ell}\left(\frac{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime}}\right)\leq C_{k_{0}}x_{0}^{-k_{0}+\ell-1},\qquad\partial_{x}^{k_{0}-\ell-j}\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime}}\right)\leq C_{k_{0}}x_{0}^{-k_{0}+\ell+j-1}.\end{split}

Next, we may expand
\partial_{x}^{j}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)
using Faà di Bruno’s formula:

\begin{split}\partial_{x}^{j}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}:a_{1}+2a_{2}+\cdots+ja_{j}=j}\frac{j!}{a_{1}!1!^{a_{1}}\cdots a_{j}!j!^{a_{j}}}(\partial^{a_{1}\cdots+a_{j}}_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}})(\partial_{x}\Phi)\prod_{b=1}^{j}(\partial_{x}^{1+b}\Phi)^{a_{b}}.\end{split}

In the above, the summation is over
j
-tuples
\mathfrak{a}=(a_{1},\cdots,a_{j})
with non-negative integer entries satisfying
a_{1}+2a_{2}+\cdots+ja_{j}=j
. Using the induction assumption (
4.22
) and the ellipticity assumption for
\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
, we obtain that

\begin{split}|\partial_{x}^{j}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)|&\leq x_{0}^{-j}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2j}\sum_{\mathfrak{a}}C_{\mathfrak{a}}\lambda_{0}^{a_{1}\cdots+a_{j}}(\partial^{a_{1}\cdots+a_{j}}_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}})(\partial_{x}\Phi)\\
&\leq C_{j}x_{0}^{-j}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2j}\lambda_{0}\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi).\end{split}

Similarly, we expand

\begin{split}\partial_{x}^{\ell}\frac{1}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}:a_{1}+2a_{2}+\cdots+\ell a_{\ell}=\ell}\frac{\ell!}{a_{1}!1!^{a_{1}}\cdots a_{\ell}!\ell!^{a_{\ell}}}(\partial^{a_{1}\cdots+a_{\ell}}_{\xi}\frac{1}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}})(\partial_{x}\Phi)\prod_{b=1}^{\ell}(\partial_{x}^{1+b}\Phi)^{a_{b}}.\end{split}

Again, using the ellipticity assumption on
\xi\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
, we see that

\begin{split}\left|(\partial^{n}_{\xi}\frac{1}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}})(\partial_{x}\Phi)\right|\leq C_{n}\lambda_{0}^{-n}\frac{1}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}}(\partial_{x}\Phi).\end{split}

This gives

\begin{split}\left|\partial_{x}^{\ell}\frac{1}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}\right|\leq C_{\ell}x_{0}^{-\ell}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2\ell}\frac{1}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}}.\end{split}

Collecting the bounds, we conclude that

\begin{split}\left|\partial_{x}^{k_{0}+2}\Phi\right|\leq C_{k_{0}}x_{0}^{-k_{0}-1}\lambda_{0}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2(k_{0}+1)}\end{split}

holds, which is just (
4.22
) with
k=k_{0}+2
. Therefore, we have arrived at the following

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\partial_{x}\Phi
be defined as in (
4.5
). Then for any
k\geq 1
,
\partial_{x}^{k}\partial_{x}\Phi
satisfy

\begin{split}{|{\partial_{x}^{k}\partial_{x}\Phi(x)}|\leq C_{k}x_{0}^{-k}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2k}\lambda_{0}\qquad\hbox{ for }x_{0}<x<x_{1}.}\end{split}

(4.23)

4.3
The case of a time-dependent background

We now generalize our construction of the phase function to the case when
f^{\prime}
has time dependence. This time, we employ the method of characteristics to analyze (
4.1
). The explicit computation we performed in the time-independent case in Section
4.2
will serve as a very useful guide.

Remark 4.6
.

A small modification of our scheme can handle
f^{\prime}
with a moving zero, by working with the new variable
\tilde{x}=x-\mathring{x}(t)
to fix the position of the zero. Then we need to add a term of the form
\mathring{x}^{\prime}(t)\partial_{x}\Phi
in the Hamiltonian, which may be handled perturbatively (dominated by the first term in
p
) in the regime
M\ll\gamma(\lambda_{0})
.

In this subsection,
we assume that (
1.18
)–(
1.20
), as well as (
1.24
)–(
1.25
)
(i.e., the hypotheses for Theorem
B
)
hold
.

Choice of
\Phi(0,x)
.

Let
\Lambda
be a large positive parameter to be fixed below.
Introduce
T
so that

0<T\leq 1,\qquad\|{\partial_{t}f^{\prime\prime}(t^{\prime},0)}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t^{\prime}}(0,\frac{100}{99}t_{f}(T))}t_{f}(T)\leq\frac{1}{10}|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|,

(4.24)

where we remind the reader that
t_{f}(\tau)
is defined by
\tau=\int_{0}^{t_{f}(\tau)}-f^{\prime\prime}(t^{\prime},0)\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}
. We then introduce
c_{x_{0}}>0
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,\frac{100}{99}t_{f}(T)]}\|{f^{\prime\prime\prime}(t,x)}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0}))}c_{x_{1}}\leq\frac{1}{10}|{f^{\prime\prime}(t,0)}|,

(4.25)

where
\epsilon(\lambda_{0})
is an nonincreasing function of
\lambda_{0}\in[\Xi_{0},\infty)
obeying (
4.3
) for
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda
. We emphasize that
we retain the freedom to shrink
T,c_{x_{1}}>0
and enlarge
\Lambda
until the end of this section.

We look for the solution
\Phi
to (
4.1
) with the initial data

\partial_{x}\Phi(0,x)=\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{-1}\left(\frac{f^{\prime}(0,x_{1})\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon)\lambda_{0})+\Gamma f^{\prime}(0,x)-\Gamma f^{\prime}(0,x_{1})}{-f^{\prime}(0,x)}\right)

(4.26)

on
\{t=0\}\times(x_{0},x_{1})
, where

{x_{0}=c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0}),\quad x_{1}=x_{0}+\Delta x_{0},\quad\frac{\Delta x_{0}}{x_{0}}=\frac{1}{10}\left(\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{3}{4}\epsilon(\lambda_{0}))\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}((1+\tfrac{1}{2}\epsilon(\lambda_{0}))\lambda_{0})}-1\right),}

(4.27)

and
c_{x_{0}}
obeys (
4.25
). Note that (
4.26
)–(
4.27
) are precisely (
4.5
), (
4.11
) with
f^{\prime}
frozen at
t=0
. The relevance of this choice will be evident in the estimate for
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi
below. As before, observe that for
\Lambda
sufficiently large,

\lambda_{0}\leq\partial_{x}\Phi(0,x)\leq(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}\quad\hbox{ on }\{t=0\}\times(x_{0},x_{1})\hbox{ for }\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda.

Setup for the method of characteristics.

We now set up the method of characteristics for (
4.1
). Let
X(t)
be a characteristic curve parametrized by
t
, and introduce
\Xi(t)=\partial_{x}\Phi(t,X(t))
. The bicharacteristic ODEs for
(X,\Xi)
read as follows:

\displaystyle\dot{X}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\xi}p(t,X,\Xi)=f^{\prime}(t,X)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi),

(4.28)

\displaystyle\dot{\Xi}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{x}p(t,X,\Xi)=-f^{\prime\prime}(t,X)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)+(\Gamma f^{\prime\prime})(t,X)\lambda_{0}.

(4.29)

Observe that these equations constitute the Hamiltonian ODEs corresponding to the time-dependent Hamiltonian
p(t,x,\xi)
. In the following, it will be always assumed that the initial data satisfy
x_{0}<X(0)<x_{1}
and
\Lambda_{0}\leq\lambda_{0}\leq\Xi(0)\leq(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}
.

Continuation criterion and set up for the bootstrap argument.

Before we continue, we briefly discuss how our bootstrap argument for constructing and estimating
\Phi
is set up. Given
\Phi
, denote by
X(t;\underline{x})
the solution to
\dot{X}=\partial_{\xi}p(t,X,\partial_{x}\Phi(t,X))
. Observe that the method of characteristics guarantees the existence of
\Phi(t,x)
for
X(t;x_{0})<x<X(t;x_{1})
, initially for some short time interval
[0,t^{\ast})
. In what follows, we will prove
\|{\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,x)}\|_{L^{\infty}(X(t;(x_{0},x_{1})))}
is uniformly bounded for
t<t^{\ast}
, provided that
t^{\ast}<\frac{100}{99}t_{f}(\tau_{M})
(the time scale in Theorem
B
). Then
\underline{x}\mapsto X(t^{\ast};\underline{x})
is a bi-Lipschitz isomorphism, and
X(t),\Xi(t)
and
\Phi(t,X(t))
exist on a longer time interval; this allows us to set up a continuous induction (bootstrap) argument for constructing and estimating
\Phi
.

With such details in mind, in what follows, for the simplicity of exposition,
we will simply assume the existence of
X(t),\Xi(t)
and
\Phi(t,X(t))
for
0\leq t\leq\frac{100}{99}t_{f}(\tau_{M})
and
demonstrate how to derive bounds for
X(t),\Xi(t)
and
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))
.

Control of
\Xi(t)
.

To control
\Xi(t)
, we employ a comparison argument that is similar to the steady case. We introduce
\lambda(t)
and
\overline{\lambda}(t)
, which are solutions to the ODEs

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{\lambda}(t)&=-(1-\epsilon)f^{\prime\prime}(t,0)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda),\\
\lambda(0)&=\lambda_{0},\end{aligned}\right.

and

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{\overline{\lambda}}(t)&=-(1+\epsilon)f^{\prime\prime}(t,0)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\overline{\lambda}),\\
\overline{\lambda}(0)&=(1+\epsilon)\lambda_{0}.\end{aligned}\right.

Recall also that
t_{f}(\tau)
is defined by the relation
\int_{0}^{t_{f}(\tau)}(-f^{\prime\prime})(t,0)\,\mathrm{d}t=\tau
. For
0\leq t\leq\frac{100}{99}t_{f}(T)
, we claim that, for
\Lambda\leq\lambda\leq\Xi(t)\leq\overline{\lambda}
and
0<X(t)<x_{1}
,

\displaystyle-(1-\epsilon)f^{\prime\prime}(t,0)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\leq-f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))+(\Gamma f^{\prime\prime})(t,X(t))\lambda_{0}

\displaystyle\leq-(1+\epsilon)f^{\prime\prime}(t,0)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\overline{\lambda}),

provided that
\Lambda
is sufficiently large depending on
\gamma
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
.
The proof is similar to the steady case using (
4.24
), (
4.25
) and (
4.27
). The first inequality implies, in particular, that
\dot{\Xi}>0
for
\lambda_{0}\leq\Lambda
. Observing furthermore that
\dot{X}<0
but
X(t)>0
, and comparing the ODEs for
\lambda(t)
,
\Xi(t)
and
\overline{\lambda}(t)
, we arrive at

\lambda(t)\leq\Xi(t)\leq\overline{\lambda}(t)\qquad\hbox{ for }0\leq t\leq\frac{100}{99}t_{f}(T).

The following analogue of Lemma
4.3
holds:

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\lambda_{0}
,
M
and
\tau_{M}
obey (
1.18
)–(
1.20
) as well as
\tau_{M}\leq T
. Then the following statements hold.

1.

\lambda(t)=M\lambda_{0}
exactly at
t_{M}:=\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}t_{f}(\tau_{M})
, and
\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda(t)\leq M\lambda_{0}
for
0\leq t\leq t_{M}
.

2.

As long as
\lambda(t)\leq M\lambda_{0}
, we have

\lambda(t)\leq\Xi(t)=\partial_{x}\Phi(t,X(t))\leq 2\lambda(t).

(4.30)

Proof.

Using the identities

\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}=(1-\epsilon)t_{f}(t),\qquad\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\overline{M}^{\prime}\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}=(1+\epsilon)t_{f}(t)

in lieu of (
4.19
) (where
M^{\prime}\lambda_{0}=\lambda(t)
and
\overline{M}^{\prime}\lambda_{0}=\overline{\lambda}(t)
as before), the proof of this lemma proceeds exactly as in that of Lemma
4.3
. We omit the details.
∎

Control of
X(t)
.

Next, we aim to obtain a bound for
X(t)
that is comparable to (
4.20
) in the steady case. Instead of a simple argument involving the Hamiltonian (which is exactly preserved in the steady case), here we need an analysis of the bicharacteristic ODEs.

Let
0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}t_{f}(\tau_{M})
with
\tau_{M}\leq T
. Recall the soft fact that
0<X(t)\leq X(0)<x_{1}
. Using the bicharacteristic ODEs, we may write

\displaystyle\frac{\dot{X}(t)}{X(t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{f^{\prime}(t,X(t))}{X(t)}\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))

\displaystyle=-\frac{|{f^{\prime}(t,X(t))}|}{|{f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))}|X(t)}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}\left(\frac{|{f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))}|\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}{|{f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))}|\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))+\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))\lambda_{0}}\right)\dot{\Xi}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{|{f^{\prime}(t,X(t))}|}{|{f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))}|X(t)}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}\left(1-\frac{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))}{|{f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))}|\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))+\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))}\right)\dot{\Xi}(t).

By (
4.24
) and (
4.25
), for
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda
with
\Lambda
sufficiently large depending on
\gamma
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
on
[0,\frac{100}{99}t_{f}(T)]\times(0,x_{1})
, we have

\left|{\frac{|{f^{\prime}(t,X(t))}|}{|{f^{\prime\prime}(t,0)}|X(t)}-1}\right|\lesssim C_{0}X(t),\qquad\left|{\frac{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))}{|{f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))}|\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))+\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}(t,X(t))}}\right|\lesssim C_{1}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))},

where the implicit constants are absolute,
C_{0}=|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}\|{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,\frac{100}{99}t_{f}(T)]\times(0,x_{1}))}
and
C_{1}=|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}\|{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,\frac{100}{99}t_{f}(T)]\times(0,x_{1}))}
. By integration in time, it follows that

\displaystyle\log\frac{X(t)}{X(0)}-CC_{0}(X(0)-X(t))

\displaystyle\leq\log\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(0))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}+CC_{1}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(0))}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}\right),

\displaystyle\log\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(0))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}-CC_{1}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(0))}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\log\frac{X(t)}{X(0)}+CC_{0}(X(0)-X(t)),

for some absolute constant
C>0
. By the monotonicity properties of
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
,
X
and
\Xi
, observe that all non-logarithmic terms may be made arbitrarily small by taking
c_{x_{0}}>0
small and
\Lambda
large depending on
\gamma
,
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
. Finally, by the slow-variance of
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
and Lemma
4.7
, we may replace
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(0))
and
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))
by
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})
and
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))
, respectively.

In conclusion, for
0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}t_{f}(\tau_{M})
,
\tau_{M}\leq T
and
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda
, we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{1}{{2^{\beta_{0}+1}}}\frac{X(0)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))}\leq X(t)\leq{2^{\beta_{0}+1}}\frac{X(0)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))}.\end{split}

(4.31)

as long as
c_{x_{0}}>0
is sufficiently small and
\Lambda
is sufficiently large depending on
\gamma
,
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}f^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
.

Control of
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))
.
Next, we turn to
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))
. Note that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))=\partial_{t}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))+\dot{X}(t)\partial_{x}^{3}\Phi(t,X(t)).

By the equation

\partial_{t}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,x)+\partial_{\xi}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\partial_{x}^{3}\Phi(t,x)+\partial_{x}^{2}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)+2\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,x)+\partial_{\xi}^{2}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)(\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi)^{2}=0,

it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))

\displaystyle=-\partial_{x}^{2}p(t,X(t),\Xi(t))-2\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p(t,X(t),\Xi(t))\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))

(4.32)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p(t,X(t),\Xi(t))(\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t)))^{2}.

Note that (
4.32
) is a Ricatti-type ODE. As is well-known, this ODE is prone to blowing up in finite time, and indeed this is the reflection of the possibility of formation of focal or conjugate points along bicharacteristics in the Hamilton–Jacobi formulation. While it is possible to play with the initial data for
\Phi
and analyze (
4.32
) directly to obtain control on
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))
, it is not clear how to obtain control on a sufficiently long time interval needed for our purposes.

Instead, motivated by (
4.21
), we perform the following inspired change of variables:

h(t):=\frac{\partial_{\xi}p(t,X(t),\Xi(t))}{\partial_{x}p(t,X(t),\Xi(t))}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))+1.

(4.33)

Our choice of the initial data (
4.26
) is such that
h
is initially zero. More precisely, by (
4.26
), we have

\partial_{x}f^{\prime}(0,0)x_{1}\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})+p(0,x,\partial_{x}\Phi(0,x))=0,

hence by differentiating in
x
, it follows that

h(0)=0\qquad\hbox{ for }X(0)=x\in(x_{0},x_{1})\,\mbox{ and }\,\Xi(0)=\partial_{x}\Phi(0,x).

(4.34)

We shall show that the variable
h
obeys the following remarkably nice evolution equation:

Lemma 4.8
.

We have

\dot{h}=s-(q+r+s)h+qh^{2},

(4.35)

where

s:=-\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}+\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{x}p},\qquad r:=\partial_{x}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p},\qquad q:=-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p},

(4.36)

which are all evaluated at
(t,x,\xi)=(t,X(t),\Xi(t))
.

Note that
s=0
when
p
is time-independent. By (
4.34
), it is then clear that when
p
is time independent,
h
remains zero; this is precisely the computation (
4.21
) in the steady case! The advantage of (
4.35
) over (
4.32
) is that we may now incorporate the time dependence of
p
in a perturbative manner in the estimate for
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))
.

Proof.

To simplify the notation, we omit the dependence of various derivatives of
p
on
(t,X(t),\Xi(t))
, as well as the dependence of
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi
on
(t,X(t))
. We begin by computing

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{\xi}p+\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p\dot{X}+\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\dot{\Xi}}{\partial_{x}p}-\frac{(\partial_{t}\partial_{x}p+\partial_{x}^{2}p\dot{X}+\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p\dot{\Xi})\partial_{\xi}p}{(\partial_{x}p)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}+\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}-\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{x}p}-\partial_{x}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}+\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p\right)\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}

\displaystyle=\left(2\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}-\partial_{x}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}+\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}-\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{x}p}\right)\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}h

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}\right)\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))+\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))

\displaystyle=\left(2\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}-\partial_{x}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}+\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}-\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{x}p}\right)\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\partial_{x}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}-2\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}(\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t)))^{2}.

Observe the cancellation of the term
2\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))
. Writing
\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))=h-1
, we moreover observe that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}h

\displaystyle=\left(-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}-\partial_{x}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}+\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}-\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{x}p}\right)(h-1)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}-\partial_{x}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}(h-1)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}+\partial_{x}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}-\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}+\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{x}p}\right)-\partial_{x}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}\left(-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}-\partial_{x}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{x}p}+\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}-\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{x}p}+2\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}\right)h-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}h^{2},

from which (
4.35
) follows.∎

We now analyze (
4.35
) to obtain a uniform control on
h
under the additional assumption (
1.25
) compared to the steady case. Then, by (
4.33
),
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))
would enjoy similar estimates as in the steady case.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
\lambda_{0}
,
M
and
\tau_{M}
satisfy (
1.18
)–(
1.20
),
\tau_{M}\leq T\leq 1
as well as (
1.25
). Then for any
\delta_{5}>0
, by taking
c_{x_{0}}
smaller and
\Lambda
larger depending on
\delta_{5}
,
\gamma
and
f
(for the precise dependence, see
C_{0}
,
C_{0}^{\prime}
and
C_{1}
in the proof), we have

|{h(t)}|\leq\delta_{5}\qquad\hbox{ for }0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}t_{f}(\tau_{M})\hbox{ and }\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda.

(4.37)

Proof.

In this proof, all implicit constants are absolute unless otherwise stated.
Consider the ODE (
4.35
). Note that
q
can be alternatively written as

q=\frac{\partial_{\xi}^{2}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}(-\partial_{x}p)(t,X(t),\Xi(t))=\left(\partial_{\xi}\log\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}\right)(\Xi(t))\dot{\Xi}(t).

(4.38)

We begin by expanding the terms in (
4.35
) that involve
\partial_{t}
:

\displaystyle-s=\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{\xi}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}-\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{x}p}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{t}f^{\prime}}{f^{\prime}}-\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{x}f^{\prime}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)+\partial_{t}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime\prime}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)+\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{t}f^{\prime}}{f^{\prime}}-\frac{\partial_{t}f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime\prime}}+\frac{\partial_{t}f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime\prime}}\frac{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime\prime}(\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)+\Gamma f^{\prime\prime})}+\frac{\partial_{t}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}{f^{\prime\prime}(\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)+\Gamma f^{\prime\prime})}.

Observe that, in the first two terms, the terms of order
O(1)
cancel and we are left with
O(x)
. On the other hand, the remaining two terms are bounded by
O(\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)})
. Combined with Lemma
4.7
and (
4.31
), we obtain (for
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda
sufficiently large)

\displaystyle|{s}|

\displaystyle\lesssim|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|\left(C_{1}X+\frac{C_{0}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}\right)\lesssim|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))}\left(C_{1}c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})+\frac{C_{0}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))^{2}}\frac{\dot{\lambda}(t)}{\lambda_{0}}\left(C_{1}c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})+\frac{C_{0}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}\right),

(4.39)

where

\displaystyle C_{0}

\displaystyle=|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-2}\|{\partial_{t}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,\tfrac{1}{1-\epsilon}t_{f}(\tau_{M})]\times(0,2c_{x_{0}}\epsilon))}+|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}\|{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,\tfrac{1}{1-\epsilon}t_{f}(\tau_{M})]\times(0,2c_{x_{0}}\epsilon))},

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-2}\|{\partial_{t}f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,\tfrac{1}{1-\epsilon}t_{f}(\tau_{M})]\times(0,2c_{x_{0}}\epsilon))}+|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}\|{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,\tfrac{1}{1-\epsilon}t_{f}(\tau_{M})]\times(0,2c_{x_{0}}\epsilon))}.

Next, for the term
r
, we again use Lemma
4.7
and (
4.31
) to estimate

|{r}|\lesssim C_{1}^{\prime}X(t)\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda(t))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))}\dot{\lambda(t)}\lesssim C_{1}^{\prime}c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda(t))\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))^{2}}\dot{\lambda}(t),

(4.40)

where

C_{1}^{\prime}=C_{1}+\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})^{-1}|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}\|{\Gamma f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,\tfrac{1}{1-\epsilon}t_{f}(\tau_{M})]\times(0,2c_{x_{0}}\epsilon))}.

We are now ready to set up a bootstrap argument (continuous induction in time) to prove (
4.37
). Assume, without any loss of generality, that
\delta_{5}<\delta_{1}
. Initially, recall that
h(0)=0
. As a bootstrap assumption, assume that
|{h(t)}|<\delta_{1}
on some time interval
[0,t^{\ast}]
. By the method of integrating factor, (
4.38
) and the bootstrap assumption, we estimate, for any
t\in[0,t^{\ast}]
,

\displaystyle|{h(t)}|

\displaystyle\leq\left|{\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}(q+r+s-qh)(t^{\prime\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime\prime}\right)s(t^{\prime})\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}(q+|{r}|+|{s}|+|{q}|\delta_{1})(t^{\prime\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime\prime}\right)|{s(t^{\prime})}|\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\max\left\{\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda(t^{\prime}))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})},\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda(t^{\prime}))}\right\}^{\delta_{1}}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda(t^{\prime}))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})}|{s(t^{\prime})}|\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{\leq}}\times\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}(|{s}|+|{r}|)(t^{\prime\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime\prime}\right).

We first claim that the last term is uniformly bounded in
t
. Indeed, by (
4.39
), monotonicity of
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
, Lemma
4.7
(by which
M^{\prime}(t)\leq M
) and
\tau_{M}\leq T\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}|{s}|(t^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0}}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}\left(C_{1}c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})+\frac{C_{0}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}\left(C_{1}c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})+\frac{C_{0}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(C_{1}c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})+\frac{C_{0}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}\right).

(4.41)

Next, by (
4.40
) and
M^{\prime}(t)\leq M

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}|{r}|(t^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\lesssim C_{1}^{\prime}c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0}}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\leq C_{1}^{\prime}c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0}).

(4.42)

In particular, by taking
c_{x_{0}}>0
smaller and
\Lambda
larger depending on
\gamma
,
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{1}^{\prime}
, we may ensure that the RHSs of (
4.41
) and (
4.42
) are smaller than, say,
1
. Thus,

\displaystyle|{h(t)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0}}\max\left\{\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})},\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)}\right\}^{\delta_{1}}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{\leq}}\times\left(C_{1}c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})+\frac{C_{0}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}\right).

We split the
\lambda
-integral as follows. Define
E:=\{\lambda\in(0,M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0}):\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})^{-1}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)\leq 1\}
. As in the proof of (
4.41
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{(0,M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})\cap E}\max\left\{\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})},\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)}\right\}^{\delta_{1}}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{M^{\prime}(t)\lambda}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda_{0}}\leq\tau_{M}\leq T\leq 1.

On the other hand, the
\lambda
-integral on
(0,M^{\prime}(t)\lambda_{0})\setminus E
is bounded from above by the LHS of (
1.25
). In conclusion,

\displaystyle|{h(t)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(C_{1}c_{x_{0}}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})+\frac{C_{0}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}\right).

Taking
c_{x_{0}}>0
smaller and
\Lambda
larger depending on
\delta_{5}
,
\gamma
,
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
, we obtain
|{h(t)}|\leq\delta_{5}
, which improves the bootstrap assumption. ∎

4.4
Estimates for the phase along characteristic curves

We pick up from the end of either Section
4.2
and
4.3
; that is,
we assume that the hypotheses, and therefore the conclusions, of one of these sections hold.
Our goal in this subsection is to obtain sharp bounds for the derivatives of
\Phi
, which are essential for sharp estimates for the amplitude function in Section
4.5
below. Our key idea is again to consider a renormalization of the form (
4.33
).

To this end, we consider the transport operator

\nabla_{t}:=\partial_{t}+\partial_{\xi}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\partial_{x}.

Observe that the characteristics for this operator are precisely
X(t)
in Sections
4.2
and
4.3
. Moreover, the solutions to
\nabla_{t}\phi=0
obey a-priori
L^{\infty}
-bounds. We introduce the commutator notation

[\nabla_{t},\partial_{x}]=A\partial_{x},\quad\hbox{ or equivalently, }A=-\partial_{x}\left(\partial_{\xi}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi(t,x))\right).

(4.43)

We generalize the characteristic-wise definition (
4.33
) of
h
by

h(t,x)=\frac{\partial_{\xi}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi(t,x))}{\partial_{x}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi(t,x))}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,x)+1.

The transport equations for
h
and its the derivatives then follow from Lemma
4.8
:

\begin{split}\nabla_{t}\partial_{x}^{k}h&=kA\partial_{x}^{k}h-(q+r+s)\partial_{x}^{k}h+2qh\partial_{x}^{k}h+\partial_{x}^{k}s\\
&\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-1}C_{k,\ell}^{1}\partial_{x}^{\ell}A\partial_{x}^{k-\ell}h+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}C_{k,\ell}^{2}\partial_{x}^{\ell}\left(q+r+s\right)\partial_{x}^{k-\ell}h\\
&\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{k-\ell}C^{3}_{k,\ell,m}\partial_{x}^{\ell}q\partial_{x}^{m}h\partial_{x}^{k-\ell-m}h.\end{split}

(4.44)

In the above,
q
,
r
and
s
are defined as in (
4.36
) but evaluated at
(t,x,\xi)=(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi(t,x))
, and
C_{k,\ell}^{1}
,
C_{k,\ell}^{2}
and
C_{k,\ell,m}^{3}
are some combinatorial coefficients.

Before we continue, we note the following key commutator computation:

\displaystyle{}[\nabla_{t},\partial_{x}]=A\partial_{x}

\displaystyle=-\left(\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)-\partial_{\xi}^{2}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\frac{\partial_{x}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)}{\partial_{\xi}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)}(1-h)\right)\partial_{x}

(4.45)

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\partial_{\xi}\partial_{x}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)}{\partial_{x}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)}-\frac{\partial_{\xi}^{2}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)}{\partial_{\xi}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)}(1-h)\right)\partial_{x}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\partial_{x}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)}-\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)}{\gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)}+\frac{O(\delta_{5}+\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})^{-1})}{\Xi}\right)\dot{\Xi}\partial_{x},

where
O(\cdot)
refers to a term whose absolute value is bounded by
C(\cdot)
with
C
depending on
\gamma
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
. In what follows,
we shall take
\Lambda_{0}
larger so that
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})^{-1}<\delta_{5}
.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
N_{0}
be a positive integer. There exist
\delta_{3}\ll\frac{\delta_{2}}{N_{0}}
and
\delta_{5}\ll\delta_{0}\delta_{3}
such that, the following holds. If
|{h(t,X(t))}|\leq\delta_{5}
for
0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}t_{f}(\tau_{M})
, where
\tau_{M}\leq 1
, then for
k=1,\ldots,N_{0}
,

\displaystyle|{\partial_{x}^{k}\partial_{x}\Phi(t,X(t))}|

\displaystyle\leq\mu(t)^{k-1}(-\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))),

(4.46)

\displaystyle|{\partial_{x}^{k}\partial_{x}\Phi(t,X(t))}|

\displaystyle\leq\mu(t)^{k}\lambda(t),

(4.47)

where

\mu(t)=\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2\delta_{3}N_{0}}}{x_{0}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda(t))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda(t))}.

(4.48)

Remark 4.11
.

We remark that the requirement
|{h(t,X(t))}|\leq\delta_{5}
is vacuous in the steady case (Section
4.2
), whereas in the time-dependent case it may be ensured by taking
\Lambda
larger and
c_{x_{0}}
smaller depending on
\delta_{5}
(Proposition
4.9
in Section
4.3
).

Proof.

In the proof, we fix a bicharacteristic curve
(X(t),\Xi(t))
with
x_{0}<X(0)<x_{1}
and
\Xi(0)=\partial_{x}\Phi(0,X(0))
.

An integration factor.

For simplicity, we set

\begin{split}I(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\left(\frac{\partial_{\xi}\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{x}p}-\frac{\partial_{\xi}^{2}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}(1-h)\right)(\tau,X(\tau),\Xi(\tau))(-\partial_{x}p)(\tau,X(\tau),\Xi(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau.\end{split}

(4.49)

Recalling the computations following (
4.45
), we have the bounds

\begin{split}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi)}\left(\frac{\Xi}{\lambda_{0}}\right)^{-\delta_{4}}\leq\exp(I(t))\leq\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi)}\left(\frac{\Xi}{\lambda_{0}}\right)^{\delta_{4}}\end{split}

(4.50)

where
\delta_{4}=C\delta_{5}
with some constant
C>0
depending on
\gamma
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
.
We shall establish the claimed bounds by propagating a sharp estimate based on the quantity
I(t)
.

Induction base case.

The following bound for
h
, which could be weaker than (
4.37
), is more convenient as an induction hypothesis:

\begin{split}|{h(t,X(t))}|\leq\lambda_{0}^{\frac{\delta_{3}}{4}}\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))}\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}}.\end{split}

(4.51)

To prove this, we introduce
h^{(0)}(t)=\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))h(t,X(t))
, which incorporates an integrating factor for the term
qh
. Then

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}h^{(0)}+(r+s-2qh)h^{(0)}=\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))s.

By the ellipticity assumption for
\xi\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
and
\dot{\Xi}=-\partial_{x}p
, we have

2|{qh}|\leq C\delta_{5}\frac{\dot{\Xi}}{\Xi},

(4.52)

where
C
depends on
\gamma
. Using (
4.39
), (
4.41
) and (
4.42
), we obtain

\displaystyle|{h^{(0)}(t)}|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}|{r}|+|{s}|+2|{qh}|\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime\prime}\right)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi)|{s}|\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\Xi(t)}{\lambda_{0}}\right)^{C\delta_{5}}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)^{2}}\dot{\Xi}\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}.

The first factor is bounded by
\lambda_{0}^{\frac{C\delta_{5}}{\delta_{0}}}
, where as the
t^{\prime}
-integral is evaluated and bounded as

\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)^{2}}\dot{\Xi}\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}=\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\Xi(t)}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\Xi=1-\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}\leq 1.

(4.53)

Hence, by taking
\delta_{5}\ll\delta_{0}\delta_{3}
and returning to
h
, (
4.51
) follows.

Induction hypothesis.

We now turn to the case
k_{0}\geq 1
. When
k_{0}>1
, we assume the following in addition to
|{h}|\leq\delta_{5}
: For
k=1,\ldots,k_{0}-1
,

\displaystyle|\partial^{k}_{x}h(t,X(t))|

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}k^{2}}x_{0}^{-k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-k}}\exp(kI(t))\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))}\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}},

(4.54)

\displaystyle|\partial^{k}_{x}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))|

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}((k+1)^{2}-1)}x_{0}^{-k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-k}}\exp(kI(t))(-\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))).

(4.55)

To handle the quadratic term in
h
, it is easier to work with the following simplification of (
4.54
). Note that

\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi)}\lesssim\frac{\Xi}{\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)}(\log\Xi)^{2}\lesssim\max\left\{\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})},\tau_{M}\right\}\delta_{0}^{-2}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}\lesssim\delta_{0}^{-2}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}.

(4.56)

In the second inequality, we used the fact that
\frac{\Xi}{\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)}
is clearly bounded by
\frac{1}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}
for
\Xi\leq 4
, and by
\tau_{M}
for
\Xi\geq 4
thanks to
\lambda\leq\Xi\leq 2\lambda
and
\lambda\leq M\lambda_{0}
. As a consequence of
|h|\leq\delta_{5}
for
k=0
and (
4.54
) and (
4.56
) for
k\geq 1
, we obtain, for
k=0,\ldots,k_{0}-1
,

|{x_{0}^{k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{k}}\exp(-kI(t))\partial^{k}_{x}h(t,X(t))}|\lesssim_{\delta_{0}}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}k^{2}}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}.

(4.57)

Induction argument.
Our goal now is to prove (
4.54
) and (
4.55
) for
k=k_{0}
.

To use (
4.44
) to establish (
4.54
) for
k=k_{0}
, we work with new variables that incorporate integrating factors for cancelling the large coefficient
kA-q
. For
k\geq 1
, define

\displaystyle h^{(k)}(t)

\displaystyle=x_{0}^{k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{k}}\exp(-kI(t))\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))\partial_{x}^{k}h(t,X(t)),

\displaystyle A^{(k)}(t)

\displaystyle=x_{0}^{k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{k}}\exp(-kI(t))\partial_{x}^{k}A(t,X(t)),

\displaystyle s^{(k)}(t)

\displaystyle=x_{0}^{k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{k}}\exp(-kI(t))\partial_{x}^{k}s(t,X(t)),

\displaystyle r^{(k)}(t)

\displaystyle=x_{0}^{k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{k}}\exp(-kI(t))\partial_{x}^{k}r(t,X(t)),

\displaystyle q^{(k)}(t)

\displaystyle=x_{0}^{k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{k}}\exp(-kI(t))\partial_{x}^{k}q(t,X(t)).

Observe that (
4.51
) and (
4.54
) are equivalent to

|{h^{(0)}}|\leq\lambda_{0}^{\frac{\delta_{3}}{4}},\quad|{h^{(k)}}|\leq\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}k^{2}}\quad\hbox{ for }1\leq k<k_{0}.

(4.58)

Moreover, evaluating along a characteristic curve, we note that (
4.44
) can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}h^{(k)}+(r+s-2qh)h^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))s^{(k)}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k-1}C^{1}_{k,\ell}A^{(\ell)}h^{(k-\ell)}

(4.59)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}C^{2}_{k,\ell}\left(q^{(\ell)}+r^{(\ell)}+s^{(\ell)}\right)h^{(k-\ell)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{k-\ell}C^{3}_{k,\ell,m}\frac{q^{(\ell)}}{\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi)}h^{(m)}h^{(k-\ell-m)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\sum_{m=1}^{k-1}C^{3}_{k,0,m}\frac{q}{\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi)}h^{(m)}h^{(k-m)}.

We shall prove the following estimates for the coefficients of (
4.59
): for
1\leq k\leq k_{0}
,

\displaystyle|{s^{(k)}(t)}|

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(k^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))^{2}\lambda_{0}}\dot{\Xi}(t)\left(C_{k,1}x_{0}+\frac{C_{k,0}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}\right),

(4.60)

\displaystyle|{r^{(k)}(t)}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{k,0}C_{k,1}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(k^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))^{2}}\dot{\Xi}(t)x_{0},

(4.61)

\displaystyle|{q^{(k)}(t)}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{k,0}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(k^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\frac{\dot{\Xi}(t)}{\Xi(t)},

(4.62)

and for
k>1
,

\displaystyle|{A^{(k-1)}(t)}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{k-1,0}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}((k-1)^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\frac{\dot{\Xi}(t)}{\Xi(t)}.

(4.63)

Here,
C_{k,1}
depends on
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}f^{\prime\prime\prime}
,
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-2}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\partial_{t}f^{\prime\prime\prime}
for
0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k
; and
C_{k,0}
depends on
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}f^{\prime\prime}
,
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-2}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\partial_{t}f^{\prime\prime}
,
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-2}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\partial_{t}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
for
0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
C_{k^{\prime},j}\leq C_{k,j}
for
k^{\prime}\leq k
and
j=0,1
.

Assuming (
4.60
)–(
4.63
), we can improve the induction hypothesis (
4.54
) for
h
as follows. Using (
4.41
), (
4.42
), (
4.52
) and (
4.59
), we have

\displaystyle|{h^{(k_{0})}}|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}(|{r}|+|{s}|+|{q}|\delta_{5}\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime\prime}\right)|{\hbox{(RHS of \eqref{eq:HJE-h-der2})}}|\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\lesssim\left(\frac{\Xi(t)}{\lambda_{0}}\right)^{C\delta_{5}}\int_{0}^{t}|{\hbox{(RHS of \eqref{eq:HJE-h-der2})}}|\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}.

By (
4.58
) for
k<k_{0}
, (
4.60
)–(
4.63
), as well as (
4.56
), we obatin

\displaystyle|{\hbox{(RHS of \eqref{eq:HJE-h-der2})}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C_{k_{0},0},C_{k_{0},1},\delta_{0}^{-1}}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(k_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\left(\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi)^{2}}+\lambda_{0}^{\frac{1}{4}}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}\frac{1}{\Xi}\right)\dot{\Xi}

\displaystyle\phantom{\lesssim_{C_{k_{0},0},C_{k_{0},1},\delta_{0}^{-1}}}+\sum_{m=1}^{k_{0}-1}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(m^{2}+(k_{0}-m)^{2})}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}\frac{\dot{\Xi}}{\Xi},

where the last term arises from the contribution of the last sum in (
4.59
) (this sum is vacuous when
k_{0}=1
). Using
m^{2}+(k_{0}-m)^{2}\leq k_{0}^{2}-2
for
1\leq m\leq k_{0}-1
, we obtain

\displaystyle|{h^{(k_{0})}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C_{k_{0},0},C_{k_{0},1},\delta_{0}^{-1}}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(k_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{4})}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}\left(\frac{\Xi(t)}{\lambda_{0}}\right)^{C\delta_{5}}\left(1-\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}+\log\frac{\Xi(t)}{\lambda_{0}}\right).

Taking
\delta_{5}\ll\delta_{0}\delta_{3}
and requiring
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda
, where
\Lambda
is sufficiently large, we obtain
|{h^{(k_{0})}}|\leq\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}k_{0}^{2}}
, which is equivalent to (
4.54
) for
k=k_{0}
, as desired.

Next, we improve (
4.55
). We begin with the formula

\partial_{x}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,x)=\left(\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}\right)\partial_{x}^{k_{0}}h+\partial_{x}^{k_{0}}\left(\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}\right)(h-1)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{0}-1}\partial_{x}^{\ell}\left(\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}\right)\partial_{x}^{k-\ell}h,

(4.64)

where
\partial_{x}p
and
\partial_{\xi}p
are evaluated at
(t,x,\xi)=(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi(t,x))
. We will prove that, for
0\leq k\leq k_{0}
,

\left|{x_{0}^{k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{k}}\exp(-kI(t))\partial_{x}^{k}\left(\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}\right)(t,X(t),\Xi(t))}\right|\leq C_{k,0}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}k^{2}}(-\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi),

(4.65)

where
C_{k,0}
depends on
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
and
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-2}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\partial_{t}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
for
0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that these constants are the same as those in (
4.60
)–(
4.63
). In the proof of (
4.65
), it is important that
\partial_{x}^{k}(\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p})
involves only
\partial_{x}^{k^{\prime}}\Phi
with
k^{\prime}\leq k+1<k_{0}+2
, so that we may apply the induction hypothesis (
4.55
). We postpone the details until later.

Assuming (
4.65
), and also using
|{h}|\leq\delta_{5}
, (
4.57
) for
k\leq k_{0}
(the case
k=k_{0}
has just been established) and (
4.68
) for
k\leq k_{0}-1
, we may estimate the RHS of (
4.64
) by

|{\partial_{x}^{k_{0}}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t)}|\lesssim_{\delta_{0},C_{k_{0},0}}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}k_{0}^{2}}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}x_{0}^{-k_{0}}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-k_{0}}}\exp(k_{0}I)(-\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi),

where used the simple inequality
(k_{0}-\ell)^{2}+\ell^{2}\leq k_{0}^{2}
. Since the exponent
\delta_{3}((k_{0}+1)^{2}-1)
on
\lambda_{0}
in (
4.55
) is strictly greater than
\delta_{3}k_{0}^{2}
, taking
\lambda_{0}
large enough depending on
\delta_{0}
,
\delta_{3}
and
C_{k_{0},0}
, we obtain (
4.55
) for
k=k_{0}
.

Proof of (
4.60
)–(
4.63
), (
4.65
).

In the proof, we fix some
k\geq 1
. We may assume that the bounds (
4.54
) and (
4.55
) are available for any
\ell
satisfying
\ell<k
.

Some preliminary computations.

We claim that

\displaystyle|{\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\lambda_{0}^{\frac{\delta_{3}}{2}}}{x_{0}{\epsilon_{0}(\lambda_{0})}}\exp(I(t))\Xi(t),

(4.66)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{X(t)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\lambda_{0}^{\frac{\delta_{3}}{2}}}{x_{0}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})}}\exp(I(t)),

(4.67)

where
C
depends on
\gamma
. Note that (
4.66
) combined with (
4.55
) would lead to, for any
\ell\leq k-1
,

|{\partial^{\ell+1}_{x}\partial_{x}\Phi(t,X(t))}|\leq\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}((\ell+1)^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}x_{0}^{-(\ell+1)}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-(\ell+1)}}\exp((\ell+1)I(t))\Xi(t).

(4.68)

Ignoring the powers of
\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}}
, (
4.68
) tells us that every derivative of
\partial_{x}\Phi
loses
x_{0}^{-1}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-1}\exp(I)
, which is essentially
\mu
in (
4.48
); (
4.67
) says that the loss
x^{-1}
is more favorable than
x_{0}^{-1}\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-1}\exp(I)
along characteristics.

Essential to the proofs of both (
4.66
) and (
4.67
) is the following consequence of (
4.3
), which is connected to the assumption (
1.18
): If
2\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda(t)\leq M\lambda_{0}
, we have

\frac{\epsilon_{0}(\lambda_{0})}{\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})}\leq C\frac{(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}\epsilon_{0}(\lambda_{0})}{\gamma(\lambda_{0})}\leq C\epsilon_{0}(\lambda_{0})(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}\tau_{M^{\prime}}\leq C(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}\frac{M^{\prime}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(M^{\prime}\lambda_{0})},

(4.69)

where
M^{\prime}=\lambda_{0}^{-1}\lambda(t)
.

To prove (
4.66
), we first use (
4.33
),
|{h}|\leq\delta_{5}
, (
4.20
) or (
4.31
), and (
4.50
) to estimate, for
\lambda_{0}\geq\Lambda
sufficiently large depending on
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
,

\displaystyle\frac{x_{0}{\epsilon_{0}(\lambda_{0})}|{\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(t,X(t))}|}{\Xi(t)\exp(I(t))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C{\epsilon_{0}(\lambda_{0})}}{\Xi(t)\exp(I(t))}\frac{\partial_{x}p(t,X(t),\Xi(t))}{\partial_{\xi}p(t,X(t),\Xi(t))}

\displaystyle\leq C{\epsilon_{0}(\lambda_{0})}\left(\frac{\lambda_{0}}{\Xi(t)}\right)^{1-\delta_{4}}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}{\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})}.

For
\Xi(t)\leq 4\lambda_{0}
, (
4.66
) is obvious, and for
\Xi(t)\geq 4\lambda_{0}
, we apply (
4.69
) and
\lambda(t)\leq\Xi(t)\leq 2\lambda(t)
(from Lemma
4.3
or
4.7
).

Next, to prove (
4.67
), we use (
4.20
) or (
4.31
) and (
4.50
) to estimate

\displaystyle\frac{x_{0}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})}}{X(t)\exp(I(t))}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\epsilon_{0}(\lambda_{0})}\left(\frac{\Xi(t)}{\lambda_{0}}\right)^{\delta_{4}}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})}.

Again, for
\Xi(t)\leq 4\lambda_{0}
, (
4.67
) is obvious. For
\Xi(t)\geq 4\lambda_{0}
, we apply (
4.69
) and
\lambda(t)\leq\Xi(t)\leq 2\lambda(t)
(from Lemma
4.3
or
4.7
) and estimate

\displaystyle{\epsilon_{0}(\lambda_{0})}\left(\frac{\Xi(t)}{\lambda_{0}}\right)^{\delta_{4}}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})}\leq C\left(\frac{\Xi(t)}{\lambda_{0}}\right)^{\delta_{4}}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}\frac{\Xi(t)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi(t))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi(t))}\leq C\lambda_{0}^{\frac{\delta_{4}}{\delta_{0}}}(\log\lambda_{0})^{2},

which is acceptable.

Derivatives of
q
,
r
,
s
and
A
.

We start with some preparations. By Faà di Bruno’s formula,

\partial_{x}^{\ell}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}:a_{1}+2a_{2}+\cdots+\ell a_{\ell}=\ell}\frac{\ell!}{a_{1}!1!^{a_{1}}\cdots a_{\ell}!\ell!^{a_{\ell}}}(\partial_{\xi}^{a_{1}+\cdots+a_{\ell}}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}})(\partial_{x}\Phi)\prod_{b=1}^{\ell}(\partial_{x}^{1+b}\Phi)^{a_{b}}.

Observe that we see at most
\ell
derivatives falling on
\partial_{x}\Phi
, to which (
4.68
) applies as long as
\ell\leq k
. Using also the ellipticity assumption for
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
and
\sum_{b=1}^{\ell}(ba_{b})^{2}\leq\ell^{2}
, we obtain, for any
0\leq\ell\leq k
,

\left|{\left.\partial_{x}^{\ell}(\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi))\right|_{(t,x)=(t,X(t))}}\right|\lesssim_{\gamma,\ell}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(\ell^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}x_{0}^{-\ell}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-\ell}}\exp(\ell I)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\Xi).

(4.70)

Similarly, but using instead the ellipticity assumption for
\xi\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
, we also obtain, for any
0\leq\ell\leq k
,

\displaystyle\left|{\left.\partial_{x}^{\ell}(\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\partial_{x}\Phi))\right|_{(t,x)=(t,X(t))}}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\gamma,\ell}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(\ell^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}x_{0}^{-\ell}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-\ell}}\exp(\ell I)\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\Xi).

(4.71)

Next, we introduce the notation
\tilde{f}=(-f^{\prime\prime}(0,0))^{-1}f
. In what follows, we write
C_{\ell,1}
(resp.
C_{\ell,0}
) for a constant, that may vary from line to line, that depends on
(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}
,
(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\Gamma\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
(-f^{\prime\prime}(0,0))^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\partial_{t}\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}
(resp.
(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}
,
(-f^{\prime\prime}(0,0))^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\partial_{t}\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}
,
(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\Gamma\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}
and

(-f^{\prime\prime}(0,0))^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\partial_{t}\Gamma\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}
) for
0\leq k^{\prime}\leq\ell
. Then thanks to (
4.67
), we easily have, for any
\ell\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\left|{\tilde{f}^{(\ell+2)}(t,X(t))}\right|+\left|{\frac{\partial_{t}\tilde{f}^{(\ell+2)}(t,X(t))}{-f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}}\right|+\left|{\Gamma\tilde{f}^{(\ell+2)}(t,X(t))}\right|+\left|{\frac{\partial_{t}\Gamma\tilde{f}^{(\ell+2)}(t,X(t))}{-f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}}\right|

(4.72)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\ell,0}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}\frac{\ell}{2}}x_{0}^{-\ell}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-\ell}}\exp(\ell I),

\left|{\tilde{f}^{(\ell+3)}(t,X(t))}\right|+\left|{\Gamma\tilde{f}^{(\ell+3)}(t,X(t))}\right|+\left|{\frac{\partial_{t}\tilde{f}^{(\ell+3)}(t,X(t))}{-f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}}\right|\leq C_{\ell,1}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}\frac{\ell}{2}}x_{0}^{-\ell}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-\ell}}\exp(\ell I).

(4.73)

We are now ready to prove (
4.60
)–(
4.63
). We start with (
4.62
) for
q
, which may be written as

q(t,x)=\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime\prime}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}(-f^{\prime\prime})(0,0)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)\left(\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}(t,x)+\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)^{-1}\Gamma\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}(t,x)\right).

By (
4.71
) and the ellipticity property of
\xi\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
, we have, for any
0\leq\ell\leq k
,

\left|{x_{0}^{\ell}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{\ell}}\exp(-\ell I)\left.\partial_{x}^{\ell}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime\prime}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}\right|_{(t,x)=(t,X(t))}}\right|\lesssim_{\gamma,\ell}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(\ell^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\frac{1}{\Xi}.

By (
4.70
) and
\dot{\Xi}=-\partial_{x}p
, we also see that

\left|{x_{0}^{\ell}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{\ell}}\exp(-\ell I)\left.\partial_{x}^{\ell}(-f^{\prime\prime}(0,0))\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)\right|_{(t,x)=(t,X(t))}}\right|\lesssim_{\gamma,\ell}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(\ell^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\dot{\Xi}.

Derivatives of the last factor are easily bounded using (
4.70
) and (
4.72
). Putting together these bounds, (
4.62
) follows.

Next, we prove (
4.63
) for
A
, which we write as

\displaystyle A(t,x)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\xi}\partial_{x}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)-q(t,x)(1-h(t,x))

\displaystyle=-f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)\lambda_{0}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\partial_{x}\Phi)\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}(t,x)-q(t,x)(1-h(t,x)).

We now apply
\partial_{x}^{k-1}
to the above expression. The contribution of
q(1-h)
is already acceptable thanks to the previous bound for derivatives of
q
,
|{h}|\leq\delta_{5}
and (
4.57
) for
k-1
(indeed, note that we need to control only up to
k-1
derivatives of
h
). By (
4.71
), ellipticity of
\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}
and
\dot{\Xi}=-\partial_{x}p
, we may also bound the contribution of the first term by
C_{k-1,0}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}((k-1)^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\Xi^{-1}\dot{\Xi}
, which is acceptable.

To prove (
4.61
) for
r
, we write

\displaystyle r(t,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}p}{\partial_{x}p}\partial_{\xi}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)=(-f^{\prime\prime}(0,0))x\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\partial_{x}\Phi)\frac{\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t,x)+\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)^{-1}\Gamma\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}(t,x)}{\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}(t,x)+\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)^{-1}\Gamma\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}(t,x)}\frac{\tilde{f}^{\prime}(t,x)}{x},

and apply
\partial_{x}^{k}
to the above expression. By Taylor expansion and (
4.72
), we have, for any
\ell\geq 0
,

\left|{x_{0}^{\ell}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{\ell}}\exp(-\ell I)\left.\partial_{x}^{\ell}\left(x^{-1}\tilde{f}^{\prime}(t,x)\right)\right|_{(t,x)=(t,X(t)}}\right|\lesssim_{\ell}C_{\ell,0}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}\frac{\ell}{2}}.

On the other hand, using (
4.20
) or (
4.31
), (
4.67
), (
4.70
), (
4.71
), (
4.72
) and (
4.73
), it is straightforward to bound each factor appropriately and establish (
4.61
).

Finally, (
4.60
) for
s
is proved by starting from the explicit form of
s
given in the proof of Proposition
4.9
, then applying Taylor expansion for
(f^{\prime})^{-1}\partial_{t}f^{\prime}-(f^{\prime\prime})^{-1}\partial_{t}f^{\prime\prime}
, (
4.70
), (
4.72
) and (
4.73
), as well as (
4.20
) or (
4.31
) and
\dot{\Xi}=-\partial_{x}p
.

Proof of (
4.65
).

We begin by writing

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}=\frac{\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}(t,x)+\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)^{-1}\Gamma\tilde{f}^{\prime\prime}(t,x)}{x^{-1}\tilde{f}^{\prime}(t,x)}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}{x\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\partial_{x}\Phi)},

and applying
\partial_{x}^{k}
to the above expression. By (
4.67
), (
4.70
), (
4.71
) and (
4.72
) (as well as a Taylor-expansion argument for
x^{-1}f^{\prime}
as before), we have

\displaystyle\left|{x_{0}^{k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{k}}\exp(-kI)\partial_{x}^{k}\left(\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}\right)(t,X(t),\Xi(t))}\right|\leq C_{k,0}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(k^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}{x\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}.

Then by the relation
\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi=-\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}(1-h)
and
|{h}|\leq\delta_{5}
and the above expression for
\frac{\partial_{x}p}{\partial_{\xi}p}
, (
4.65
) follows. ∎

4.5
Estimates for the amplitude

In this section, we obtain
L^{2}
-estimates for the amplitude function
a
,
working under the same hypotheses as in Section
4.4
.
First, we rewrite the amplitude equation (
3.16
), incorporating the time dependence of
f
and using the notations and conventions in this section:

\begin{split}\partial_{t}a+\partial_{\xi}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\partial_{x}a+\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\xi}^{2}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\partial^{2}_{x}\Phi+s(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\right)a=0.\end{split}

(4.74)

Moreover, we recall that

\begin{split}s(t,x,\xi)&=\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(t,x)\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)\lambda_{0}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}(t,x)\frac{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)}\lambda_{0}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p(t,x,\xi)-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}(t,x)\frac{\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\xi)}\lambda_{0}.\end{split}

(4.75)

It will be convenient to define

\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}=\partial_{t}+\partial_{\xi}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\partial_{x}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)+\partial_{\xi}^{2}p(t,x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi\right)

so that solutions to
\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}a=0
obey a-priori
L^{2}
-bounds. Then, (
4.74
) is simply given by

\begin{split}\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}a=\frac{1}{2}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}\frac{\lambda_{0}\partial_{\xi}\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi)}a.\end{split}

Commutation with
\partial_{x}
again gives rise to a similar factor as before: recalling (
4.43
), we compute that

\begin{split}[\partial_{x},\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}]&=[\partial_{x},\nabla_{t}]+\frac{1}{2}[\partial_{x},\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)+\partial_{\xi}^{2}p(x,\partial_{x}\Phi)\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi]{=-A\partial_{x}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}A}.\end{split}

For simplicity, we also set

\begin{split}H(t,x):=-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}(t,x)\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\partial_{x}\Phi(t,x))}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\partial_{x}\Phi(t,x))}\lambda_{0}\end{split}

so that the equations for
a
,
\partial_{x}a
, and
\partial_{x}^{2}a
read

\begin{split}\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}a=Ha,\end{split}

\begin{split}\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}\partial_{x}a=[\nabla_{t},\partial_{x}]a+{\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}A}a+\partial_{x}(Ha),\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}\partial_{x}^{2}a=2[\nabla_{t},\partial_{x}]\partial_{x}a+{\frac{3}{2}\partial_{x}A}\partial_{x}a+{\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}((\partial_{x}A)a)}+\partial_{x}^{2}(Ha).\end{split}

Proposition 4.12
.

Assume that
a_{0}(x)
is sufficiently smooth and supported in
(x_{0},x_{1})
. For the solution of (
4.74
) with initial data
a(t=0)=a_{0}
, we have, for
0\leq k\leq N_{0}
,

\|{\mu^{-k}\partial_{x}^{k}a}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\|{({\mu_{0}^{-1}}\partial_{x})^{\ell}a_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}\qquad\hbox{ for }t\leq\tau

where

\mu_{0}=\frac{(\log\lambda_{0})^{2}}{x_{0}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})}},

(4.76)

and the implicit constant depends on
k
and
\gamma
, as well as
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}f^{\prime\prime}
,
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
for
0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k
.

Proof.

In what follows, the symbol
\lesssim_{k}
signifies an implicit constant that may depend on
k
and
\gamma
, as well as
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}f^{\prime\prime}
,
|{f^{\prime\prime}(0,0)}|^{-1}(x\partial_{x})^{k^{\prime}}\Gamma f^{\prime\prime}
for
0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k
.

To begin with, we observe the following form of the equation for
\partial_{x}^{k}a
:

\begin{split}\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}\partial_{x}^{k}a=k[\nabla_{t},\partial_{x}]\partial_{x}^{k-1}a+\widetilde{R}_{k}=kA\partial_{x}^{k}a+\widetilde{R}_{k},\end{split}

where
\widetilde{R}_{k}
is a linear combination of the terms

\begin{split}{\partial_{x}^{\ell}A\partial_{x}^{k-\ell}a,\quad\partial_{x}^{\ell}H\partial_{x}^{k-\ell}a}\end{split}

for
0\leq\ell\leq k
, with the exception that the term with
\ell=0
does not appear in the case of
A
. It will be convenient to first solve the following transport equation:

\begin{split}\nabla_{t}I=A,\end{split}

with the initial data
I(t=0)=0
. The solution, which is simply the integral in time of
A
along each characteristic curve, is nothing but
I
defined in (
4.49
). Since
\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}-\nabla_{t}
is simply a multiplication operator, we have that

\begin{split}\widetilde{\nabla}_{t}\left(\exp(-kI(t,x))\partial_{x}^{k}a(t,x)\right)=\exp(-kI(t,x))\widetilde{R}_{k}.\end{split}

(4.77)

We now set up the induction hypothesis: For
0\leq k\leq k_{0}
, we shall require

\begin{split}\sup_{0\leq t\leq\tau}\|{\exp(-kI(t,x))\partial_{x}^{k}a(t,x)}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{k}x_{0}^{-k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-k}}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}k^{2}}\sum_{0\leq\ell\leq k}\|{(x_{0}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})}\partial_{x})^{k}a_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

(4.78)

We first need to check the above for
k=0
. Recall the definition of
H
. By Lemma
4.3
or
4.7
and the definition of
\dot{\lambda}
, we have the obvious bound

|{H}|\lesssim\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{2}}\dot{\lambda},

Then from the equation for
a
, we have, by Grönwall’s inequality,

\begin{split}\sup_{0\leq t\leq\tau}\|{a(t)}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|{a_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

Let us now estimate
\exp(-kI(t,x))\partial_{x}^{k}a(t,x)
in
L^{2}
for some
k>0
, using (
4.78
) for all
\ell<k
. Recalling the bound (
4.63
) for
\partial_{x}^{k}A
along characteristics, we obtain the uniform bound
(k>0)

\begin{split}|\partial_{x}^{k}A|\lesssim_{k}x_{0}^{-k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-k}}\frac{\dot{\Xi}}{\Xi}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(k^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\exp(kI)\lesssim_{k}x_{0}^{-k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-k}}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(k^{2}-\frac{1}{2})}\exp(kI)\frac{\dot{\lambda}}{\lambda}\end{split}

using simply that
\frac{\dot{\Xi}}{\Xi}\leq C\frac{\dot{\lambda}}{\lambda}
. This allows us to bound

\begin{split}\|{\exp(-kI)\partial_{x}^{\ell}A\partial_{x}^{k-\ell}a}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{k}x_{0}^{-\ell}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-\ell}}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(\ell^{2}-\frac{1}{4})}\frac{\dot{\lambda}}{\lambda}\|{\exp(-(k-\ell)I)\partial_{x}^{k-\ell}a}\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

Finally, we need to estimate derivatives of
H
. We claim that for
k>0
,

\begin{split}|\partial_{x}^{k}H|\lesssim_{k}x_{0}^{-k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-k}}\exp(kI)\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(k^{2}-\frac{1}{4})}{\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{2}}\dot{\lambda}}.\end{split}

We omit the proof, which can be done along the lines of the proof of (
4.60
)–(
4.63
). Applying these bounds to (
4.77
) and taking the
L^{2}
inner product with
\exp(-kI(t,x))\partial_{x}^{k}a(t,x)
, we obtain that

\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\|{\exp(-kI(t,x))\partial_{x}^{k}a(t,x)}\|_{L^{2}}&\lesssim_{k}\sum_{\ell={1}}^{k}x_{0}^{-\ell}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-\ell}}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(\ell^{2}-\frac{1}{4})}{\left(\frac{\dot{\lambda}}{\lambda}+\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)\dot{\lambda}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{2}}\right)}\|{\exp(-(k-\ell)I)\partial_{x}^{k-\ell}a}\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\mathrel{\phantom{\lesssim}}+{\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)\dot{\lambda}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{2}}}\|{\exp(-kI(t,x))\partial_{x}^{k}a(t,x)}\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim_{k}x_{0}^{-k}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})^{-k}}\lambda_{0}^{\delta_{3}(k^{2}-\frac{1}{4})}{\left(\frac{\dot{\lambda}}{\lambda}+\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)\dot{\lambda}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{2}}\right)}\sum_{\ell=0}^{{k-1}}\|{(x_{0}{\epsilon(\lambda_{0})}\partial_{x})^{\ell}a_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\mathrel{\phantom{\lesssim}}+{\frac{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}^{\prime}(\lambda)\dot{\lambda}}{\gamma_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{2}}}\|{\exp(-kI(t,x))\partial_{x}^{k}a(t,x)}\|_{L^{2}}.\end{split}

Applying Grönwall’s inequality finishes the proof of (
4.78
). Recalling the definitions of
\mu
and
\mu_{0}
from (
4.48
) and (
4.76
), respectively, this completes the proof of the proposition (by taking
\Lambda
larger if necessary). ∎

4.6
Cutoff and extension of the phase function

In this section, we shall extend
\Phi
constructed in the previous subsections globally in space. For this purpose, we take points
x_{0}^{\prime}=\frac{2x_{0}+x_{1}}{3}
and
x_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{x_{0}+2x_{1}}{3}
so that
x_{0}<x_{0}^{\prime}<x_{1}^{\prime}<x_{1}
and let
\chi_{(x_{0},x_{1})}
be a smooth cutoff supported in
(x_{0},x_{1})
that equals
1
on
(x_{0}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime})
. Then the support of
1-\chi_{(x_{0},x_{1})}
has two components; we denote by
\chi_{(-\infty,x_{0})}
and
\chi_{(x_{1},\infty)}
the smooth cutoffs supported in the left and the right components, respectively, such that
1-\chi_{(x_{0},x_{1})}=\chi_{(-\infty,x_{0})}+\chi_{(x_{1},\infty)}
. For
\underline{x}\in(x_{0},x_{1})
, we define

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\Phi^{global}(t,X(t;\underline{x}))

\displaystyle=\chi_{(x_{0},x_{1})}(\underline{x})\partial_{x}\Phi(t,X(t;\underline{x}))

(4.79)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}+\chi_{(-\infty,x_{0})}(\underline{x})\partial_{x}\Phi(t,X(t;x_{0}))+\chi_{(x_{1},\infty)}(\underline{x})\partial_{x}\Phi(t,X(t;x_{1})),

where
X(t;\underline{x})
is the characteristic curve solving
\dot{X}=\partial_{\xi}p(t,X,\partial_{x}\Phi(t,X))
with
X(0;\underline{x})=\underline{x}
. We furthermore normalize

\Phi^{global}(t,X(t;\tfrac{x^{\prime}_{0}+x^{\prime}_{1}}{2}))=\Phi(t,X(t;\tfrac{x^{\prime}_{0}+x^{\prime}_{1}}{2})).

Finally, for
x
outside of the image of
X(t;(x_{0},x_{1}))
, we extend
\partial_{x}\Phi^{global}(t,x)
(smoothly) by constants.

By definition,

\Phi^{global}=\Phi(t,x)\qquad\hbox{ for }X(t,x_{0}^{\prime})\leq x\leq X(t,x_{1}^{\prime}).

To continue, for each
t
, denote by
\underline{x}(t,x)
the inverse of the map
\underline{x}\mapsto X(t;\underline{x})
. Since

\nabla_{t}\underline{x}(t,x)=0,\qquad\underline{x}(0,x)=x,

by an argument similar to Section
4.4
using (
4.63
), we have

|{\partial_{x}^{k}\underline{x}(t,X(t;\underline{x}))}|\lesssim_{k}\mu^{k}.

It follows that

\lambda\leq\partial_{x}\Phi^{global}\leq 2\lambda,\qquad|{\partial_{x}^{k}\partial_{x}\Phi^{global}}|\lesssim_{k}\mu^{k}\lambda\qquad\hbox{ for }1\leq k\leq N.

In Sections
6
and
7
,
we shall write
\Phi
for
\Phi^{global}
. This abuse of notation is minor, since our wave packet would be of the form
\Re(ae^{i\Phi})
, where
a(t,\cdot)
is supported in
(X(t,x_{0}^{\prime}),X(t,x_{1}^{\prime}))
, on which
\Phi^{global}=\Phi
.

5
Oscillatory integrals

The purpose of this section is to formulate and prove a result concerning the
L^{2}
-bound for the operators arising in the remainder in Proposition
3.3
. Logically, this section is
self-contained
so
the symbols in this section are detached from their meanings in the previous sections.
On the other hand, the results of this section will be applied in Section
6
to objects that are suggested by the notation here.

To begin with, observe that each term in the remainder symbols
{}^{(\Phi)}r_{p,-1}(x,\xi)
and
{}^{(\Phi)}r_{p,-2}(x,\xi)
in (
3.7
)–(
3.8
) is of the form
\int_{0}^{1}{}^{(\Phi)}q(x,\xi;\sigma)\,\mathrm{d}\sigma
, where

{}^{(\Phi)}q(x,\xi;\sigma):=\iint q(x,\sigma\eta+(1-\sigma)\partial_{y}\Phi(y))e^{i(\Phi(y)-\Phi(x)+(\eta-\xi)\cdot(x-y))}\,\mathrm{d}y\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{(2\pi)^{2}}

(5.1)

for some symbol
q
. Hence, it is expedient to formulate a
L^{2}
-bound result for an operator of the form
{}^{(\Phi)}q(x,D;\sigma)
under suitable assumptions on
q
.

Our main result is as follows:

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}
,
\mu>0
,
\lambda_{q}>0
and
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that the phase function
\Phi:\mathbb{R}_{x_{2}}\to\mathbb{R}
and the symbol
q(x_{2},\xi_{2}):\mathbb{R}_{x_{2}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\xi_{2}}\to\mathbb{C}
satisfy, for any integer
0\leq k\leq N_{0}
,

\displaystyle|{\partial_{x_{2}}^{k}\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi(x)}|

\displaystyle\leq A_{\Phi,k}\mu^{k}\lambda,

(5.2)

\displaystyle|{\partial_{\xi_{2}}^{k}q(x_{2},\xi_{2})}|

\displaystyle\leq A_{q,k}\lambda_{q}^{-k}g(x_{2})M(\xi_{2}),

(5.3)

respectively, where
A_{\Phi,j},A_{q,j}>0
are increasing. Furthermore, assume that
\lambda_{q},\lambda,\mu
satisfy

\frac{\mu}{\lambda_{q}}\leq 1,\quad\frac{\lambda\mu}{\lambda_{q}^{2}}\leq 1.

(5.4)

Then for any smooth
a=a(x_{2})
,
0\leq\sigma\leq 1
,
X_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\max\{10^{4},100|{s}|\}\leq N\leq N_{0}
, we have the bound

\displaystyle\left\|{\left({}^{(\Phi)}q(x_{2},D_{2};\sigma)a\right){\langle{\mu(x_{2}-X_{0})}\rangle^{s}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}

(5.5)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{s,N,A_{\Phi,N},A_{q,N}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{N}\left\|{\left(\overline{g}_{<\mu^{-1}}(x_{2})\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{<\mu^{-1}}(x_{2};\sigma)(\mu^{-1}\partial_{x_{2}})^{\ell}a(x_{2})\right){\langle{\mu(x_{2}-X_{0})}\rangle^{s}}}\right\|_{L^{2}},

where

\displaystyle\overline{g}_{<\mu^{-1}}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=\sup_{y_{2}}\frac{g(y_{2})}{\langle{\mu(x_{2}-y_{2})}\rangle^{\frac{N}{100}}},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{<\mu^{-1}}(x_{2};\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sup_{y_{2}}\int\frac{\mathcal{M}_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1},<\lambda_{q}}(y_{2},\xi_{2};\sigma)}{\langle{\mu(x_{2}-y_{2})}\rangle^{\frac{N}{100}}}\frac{\mu^{-1}}{\langle{\mu^{-1}\xi_{2}}\rangle^{\frac{N}{100}}}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{2},

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1},<\lambda_{q}}(x_{2},\xi_{2};\sigma)

\displaystyle=\iint M(\sigma(\zeta+\xi_{2}+\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi(x)-\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi(x_{1},x_{2}+z)-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\Phi(x)z)+\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi(x_{1},x_{2}+z))

\displaystyle\phantom{=\iint}\times\frac{\lambda_{q}}{\langle{\lambda_{q}z}\rangle^{\frac{N}{100}}}\frac{\lambda_{q}^{-1}}{\langle{\lambda_{q}^{-1}\zeta}\rangle^{\frac{N}{100}}}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}z.

Remark 5.2
.

Note that the RHS of (
5.5
) involves a spatial weight
\overline{g}_{<\mu^{-1}}(x_{2})\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{<\mu^{-1}}(x_{2};\sigma)
; this feature is important for exploiting the degeneracy of the principal symbol
p_{\mathring{\theta}}
in the proof of the error bound in Section
6.3
. We also point out that in (
5.5
), we are allowed to lose as many derivatives of
a
(with weights in
\mu^{-1}
) as we wish; this feature simplifies the proof (see Remark
5.4
).

5.1
Oscillatory integral bounds for the symbol

In this section, we aim to derive key pointwise bounds for the symbol
{}^{(\Phi)}q(x,\xi;\sigma)
and its
\xi
-derivatives.

The main goal of this subsection is to prove the following pointwise bound for the symbol
{}^{(\Phi)}q(x_{2},\xi_{2};\sigma)
:

Lemma 5.3
.

For any
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\sigma\in[0,1]
,
\ell,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
C_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\ell+10C_{0}\leq N_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle|{\partial_{\xi_{2}}^{\ell}{}^{(\Phi)}q(x_{2},\xi_{2};\sigma)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\ell,A_{\Phi,10C_{0}},A_{q,\ell+10C_{0}}}\lambda_{q}^{-\ell}g(x_{2})\mathcal{M}_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1},<\lambda_{q}}(x_{2},\xi_{2};\sigma)\max\left\{1,\mu\lambda_{q}^{-1},\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{q}}\left(\mu\lambda_{q}^{-1}+\mu^{3}\lambda_{q}^{-3}\right)\right\}^{C_{0}+2},

(5.6)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1},<\lambda_{q}}(x_{2},\xi_{2};\sigma)

\displaystyle=\iint M(\sigma(\zeta+\xi_{2}+\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi(x_{2})-\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi(x_{2}+z)-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\Phi(x_{2})z)+\partial_{x_{2}}\Phi(x_{2}+z))

\displaystyle\phantom{=\iint}\times\frac{\lambda_{q}}{\langle{\lambda_{q}z}\rangle^{C_{0}}}\frac{\lambda_{q}^{-1}}{\langle{\lambda_{q}^{-1}\zeta}\rangle^{C_{0}}}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}z.

Proof.

We begin by laying out some notational simplifications to be used in the proof. To simplify the notation, we shall write
x=x_{2}
,
\xi=\xi_{2}
,
q(\xi)=q(x,\xi)
and
Q(x,\xi)={}^{(\Phi)}q(x,\xi;\sigma)
. Then

Q(x,\xi)=\iint q(\sigma\eta+(1-\sigma)\partial_{x}\Phi(y))e^{i(\Phi(y)-\Phi(x)+(\eta-\xi)(x-y))}\,\mathrm{d}y\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{2\pi}.

Moreover, the assumption (
5.3
) becomes

|{\partial_{\xi}^{n}q(\xi)}|\lesssim_{n,A_{q,n}}\lambda_{q}^{-n}g(x)M(\xi).

(5.7)

Finally, by the second assumption on the phase, we have
|{\partial_{x}^{n}\partial_{x}\Phi(x)}|\lesssim_{n,A_{\Phi,n}}\mu^{n}\lambda
.

In what follows, we first give a detailed proof of the case
\ell=0
, then indicate the necessary modifications for higher
\ell
’s.

The phase function
\psi(x,\xi;y,\eta)
, defined by

\displaystyle\psi(x,\xi;y,\eta)=\Phi(y)-\Phi(x)+(\eta-\xi)(x-y),

is stationary at
y=x
and
\eta=\xi+\partial_{x}\Phi(x)
. By Taylor expansion, we may write

\displaystyle\psi(x,\xi;y,\eta)

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\Phi(x)(y-x)+\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(x)(y-x)^{2}+(\eta-\xi)(x-y)+\frac{1}{3!}r^{(3)}[\Phi](y,x)(y-x)^{3}

\displaystyle=(x-y)(\eta-\xi-\partial_{x}\Phi(x)+\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(x)(x-y))+\frac{1}{3!}r^{(3)}[\Phi](y,x)(y-x)^{3}.

We make the change of variables
(y,\eta)\mapsto(z,\zeta)
, where

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=y-x,

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=\eta-\xi-\partial_{x}\Phi(x)+\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(x)(x-y),

so that

\displaystyle Q(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\iint\widetilde{q}(x,\xi;z,\zeta)e^{ir^{(3)}[\Phi](x+z,x)\frac{z^{3}}{3!}}e^{-i\zeta z}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}z,

(5.8)

where

\widetilde{q}(x,\xi;z,\zeta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}q\left(\sigma(\zeta+\xi+\partial_{x}\Phi(x)-\partial_{x}\Phi(x+z)-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(x)z)+\partial_{x}\Phi(x+z)\right).

Now observe that

\displaystyle|{\partial_{\zeta}^{m}\partial_{z}^{n}\widetilde{q}(x,\xi;z,\zeta)}|

(5.9)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,n,A_{\Phi,n},A_{q,m+n}}\lambda_{q}^{-m}\max\left\{\frac{\lambda\mu}{\lambda_{q}},\mu\right\}^{n}

\displaystyle\times g(x)M(\sigma(\zeta+\xi+\partial_{x}\Phi(x)-\partial_{x}\Phi(x+z)-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(x)z)+\partial_{x}\Phi(x+z)).

We shall view (
5.8
) as an oscillatory integral with the simple phase
\zeta z
. Accordingly, we introduce the dyadic decomposition

Q_{Z,H}(x,\xi)=\iint\chi_{H}(\zeta)\chi_{Z}(z)\widetilde{q}(x,\xi;z,\zeta)e^{ir^{(3)}[\Phi](x+z,x)\frac{z^{3}}{3!}}e^{-i\zeta z}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}z,

as well as
Q_{<Z,H}(x,\xi)
,
Q_{Z,<H}(x,\xi)
and
Q_{<Z,<H}(x,\xi)
, which are similarly defined. We also introduce the shorthands

M_{Z,H}=\int_{z\in(\frac{1}{2}Z,2Z)}\int_{\zeta\in(\frac{1}{2}H,2H)}M(\sigma(\zeta+\xi+\partial_{x}\Phi(x)-\partial_{x}\Phi(x+z)-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\Phi(x)z)+\partial_{x}\Phi(x+z))\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}z,

as well as
M_{<H,Z}
,
M_{H,<Z}
and
M_{<H,<Z}
, which are similarly defined.

The core localized integral bounds are as follows: For dyadic numbers
Z\gtrsim\lambda_{q}^{-1}
and
H\gtrsim\lambda_{q}
,

\displaystyle|{Q_{Z,H}(x,\xi)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,n,A_{\Phi,n+2},A_{q,m+n}}g(x)M_{Z,H}\left(Z^{-1}\lambda_{q}^{-1}\right)^{m}

(5.10)

\displaystyle\phantom{\lesssim_{m,n,A_{\Phi,n+2},A_{q,m+n}}}\times\left(H^{-1}\max\left\{Z^{-1},\frac{\lambda\mu}{\lambda_{q}},\mu,\lambda\mu^{2}Z^{2}+\lambda\mu^{3}Z^{3}\right\}\right)^{n},

\displaystyle|{Q_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1},H}(x,\xi)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{n,A_{\Phi,n+2},A_{q,n}}g(x)M_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1},H}\left(H^{-1}\max\left\{\lambda_{q},\frac{\lambda\mu}{\lambda_{q}},\mu,\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{q}}\frac{\mu^{2}}{\lambda_{q}}+\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{q}}\frac{\mu^{3}}{\lambda_{q}^{2}}\right\}\right)^{n},

(5.11)

\displaystyle|{Q_{Z,<\lambda_{q}}(x,\xi)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,A_{q,m}}g(x)M_{Z,<\lambda_{q}}\left(Z^{-1}\lambda_{q}^{-1}\right)^{m},

(5.12)

\displaystyle|{Q_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1},<\lambda_{q}}(x,\xi)}|

\displaystyle\lesssim g(x)M_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1},<\lambda_{q}}.

(5.13)

Indeed, (
5.13
) is trivial by the definition of
M_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1},<\lambda_{q}}
and the fact that the volume of the support of
\chi_{<\lambda_{q}}(\zeta)\chi_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1}}(z)
is
O(1)
. To prove (
5.12
), we simply use
e^{-i\zeta z}=iz^{-1}\partial_{\zeta}e^{-i\zeta z}
and integration by parts and estimate

\displaystyle|{Q_{Z,<\lambda_{q}}}|

\displaystyle=\left|{\iint\chi_{<\lambda_{q}}(\zeta)\chi_{Z}(z)\widetilde{q}(x,\xi;z,\zeta)e^{ir^{(3)}[\Phi](x+z,x)\frac{z^{3}}{3!}}z^{-n}\partial_{\zeta}^{n}e^{-i\zeta z}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}z}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\iint\partial_{\zeta}^{m}\left(\chi_{<\lambda_{q}}(\zeta)\widetilde{q}(x,\xi;z,\zeta)\right)\chi_{Z}(z)e^{ir^{(3)}[\Phi](x+z,x)\frac{z^{3}}{3!}}z^{-m}e^{-i\zeta z}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}z}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{m,A_{q,m}}g(x)M_{Z,<\lambda_{q}}\,Z^{-m}\lambda_{q}^{-m},

where
Z\lambda_{q}
is the volume of the support of
\chi_{<\lambda_{q}}(\zeta)\chi_{Z}(z)
.
Next, to prove (
5.11
), we use
e^{-i\zeta z}=i\zeta^{-1}\partial_{z}e^{-i\zeta z}
and integration by parts to bound

\displaystyle\left|{Q_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1},H}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|{\iint\chi_{H}(\zeta)\partial_{z}^{n}\left(\chi_{<\lambda_{q}^{-1}}(z)\widetilde{q}(x,\xi;z,\zeta)e^{ir^{(3)}[\Phi](x+z,x)\frac{z^{3}}{3!}}\right)\zeta^{-n}e^{-i\zeta z}\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}z}\right|.

Then the desired estimate (
5.11
) follows, via the chain rule and the Leibniz rule, from (
5.9
) and
